The Patterns We Want to Work On

Proposed Facet-to-Aspect Crosswalks: Three Papers Compared**This document compiles every facet-to-aspect (or facet-to-subdomain) mapping proposed by the three papers in the project files. It is a **descriptive inventory** of what each set of researchers proposed — not an evaluation of whether they are correct.

**Sources**

  - **DeYoung, Quilty & Peterson (2007).** Between facets and domains: 10 aspects of the Big Five. *JPSP, 93*(5), 880–896.
  - **DeYoung (2015).** Cybernetic Big Five Theory. *Journal of Research in Personality, 56*, 33–58.
  - **Soto & John (2017).** The next Big Five Inventory (BFI-2). *JPSP, 113*(1), 117–143.

**How to Read This Document**

  - **DeYoung et al. (2007)** proposed **10 aspects** (2 per domain) derived from factor analysis of 15 facets per domain (6 NEO-PI-R + 9 AB5C-IPIP). Their Table 3 gives factor loadings for every facet on both aspects within its domain.
  - **DeYoung (2015)** did not propose a new crosswalk. CB5T elaborates the theoretical rationale for the 2007 aspect assignments but does not reassign any facets. Where CB5T adds nuance (e.g., the wanting/liking distinction for Assertiveness/Enthusiasm), it is noted.
  - **Soto & John (2017)** proposed **15 BFI-2 facets** (3 per domain) and aligned them to four prior models (NEO PI-R, AB5C, Saucier & Ostendorf lexical subcomponents, and the DeYoung et al. 2007 aspects) in their Table 1. Their crosswalk is **not identical** to the 2007 mapping — they added a third facet per domain and reassigned some content. Their Table 1 also leaves some cells blank ("—") where they judged no clean correspondence.

**Extraversion**

**DeYoung et al. (2007) — Two Aspects** **Aspect****** **NEO-PI-R Facets (primary loading)****** **AB5C-IPIP Facets (primary loading)****** **Enthusiasm******

        Warmth (.79), Gregariousness (.71), Positive Emotions (.67)

        Friendliness (.88), Gregariousness (.77), Poise (.71), Self-disclosure (.60), Sociability (.48)

        **Assertiveness******

        Assertiveness (.72), Activity (.59)

        Leadership (.85), Assertiveness (.83), Provocativeness (.71), Talkativeness (.58)

        *Peripheral***

        Excitement Seeking (.27 on each)

        —

**Notes from Table 3:** Excitement Seeking loaded weakly on both aspects (.27/.27). The MAP test indicated 3 factors for E until Excitement Seeking was excluded, at which point 2 factors emerged. DeYoung et al. retained it in the analysis but flagged it as peripheral to both aspects.

**DeYoung (2015) CB5T — Theoretical Elaboration**CB5T maps Assertiveness to **wanting** (incentive/appetitive reward sensitivity, dopaminergic) and Enthusiasm to **liking** (consummatory/hedonic reward, opioidergic). Activity level falls primarily under Assertiveness. No facet reassignments.

**Soto & John (2017) — Three BFI-2 Facets** **BFI-2 Facet****** **Aligned NEO PI-R Facet(s)****** **Aligned BFAS Aspect****** **Empirical convergence (r)****** **Sociability******

        Gregariousness

        Enthusiasm

        r = .62 with NEO Gregariousness

        **Assertiveness******

        Assertiveness

        Assertiveness

        r = .85 with BFAS Assertiveness, r = .79 with NEO Assertiveness

        **Energy Level******

        Positive Emotions / Activity

        Enthusiasm

        r = .70 with BFAS Enthusiasm, r = .63 with NEO Positive Emotions, r = .59 with NEO Activity

**Key difference from 2007:** Soto & John split the Enthusiasm aspect into two BFI-2 facets (Sociability and Energy Level), treating sociability/gregariousness and positive energy/activity as distinguishable components. Their Table 1 maps both Sociability and Energy Level to the Enthusiasm aspect, keeping Assertiveness aligned the same way.

**Agreeableness**

**DeYoung et al. (2007) — Two Aspects** **Aspect****** **NEO-PI-R Facets (primary loading)****** **AB5C-IPIP Facets (primary loading)****** **Compassion******

        Altruism (.65), Tender-mindedness (.50), Trust (.42)

        Warmth (.87), Sympathy (.86), Understanding (.83), Empathy (.69), Tenderness (.65)

        **Politeness******

        Compliance (.71), Straightforwardness (.67), Modesty (.44)

        Nurturance (.80), Cooperation (.74), Pleasantness (.72), Morality (.67)

**Notes from Table 3:** Altruism (.65/.64) and Trust (.42/.42) loaded nearly equally on both aspects — they are markers of the A domain as a whole, not cleanly of one aspect. DeYoung et al. explicitly noted no good NEO markers for Compassion specifically (as distinct from Politeness).

**DeYoung (2015) CB5T — Theoretical Elaboration**

CB5T maps Compassion to empathic emotional affiliation and Politeness to suppression of aggressive/exploitative impulses. Notes similarity of Politeness to Ashton & Lee's Honesty-Humility. No facet reassignments.

**Soto & John (2017) — Three BFI-2 Facets** **BFI-2 Facet****** **Aligned NEO PI-R Facet(s)****** **Aligned BFAS Aspect****** **Empirical convergence (r)****** **Compassion******

        Altruism

        Compassion

        r = .70 with BFAS Compassion, r = .67 with NEO Altruism, r = .50 with NEO Tender-mindedness

        **Respectfulness******

        Compliance

        Politeness

        r = .66 with BFAS Politeness, r = .52 with NEO Compliance, r = .48 with NEO Straightforwardness

        **Trust******

        Trust

        — (no BFAS alignment given)

        r = .65 with NEO Trust

**Key difference from 2007:** Soto & John added Trust as a third, standalone facet. In the 2007 analysis, NEO Trust loaded almost equally on Compassion and Politeness (.42/.42). Soto & John's Table 1 leaves the BFAS aspect column blank for Trust, acknowledging it doesn't map cleanly to either the Compassion or Politeness aspect.

**Conscientiousness**

**DeYoung et al. (2007) — Two Aspects** **Aspect****** **NEO-PI-R Facets (primary loading)****** **AB5C-IPIP Facets (primary loading)****** **Industriousness******

        Self-discipline (.83), Competence (.75), Achievement-striving (.65), Dutifulness (.63), Deliberation (.55)

        Purposefulness (.86), Efficiency (.84), Organization (.74), Dutifulness (.52)

        **Orderliness******

        Order (.79)

        Orderliness (.87), Conscientiousness (.79), Perfectionism (.67), Rationality (.60), Cautiousness (.46)

**Notes from Table 3:** Most NEO facets loaded primarily on Industriousness. The AB5C Conscientiousness facet (factor-pure) loaded highly on both (.78/.79). These two aspects replicated Roberts et al.'s (2005) Industriousness and Order factors from their seven-instrument study.

**DeYoung (2015) CB5T — Theoretical Elaboration**

CB5T notes a critical discriminant validity finding: Industriousness is negatively correlated with Neuroticism, while Orderliness is positively correlated with Neuroticism (especially anxiety) when Industriousness is controlled. CB5T interprets Industriousness as reflecting motivation to pursue non-immediate rewards (linked to Extraversion/reward sensitivity) and Orderliness as reflecting attention to rules (linked to defensive reactions to uncertainty). No facet reassignments.

**Soto & John (2017) — Three BFI-2 Facets** **BFI-2 Facet****** **Aligned NEO PI-R Facet(s)****** **Aligned BFAS Aspect****** **Empirical convergence (r)****** **Organization******

        Order

        Orderliness

        r = .74 with NEO Order, r = .70 with BFAS Orderliness

        **Productiveness******

        Industriousness

        r = .77 with BFAS Industriousness, r = .73 with NEO Self-discipline, r = .60 with NEO Achievement-striving

        **Responsibility******

        Dutifulness

        — (no BFAS alignment given)

        r = .58 with NEO Dutifulness, r = .47 with NEO Deliberation

**Key difference from 2007:** Soto & John added Responsibility as a third facet. In the 2007 analysis, NEO Dutifulness loaded primarily on Industriousness (.63) with a secondary on Orderliness (.50). Soto & John's Table 1 leaves the BFAS aspect column blank for Responsibility, treating it as a prosocial component of Conscientiousness that doesn't align cleanly with either aspect.

**Neuroticism / Negative Emotionality**

**DeYoung et al. (2007) — Two Aspects** **Aspect****** **NEO-PI-R Facets (primary loading)****** **AB5C-IPIP Facets (primary loading)****** **Volatility******

        Angry Hostility (.76), Impulsiveness (.59)

        Stability (R) (.86), Calmness (R) (.81), Tranquility (R) (.75), Impulse Control (R) (.70), Moderation (R) (.70), Imperturbability (R) (.54), Cool-headedness (R) (.30)

        **Withdrawal******

        Depression (.85), Vulnerability (.78), Anxiety (.78), Self-consciousness (.76)

        Happiness (R) (.88), Toughness (R) (.77)

**Notes from Table 3:** Anxiety loaded substantially on both aspects (.57/.78), but more strongly on Withdrawal. Impulsiveness loaded on both (.59/.43), more on Volatility. The factor correlation was .64, the highest of any domain.

**DeYoung (2015) CB5T — Theoretical Elaboration**CB5T maps Volatility to the **fight-flight-freeze system (FFFS)** — reactive anger, panic, emotional lability, active defense. Maps Withdrawal to the **behavioral inhibition system (BIS)** — anxiety, depression, passive avoidance in response to approach-avoidance conflict. No facet reassignments, but provides mechanistic rationale for why these aspects should be distinct.

**Soto & John (2017) — Three BFI-2 Facets** **BFI-2 Facet****** **Aligned NEO PI-R Facet(s)****** **Aligned BFAS Aspect****** **Empirical convergence (r)****** **Anxiety******

        Anxiety

        Withdrawal

        r = .70 with NEO Anxiety

        **Depression******

        Depression

        Withdrawal

        r = .77 with BFAS Withdrawal, r = .71 with NEO Depression

        **Emotional Volatility******

        Angry Hostility

        Volatility

        r = .79 with BFAS Volatility, r = .54 with NEO Angry Hostility

**Key difference from 2007:** Soto & John split the Withdrawal aspect into Anxiety and Depression as separate BFI-2 facets. Both are aligned to the Withdrawal aspect in their Table 1. Their Emotional Volatility facet aligns to the Volatility aspect but directly assesses moodiness/instability rather than anger/irritability (which they found fell interstitially between N and A domains). They note that BFI-2 Emotional Volatility still converges strongly with BFAS Volatility and NEO Angry Hostility empirically.

**Openness/Intellect / Open-Mindedness**

**DeYoung et al. (2007) — Two Aspects** **Aspect****** **NEO-PI-R Facets (primary loading)****** **AB5C-IPIP Facets (primary loading)****** **Intellect******

        Ideas (.76)

        Quickness (.86), Creativity (.85), Intellect (.81), Ingenuity (.73), Competence (.71), Depth (.55), Introspection (.41)

        **Openness******

        Aesthetics (.87), Fantasy (.64), Feelings (.58), Actions (.54), Values (.44)

        Imagination (.85), Reflection (.73)

**Notes from Table 3:** Ideas was the only NEO facet loading clearly on Intellect. Openness had more NEO markers. The factor correlation was .51 (lowest of any domain), suggesting these are the most separable pair of aspects.

**DeYoung (2015) CB5T — Theoretical Elaboration**CB5T maps Intellect to **conscious cognitive exploration** (working memory, fluid intelligence, propositional/logical learning) and Openness to **perceptual/aesthetic exploration** (implicit learning, pattern detection, associational learning). Reports a double dissociation: Intellect predicts working memory but not implicit learning; Openness predicts implicit learning but not working memory. Also notes that Psychoticism/apophenia is positively associated with Openness but negatively associated with Intellect — a suppression effect that makes Psychoticism hard to locate at the Big Five domain level. No facet reassignments.

**Soto & John (2017) — Three BFI-2 Facets** **BFI-2 Facet****** **Aligned NEO PI-R Facet(s)****** **Aligned BFAS Aspect****** **Empirical convergence (r)****** **Intellectual Curiosity******

        Ideas

        Intellect

        r = .72 with NEO Ideas, r = .62 with BFAS Intellect

        **Aesthetic Sensitivity******

        Aesthetics

        Openness

        r = .76 with BFAS Openness, r = .74 with NEO Aesthetics

        **Creative Imagination******

        Fantasy

        — (no BFAS alignment given)

        r = .38 with NEO Fantasy (modest; reflects Creative Imagination's focus on creativity vs. Fantasy's focus on idle daydreaming)

**Key difference from 2007:** Soto & John added Creative Imagination as a third facet. In the 2007 analysis, NEO Fantasy loaded primarily on Openness (.64) with a secondary loading on Intellect (.45). Soto & John's Table 1 leaves the BFAS aspect column blank for Creative Imagination, and their BFI-2 facet is defined more around creativity/originality than idle daydreaming, which partly explains the modest convergence with NEO Fantasy.

**Summary: Where the Three Papers Agree and Disagree**

**Points of agreement**

All three papers agree on the basic ten-aspect structure within the Big Five: Enthusiasm/Assertiveness, Compassion/Politeness, Industriousness/Orderliness, Volatility/Withdrawal, Intellect/Openness. Soto & John (2017) explicitly build on and validate the 2007 aspect structure.

**Points of divergence** **Domain****** **Issue****** **2007 Assignment****** **Soto & John (2017) Treatment****** **Extraversion******

        NEO Excitement Seeking

        Peripheral to both aspects (.27/.27)

        Excluded from BFI-2 entirely as too peripheral

        **Agreeableness******

        NEO Trust

        Equal loadings on both aspects (.42/.42)

        Standalone BFI-2 facet; no BFAS aspect alignment

        **Agreeableness******

        NEO Altruism

        Loaded on Compassion (.65) but nearly equal on Politeness (.64)

        Aligned to Compassion in BFI-2

        **Conscientiousness******

        NEO Dutifulness

        Loaded primarily on Industriousness (.63/.50)

        Spun off as separate "Responsibility" facet; no BFAS aspect alignment

        **Neuroticism******

        NEO Anxiety

        Loaded on both (.57/.78); assigned to Withdrawal

        Standalone BFI-2 facet aligned to Withdrawal

        **Neuroticism******

        Anger/irritability content

        Part of Volatility aspect (NEO Angry Hostility .76)

        BFI-2 Emotional Volatility directly assesses moodiness, not anger; anger content fell interstitially between N and A

        **O/I******

        NEO Fantasy

        Loaded on Openness (.64) with Intellect secondary (.45)

        Reconceived as "Creative Imagination" (creativity/originality emphasis); no BFAS aspect alignment

**Facets with no clean aspect home (per any paper)**- **E5 Excitement Seeking** — peripheral in 2007; excluded in 2017
  - **A3 Trust** — dual-loading in 2007; standalone in 2017 with no aspect alignment
  - **C3 Dutifulness** — primarily Industriousness in 2007; reconceived as "Responsibility" in 2017 with no aspect alignment
  - **O4 Actions** — loaded on Openness (.54) in 2007; excluded from BFI-2 as too peripheral to the O/I domain
  - **O6 Values** — loaded weakly on Openness (.44) in 2007; excluded from BFI-2

**Ludeke, Bainbridge, Liu, Zhao, Smillie & Zettler (2019)**

**Citation**

Ludeke, S. G., Bainbridge, T. F., Liu, J., Zhao, K., Smillie, L. D., & Zettler, I. (2019). Using the Big Five Aspect Scales to translate between the HEXACO and Big Five personality models. *Journal of Personality, 87*(5), 1025–1038. https://doi.org/10.1111/jopy.12453

**Design**- **Type:** Cross-sectional, meta-analysis of four samples
  - **N:** 1,586 total (Sample 1: 456 Australian students; Sample 2: 520 US MTurk; Sample 3: 382 Eugene-Springfield Community Sample; Sample 4: 228 Canadian students)
  - **Age range:** 16–83 across samples (S1: M = 20.17; S2: M = 30.45; S3: M = 51.06; S4: M = 21.31)
  - **Instrument(s):** Big Five Aspect Scales (BFAS; DeYoung et al., 2007) + HEXACO-100 (Samples 1, 2, 4) or HEXACO-PI 192-item (Sample 3)
  - **Assessment interval:** Not longitudinal per se, but Sample 3 had up to a 9-year gap between BFAS items and HEXACO completion (BFAS items collected as early as 1994; HEXACO completed 2003). [from PDF p. 8]
  - **Key limitations:**

    - This is a correlational mapping study, not a heritability or developmental trajectory paper. Evidence stream = structural/factor-loading only.
    - Sample 3 used an older HEXACO version lacking Altruism and Social Self-Esteem facets, and with a different Extraversion facet (Expressiveness instead of Social Self-Esteem). HEXACO Extraversion domain data from Sample 3 excluded from meta-analysis. [from PDF p. 8–9]
    - Sample 3 had the 9-year gap between instruments, which likely attenuates correlations (partially offset by 8-item rather than 4-item facets). [from PDF p. 8]
    - Self-report only in main analyses (peer report available for Sample 4 only, reported separately). [from PDF p. 9]
    - Random-effects meta-analytic correlations reported; no correction for unreliability. [from PDF p. 9]

**Granularity**

**Aspect-level and facet-level.** This paper reports correlations between all 10 BFAS aspects and all HEXACO domains (6) and facets (25). This is directly useful because it maps the intermediate BFAS aspect level to the HEXACO facet level — showing which aspects are coherent constructs that translate cleanly across taxonomies and which fragment or get lost.

**Not NEO-PI-R facets.** The paper does not report at the level of the 30 NEO facets. The BFAS aspects are the finest Big Five granularity; HEXACO facets are the finest HEXACO granularity.

**Core Findings**

**Evidence stream**This paper provides **structural mapping** evidence. No heritability data. No developmental trajectories. The relevance to the crosswalk is: an aspect that cannot be recovered from a well-validated alternative taxonomy may not be a coherent construct, OR it may represent content unique to Big Five space. Both possibilities matter for evaluating the published aspect structure.

**Domain: Agreeableness**

This is the most informative domain for the crosswalk. The HEXACO model rotates Big Five Agreeableness and Neuroticism, and the two BFAS Agreeableness aspects land in very different places in HEXACO space.

        **BFAS Aspect****** **Best HEXACO domain correlate****** **r****** **Best HEXACO facet correlate****** **r****** **Notes******

        Politeness

        Honesty-Humility

        .51

        H-Modesty (.49), H-Fairness (.42), A-Gentleness (.41), Altruism (.51)

        see values

        Politeness is best captured by HEXACO H-H, not HEXACO Agreeableness (.41). This means the published "Agreeableness → Politeness" mapping in the Big Five maps primarily to "Honesty-Humility" in HEXACO space. [from PDF Table 1, Table 2a]

        Compassion

        Emotionality

        .38

        Altruism (.58), E-Sentimentality (.56)

        see values

        Compassion is the **worst-captured** aspect at the domain level (highest r = .38). It is substantially better indexed by the interstitial HEXACO Altruism facet (.58) and Emotionality-Sentimentality (.56). [from PDF Table 1, Table 2a]

**Key structural finding:** When both aspects are used simultaneously to predict Honesty-Humility, Compassion's β drops to .07 while Politeness β = .48. Compassion's correlation with H-H is entirely attributable to shared Agreeableness variance with Politeness. [from PDF p. 10]

**Regression coverage:** HEXACO domains capture only R² = .40 (Compassion) and R² = .45 (Politeness) of aspect variance — the two lowest-captured aspects along with Intellect (R² = .42). Even with HEXACO facets, these remain the least well-captured aspects. [from PDF pp. 16–17]

**Crosswalk implication:** The two Agreeableness aspects are structurally distinct enough that they land in entirely different HEXACO domains. This is convergent evidence for treating them as separable constructs, though it does NOT prove the aspect assignments are correct for every NEO facet — it only shows that the two aspects, as measured by the BFAS, capture different content.

**Domain: Neuroticism**

        **BFAS Aspect****** **Best HEXACO domain correlate****** **r****** **Best HEXACO facet correlate****** **r****** **Notes******

        Volatility

        HEXACO Agreeableness (negative)

        −.61

        A-Patience (negative)

        −.70

        Volatility maps primarily to low HEXACO Agreeableness, especially low Patience. NOT to HEXACO Emotionality (.40). [from PDF Table 1, Table 2b]

        Withdrawal

        HEXACO Emotionality

        .53

        E-Anxiety (.61), X-Social Self-Esteem (negative, −.62)

        see values

        Withdrawal is jointly indexed by high Emotionality-Anxiety and low Extraversion-Social Self-Esteem. [from PDF Table 1, Table 2b]

**Key structural finding:** When both Neuroticism aspects simultaneously predict HEXACO Emotionality, Withdrawal dominates (β = .46 vs. β = .12 for Volatility). [from PDF p. 10]

**Key structural finding:** HEXACO Extraversion has a notably large correlation with BFAS Withdrawal (r = −.59), larger than typically observed between Big Five Extraversion and Withdrawal in DeYoung et al. (2007). This suggests HEXACO Extraversion contains more low-Neuroticism content (via Social Self-Esteem) than typical Big Five Extraversion measures. [from PDF p. 9]

**Crosswalk implication:** The two Neuroticism aspects map to completely different HEXACO domains (Volatility → HEXACO Agreeableness; Withdrawal → HEXACO Emotionality). This provides strong structural evidence that they are separable constructs. This converges with the heritability evidence (Jang et al., 1996, 2002) and developmental trajectory evidence (Terracciano et al., 2005) showing within-Neuroticism heterogeneity.

**Domain: Extraversion**

        **BFAS Aspect****** **Best HEXACO domain correlate****** **r****** **Best HEXACO facet correlate****** **r****** **Notes******

        Enthusiasm

        HEXACO Extraversion

        .72

        X-Sociability (.62), X-Liveliness (.61), X-Social Self-Esteem (.51)

        see values

        Well-mirrored at domain level. No single facet improves over the domain. [from PDF Table 1, Table 2d]

        Assertiveness

        HEXACO Extraversion

        .67

        X-Social Boldness (.74)

        .74

        Assertiveness is substantially better indexed by Social Boldness (.74) than by the X domain (.67). [from PDF Table 1, Table 2d]

**Regression:** β coefficients predicting HEXACO Extraversion are Enthusiasm = .52, Assertiveness = .44 — similar, no evidence that one aspect dominates. [from PDF p. 9]

**Crosswalk implication:** Both Extraversion aspects translate cleanly. Enthusiasm is a broad construct spread across multiple HEXACO X facets; Assertiveness is more narrowly captured by Social Boldness. This is structural evidence that Assertiveness is a tighter, more unitary construct than Enthusiasm — consistent with the factor-analytic evidence in DeYoung et al. (2007) where Assertiveness had fewer high-loading facets.

**Domain: Conscientiousness**

        **BFAS Aspect****** **Best HEXACO domain correlate****** **r****** **Best HEXACO facet correlate****** **r****** **Notes******

        Industriousness

        HEXACO Conscientiousness

        .66

        C-Diligence (.59)

        .59

        Domain better than any single facet. Also notable secondary correlations with X (.45), X-Social Self-Esteem (.46), and low Withdrawal. [from PDF Table 1, Table 2c]

        Orderliness

        HEXACO Conscientiousness

        .63

        .68

        Organization facet surpasses the domain. Clean one-to-one mapping. [from PDF Table 1, Table 2c]

**Regression:** β for Industriousness = .48, Orderliness = .42 predicting HEXACO C. Similar, no dominance. [from PDF p. 9]

**Notable cross-domain loadings:** HEXACO C-Diligence correlates with aspects from four Big Five domains (Orderliness, Industriousness, Intellect, Assertiveness, low Withdrawal). This suggests Diligence contains broader motivational/agentic content, not pure Conscientiousness. [from PDF p. 15]

**Crosswalk implication:** Both C aspects translate cleanly. Orderliness maps almost one-to-one to HEXACO Organization. Industriousness is broader and connects to Extraversion and Intellect — consistent with the idea that Industriousness captures a motivational/energetic component that overlaps with agentic traits.

        Intellect

        HEXACO Openness to Experience

        .50

        O-Inquisitiveness (.39), O-Unconventionality (.45), C-Diligence (.43)

        see values

        Least well-mirrored aspect at domain level. Facets don't improve. Multiple weak predictors, lowest R² of all aspects. [from PDF Table 1, Table 2e]

**Regression:** β for Openness aspect = .63, Intellect = .27 predicting HEXACO O. Openness dominates; the two aspects are asymmetrically represented. [from PDF p. 10]

**Key finding:** Intellect, along with Compassion and Politeness, has the lowest R² when predicted by all HEXACO domains (R² = .42). Even HEXACO facets don't substantially improve this. [from PDF pp. 16–17]

**Crosswalk implication:** Intellect is poorly captured by HEXACO space. This could mean (a) Intellect contains unique Big Five content not present in HEXACO, or (b) Intellect is a heterogeneous construct that doesn't cohere well even within Big Five measures. The authors note that Intellect also correlates modestly with its own Big Five domain in other measures (DeYoung et al., 2007, Table A7). [from PDF p. 17]

**Cross-domain and structural findings**

**HEXACO Agreeableness is primarily BFAS Volatility (reversed).** The strongest correlate of HEXACO Agreeableness is not any BFAS Agreeableness aspect but BFAS Volatility (r = −.61). This is a rotational shift: the HEXACO model took the anger/irritability content out of Neuroticism and combined it with non-antagonism content to form HEXACO Agreeableness. [from PDF Table 1, p. 10]

**HEXACO Emotionality is primarily BFAS Withdrawal.** HEXACO Emotionality correlates .53 with Withdrawal, .40 with Volatility, and .38 with Compassion. When Withdrawal and Volatility are entered simultaneously, Withdrawal dominates (β = .46 vs. .12). [from PDF p. 10]

**HEXACO Honesty-Humility maps to BFAS Politeness.** r = .51. Compassion adds nothing unique (β = .07 when both aspects predict H-H). [from PDF p. 10]

**Several HEXACO facets have weak links to all BFAS aspects.** Sincerity and Greediness (H-H), Dependence and Fearfulness (Emotionality), Forgiveness and Flexibility (Agreeableness) — none of these have any BFAS aspect correlate above .40. This is NOT dominated by H-H facets; it's mostly Emotionality and Agreeableness facets. The authors argue these facets represent additional content beyond Big Five space, not just rotational rearrangement. [from PDF pp. 13–14]

**BFAS has higher inter-domain correlations than HEXACO.** Average absolute inter-domain r: BFAS = .22, HEXACO = .12. Even restricting to the three shared domains (E, C, O): BFAS = .26, HEXACO = .17. This suggests BFAS aspects have more cross-domain bleed, which could reflect either (a) aspects capturing variance that is genuinely shared across domains, or (b) BFAS items being less discriminating. [from PDF p. 15]

**Politeness–Honesty-Humility convergence is moderate, not high.** r = .52 (meta-analytic). DeYoung (2014) claimed H-H content was captured by Politeness. The authors note that within-trait correlations across Big Five instruments average .77 (Soto & John, 2017). At r = .52, the overlap is real but insufficient to treat the two as interchangeable. [from PDF pp. 19–20]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Compassion vs. Politeness: land in different HEXACO domains**- **Facets involved:** BFAS Compassion and BFAS Politeness (both assigned to Big Five Agreeableness)
  - **Currently assigned to:** Compassion → Compassion aspect of Agreeableness; Politeness → Politeness aspect of Agreeableness
  - **Evidence:** Politeness maps primarily to HEXACO Honesty-Humility (r = .51). Compassion maps primarily to HEXACO Altruism (.58) and Emotionality-Sentimentality (.56). These are entirely different HEXACO domains. When predicting H-H, Compassion β = .07 — it adds nothing beyond Politeness.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Strong** for the claim that Compassion and Politeness are structurally distinct constructs that happen to share Big Five Agreeableness space. This is structural convergent evidence with heritability data (if available at aspect level) and developmental trajectory data.
  - **Caveat:** This does NOT tell us whether the NEO facets are correctly assigned to these aspects. It tells us the aspects themselves, as measured by the BFAS, capture genuinely different content.

**2. Volatility vs. Withdrawal: land in different HEXACO domains**- **Facets involved:** BFAS Volatility and BFAS Withdrawal (both assigned to Big Five Neuroticism)
  - **Currently assigned to:** Volatility → Volatility aspect of Neuroticism; Withdrawal → Withdrawal aspect of Neuroticism
  - **Evidence:** Volatility maps primarily to low HEXACO Agreeableness (r = −.61, especially Patience r = −.70). Withdrawal maps primarily to high HEXACO Emotionality (r = .53, especially Anxiety r = .61) and low HEXACO Extraversion (r = −.59, especially Social Self-Esteem r = −.62). These are entirely different HEXACO locations.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Strong** for the claim that Volatility and Withdrawal are structurally distinct. Converges with the HEXACO model's explicit rotation of N and A.
  - **Caveat:** Same as above — tells us about aspect-level distinctness, not about whether specific NEO facets are assigned to the right aspect.

**3. Intellect is poorly captured by any taxonomy**- **Currently assigned to:** Intellect aspect of Openness/Intellect
  - **Evidence:** R² = .42 when predicted by all HEXACO domains. No single HEXACO facet correlates above .50 with Intellect. Multiple weak facet-level predictors, lowest R² of any aspect. Also correlates modestly with its own Big Five domain in other Big Five instruments (DeYoung et al., 2007).
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Moderate** that Intellect may be a construct that doesn't sit cleanly in standard personality space. Could reflect (a) unique content, (b) heterogeneity within the aspect, or (c) difficulty measuring this construct with self-report items.
  - **Caveat:** The authors note this parallels Intellect's behavior within Big Five instruments — it's not just a HEXACO problem. This is a construct-level issue, not a taxonomic one.

**4. Openness vs. Intellect: asymmetrically represented in HEXACO**- **Currently assigned to:** Two aspects of Openness/Intellect domain
  - **Evidence:** Openness aspect correlates .73 with HEXACO O (β = .63). Intellect correlates .50 (β = .27). HEXACO Openness to Experience is dominated by the Openness aspect, not Intellect.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Moderate.** Suggests HEXACO O is primarily an aesthetic/creative openness construct and underrepresents the intellectual curiosity/engagement side. Consistent with the idea that these are separable aspects, but could also reflect HEXACO's item content choices.

**Notes**

**What this paper contributes to the crosswalk**This is a **structural mapping** paper, not a heritability or developmental trajectory study. Its primary contribution is convergent evidence that certain aspects are structurally distinct (they land in different places in HEXACO space). This is a different evidence stream from heritability and developmental trajectories, and convergence across streams strengthens the case for aspect separability.

The strongest contributions are:

  - Compassion and Politeness are structurally separable (different HEXACO homes)
  - Volatility and Withdrawal are structurally separable (different HEXACO homes)
  - Intellect is poorly captured by any standard taxonomy — a construct-level concern

**What this paper does NOT contribute**

  - No NEO-PI-R facet-level data. We cannot use this paper to evaluate whether specific NEO facets (e.g., N5 Impulsiveness, A4 Compliance) are correctly assigned to their aspects.
  - No heritability data.
  - No developmental trajectory data.
  - No factor loadings of NEO facets on aspects (that's DeYoung et al., 2007).

**Connection to other papers**- **DeYoung et al. (2007):** This paper uses the BFAS derived from that study and examines how the aspects translate to HEXACO space. The finding that Intellect and the Agreeableness aspects are the hardest to capture converges with DeYoung et al.'s observation that these aspects had the most variable correlations with other Big Five instruments.
  - **Jang et al. (1996, 2002):** The structural distinctness of Volatility vs. Withdrawal and Compassion vs. Politeness converges with the genetic evidence that Neuroticism and Agreeableness domains require more than one genetic factor.
  - **Terracciano et al. (2005) / Roberts et al. (2006):** The structural distinctness of the Neuroticism aspects converges with developmental trajectory evidence showing different facets of Neuroticism changing in different directions.
  - **Soto & John (2017):** The BFI-2's three-per-domain structure partially addresses the same problem this paper identifies — that some Big Five domains contain structurally distinct subcomponents that map to different locations in alternative taxonomies.

**Honest assessment of utility for the crosswalk**This paper's value is **moderate**. It provides convergent structural evidence that certain aspect-level distinctions are real, but it operates at a level of granularity (BFAS aspects, HEXACO facets) that doesn't directly test whether individual NEO facets are assigned to the right aspects. It's supporting evidence, not primary evidence. The primary evidence streams remain heritability (Jang), developmental trajectories (Terracciano, Roberts), and the original factor loadings (DeYoung et al., 2007).

**Schwaba et al. (2025) — Big Five GWAS**

**Citation**

Schwaba, T., Clapp Sullivan, M. L., Akingbuwa, W. A., Ilves, K., Tanksley, P. T., Williams, C. M., ... Nivard, M. G., & Tucker-Drob, E. M. (2025). Robust inference and widespread genetic correlates from a large-scale genetic association study of human personality. *bioRxiv*. https://doi.org/10.1101/2025.05.16.648988

**Design**- **Type:** GWAS meta-analysis (population-level + within-family), cross-sectional genetic architecture
  - **N:** 611K–1.14M (EUR + AFR) across 46 cohorts for population GWAS; 31,544–50,725 for within-family GWAS; 34,506 parent-offspring dyads for genetic nurture analyses
  - **Age range:** Broad (young ≤25, midlife 25–64, older 65+); exact range not specified but spans adolescence through old age across cohorts
  - **Instrument(s):** Multiple personality instruments pooled (not enumerated individually in main text; includes various Big Five measures across 46 cohorts). From supplementary references: NEO-PI-R, BFI, BFI-2, IPIP variants, EPQ, and others. Critically, analyses are conducted at domain level regardless of instrument granularity.
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (GWAS design); some prediction cohorts are longitudinal but personality measured at single timepoints for genetic analyses
  - **Key limitations:**- **Domain-level only.** All analyses are at the Big Five level. No facet-level, aspect-level, or item-level results are reported anywhere. The authors explicitly acknowledge this: "we focused only on the very broad Big Five personality traits" (Discussion, p. 21). This is the primary limitation for our crosswalk purposes.
    - **Preprint** — not yet peer-reviewed (posted May 20, 2025).
    - **Primarily EUR genomes.** AFR sample is small (43K–48K) and used EUR-derived weights for PGI prediction.
    - **Multiple instruments pooled without facet harmonization.** Many cohorts almost certainly collected facet-level data (e.g., NEO-PI-R has 30 facets, BFI-2 has 15 facets) but this information was collapsed to domain scores for meta-analysis. This is a significant missed opportunity.

**Granularity**

**Domain-level only** (Extraversion, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness, Neuroticism, Openness to Experience).

No facet-level, aspect-level, or item-level results reported. Many contributing cohorts used instruments with facet-level data available (NEO-PI-R, BFI-2, IPIP-NEO, etc.) but all analyses were conducted at the domain level. The authors flag more granular analysis as a direction for future research. [from Discussion, p. 21]

**Core Findings**

**SNP Heritability Estimates (Domain-Level)**

These are the best-powered current estimates of SNP heritability for the Big Five. All from EUR samples using LDSC. [from Table 1, p. 7]

        **Domain****** **h²_SNP (meta-analytic GWAS)****** **h²_SNP (RE-meta of cohort estimates)****** **h²_SNP (disattenuated for measurement error)******

        Conscientiousness

        7.9% (SE = 1.0%)

**Notes on these estimates:**

  - The meta-analytic GWAS h²_SNP (column 1) captures genetic effects that are *consistent across cohorts*. The RE-meta estimates (column 2) allow for variability, so they're generally higher. [from p. 9]
  - The disattenuated estimates (column 3) correct for measurement error using meta-regression of h²_SNP on Cronbach's alpha: b = 6.7% (SE = 0.7%) per unit alpha increase. These represent estimated heritability for an error-free measure. [from p. 9]
  - **Agreeableness is a clear outlier** — lowest h²_SNP by every method, and the gap widens with the meta-analytic estimate (4.8% vs. next-lowest 7.1% for C). This is partly explained by lower cross-cohort consistency (see below).

Notable patterns:**

  - Average absolute genetic correlation among Big Five = .19 — similar to phenotypic correlations. [from p. 6]
  - Agreeableness–Neuroticism shows the strongest between-domain genetic correlation (r_g = −.36), which is relevant to the crosswalk because this overlaps the Compassion/Politeness vs. Withdrawal/Volatility boundary.
  - Openness–Agreeableness genetic correlation is near zero (.05), suggesting these domains are genetically quite independent.
  - Openness–Neuroticism is also near zero (−.05).

**Cross-Group Consistency of Genetic Effects**

**Agreeableness is the clear outlier again.** Its genetic architecture is less consistent across geography (r_g = .65 vs. mean .89 for others) and measurement instrument (r_g = .68 vs. mean .91 for others). The authors note this heterogeneity partly explains its lower h²_SNP in the combined meta-analysis — the genetic effects exist but are less uniform across cohorts. [from p. 10, p. 20]

Conscientiousness also shows a notable anomaly: low reporter-perspective consistency (r_g = .68 for self vs. other report), lower than all other traits. [from Table 1]

**Within-Family vs. Population-Level Estimates**

This is important for the crosswalk because it tells us how much to trust twin-study heritability estimates for personality (answer: quite a lot — minimal confounding).

**Within-family h²_SNP estimates** [from p. 19, Figure 4C]:

**Key finding:** Population-level and within-family h²_SNP are largely indistinguishable for personality. This contrasts sharply with educational attainment (h²_SNP attenuated ≥50% within-family) and other behavioral traits. [from p. 19]

**Genetic correlations between pop-level and within-family GWAS** averaged r_g = .86. Significantly < 1.00 only for Neuroticism (r_g = .75, p < .001) and Agreeableness (r_g = .79, p = .03). [from p. 19]

**Genetic nurture analysis** (deCODE, N = 34,506): Direct genetic effects accounted for 96.2% of population-level PGI prediction on average. Only Conscientiousness showed significant indirect (non-transmitted parental) effects (8.1% of total, p = .01). For educational attainment, the comparison figure is 75.1%. [from p. 17–18, Figure 4B]

**Assortative mating:** Minimal for all Big Five (inferred parental r_g = .01–.04). Compare to educational attainment (r_g = .37). [from p. 16, Figure 2C]

**Polygenicity**

Personality traits are extremely polygenic. Estimated effective number of independently associated common SNPs averaged 16,180 per trait. E, A, C, and O were each as or more polygenic than 31 of 32 biobehavioral phenotypes compared (including schizophrenia). [from p. 9]

Median individual SNP effect for extraversion lead SNPs: .009 SD per effect allele (i.e., 50.35th vs. 50th percentile). [from p. 9]

**Functional Genomics**

  - Enriched gene-sets overlapped substantially across all Big Five (mean enrichment rank-order ρ = .72), indicating shared molecular/cellular systems despite modest genetic correlations. [from p. 9]
  - Prefrontal cortex gene expression enriched for E, C, N, O — but **not Agreeableness**. [from p. 9–10]
  - All Big Five showed strong enrichment in protein truncating variant intolerant gene-sets in neurons → evidence for **negative selection** on personality-relevant variants, against balancing selection theories. [from p. 10]
  - **Little support for dopamine/serotonin trait-differentiation hypothesis** (dopamine → E/O, serotonin → C/N/A). Neither dopaminergic nor serotonergic neuron types were among the most enriched for any trait. [from p. 10]

**Genetic Correlates and Consequences**

[from Figure 3, p. 14–15; these are domain-level genetic correlations with external phenotypes]

Selected highlights relevant to crosswalk framing:

  - **Neuroticism** genetically correlated with increased risk for all forms of psychopathology, especially internalizing disorders.
  - **Agreeableness** showed cross-cutting *protective* associations with psychopathology.
  - **Openness** showed broad genetic correlations with psychopathology — notably discrepant from the small/inconsistent phenotypic associations typically reported.
  - **Extraversion** genetics associated with *lower* internalizing but *higher* neurodevelopmental disorder risk.
  - **Conscientiousness** genetics associated with *lower* neurodevelopmental but *higher* compulsive disorder risk.

**Polygenic Prediction (PGI)**

[from Figure 2A, p. 11–12]

Mean standardized betas across 5 EUR cohorts:

  - Extraversion: Mβ = .19 (R² ≈ 3.6%)
  - Agreeableness: Mβ = .10 (R² ≈ 1.0%)
  - Conscientiousness: Mβ = .16 (R² ≈ 2.6%)
  - Neuroticism: Mβ = .16 (R² ≈ 2.6%)
  - Openness: Mβ = .17 (R² ≈ 2.9%)

Within-twin-pair PGI prediction was indistinguishable from population-level (mean within-family β = 100.9% of population-level β). [from p. 17]

**Dissociation Flags**

**None directly.** This paper operates entirely at domain level and cannot speak to facet-level or aspect-level dissociations.

However, several findings create *context* for the crosswalk:

**1. Agreeableness as a structurally anomalous domain**- **Evidence:** Lowest h²_SNP by every method. Lowest cross-geography genetic correlation (.65 vs. .86–.99 for others). Lowest cross-instrument genetic correlation (.68 vs. .82–.97). Only domain without prefrontal cortex gene expression enrichment. Within-family r_g with population GWAS significantly < 1 (r_g = .79). [from Table 1, p. 7, 9–10, 19]
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** If Agreeableness as a domain has less coherent genetic architecture across contexts, this raises the question of whether Compassion and Politeness (its two aspects) are being held together by something other than shared genetic etiology. The low cross-instrument r_g (.68) is particularly striking — different A measures may be tapping different genetic substrates. This is consistent with the possibility that Compassion and Politeness have substantially distinct genetic architectures.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Strong, but indirect (domain-level inference only).

**2. Openness within-family discrepancy**- **Evidence:** Only domain where matched population h²_SNP was significantly greater than within-family h²_SNP (19.4% vs. 13.4%, p = .02). Driven by inflated Estonian Biobank estimate. [from p. 19]
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** Suggests some confounding in population-level O estimates, possibly cultural transmission effects. If Intellect and Openness (the two aspects) differ in their susceptibility to cultural transmission, this could produce aspect-level dissociations in heritability.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Moderate — the discrepancy was driven by one cohort and may not generalize.

**3. Neuroticism genetic effects partially differ within-family**- **Evidence:** Population-to-within-family r_g = .75 (SE = .08, p < .001 for deviation from 1.0). [from p. 19]
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** Some portion of neuroticism's genetic signal at population level reflects family-level confounding. If Withdrawal and Volatility (its two aspects) differ in susceptibility to family-level confounding, the population-level heritability estimates for their constituent facets may be differentially biased.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Moderate.

**4. Conscientiousness shows unique indirect genetic effects**- **Evidence:** Only domain with significant non-transmitted parental genetic effects (8.1% of total PGI prediction, p = .01). [from p. 17]
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** Genetic nurture (parental behavior shaped by parental genotype) matters for C but not other domains. If this operates through Industriousness-related parenting behaviors vs. Orderliness-related ones, it could produce aspect-level dissociations.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive — single finding, small effect.

**5. Conscientiousness shows low self-other agreement in genetic architecture**- **Evidence:** Reporter perspective genetic correlation for C = .68, lower than any other domain (next lowest: N at .77). [from Table 1]
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** What observers see as Conscientiousness may be genetically partially different from what people report about themselves. If Orderliness is more observable and Industriousness is more self-perceived, this could produce aspect-level differences in genetic architecture across reporter perspectives.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive.

**Notes**

**Value for the crosswalk**This paper's primary value is as **anchoring context**, not as direct evidence for facet- or aspect-level structure. It tells us:

  - How heritable each domain is at the SNP level (useful for sanity-checking facet-level heritability estimates from twin studies)
  - That personality GWAS is minimally confounded by shared environment, assortative mating, or population stratification — which means twin-study heritability estimates for personality facets are likely more trustworthy than for, say, educational attainment facets
  - That Agreeableness is the domain most likely to harbor within-domain structural problems
  - That the disattenuated h²_SNP estimates (10.5%–16.2%) are substantially below twin-study h² estimates (typically 40–60%), confirming the large "missing heritability" gap for personality — most genetic variance is not captured by common SNPs

**What this paper cannot tell us**

  - Whether facets within the same aspect share SNP heritability more than facets in different aspects
  - Whether aspect-level genetic correlations are higher within-domain than between-domain
  - Whether individual facets show heterogeneous genetic architectures
  - Anything about the DeYoung (2007) aspect model's validity at the genetic level

**Missed opportunity**

This is one of the largest personality genetics datasets ever assembled (46 cohorts, up to 1.14M participants). Many contributing cohorts used faceted instruments. Facet-level or aspect-level GWAS from this consortium would be transformative for the crosswalk. The authors acknowledge this gap and point to it as future work. Until that future work is done, we are relying on much smaller, older twin studies for facet-level heritability data.

**Connections to other papers in the crosswalk**- **Jang, Livesley & Vernon (1996):** This paper's domain-level SNP h² estimates (disattenuated: 10.5%–16.2%) can be compared to Jang et al.'s twin-study domain h² estimates. The gap between SNP h² and twin h² reflects non-additive genetic variance + rare variant effects + remaining missing heritability.
  - **DeYoung, Quilty & Peterson (2007):** The finding that agreeableness has heterogeneous genetic architecture across instruments is consistent with Compassion/Politeness being genetically distinct aspects held together more by semantic similarity than shared biology.
  - The negative selection finding cuts against evolutionary psychology theories that predict facet-level variation is maintained by niche-specific balancing selection.

**Schwaba, Bleidorn, Hopwood, Manuck & Wright (2022)**

**Citation**

Schwaba, T., Bleidorn, W., Hopwood, C. J., Manuck, S. B., & Wright, A. G. C. (2022). Refining the maturity principle of personality development by examining facets, close others, and co-maturation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 122*(5), 942–958. https://doi.org/10.1037/pspp0000400

**Design**- **Type:** Two-wave longitudinal (plus cross-sectional component via MSEM combining within- and between-person data across age cohorts)
  - **N:** N_wave1 = 1,785; N_wave2 = 401 (longitudinal subsample)
  - **Age range:** 30–70 (Wave 1: 30–54; Wave 2: 40–70)
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (self-report, 240 items); NEO-FFI (other-report, 60 items, rated by 2 close others); MPQ short form (155 items, 11 facets); Cook-Medley Hostility Scale (50 items, 5 facets)
  - **Assessment interval:** 11–16 years (M = 14.72 years, SD = 2.99)
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Only two waves — limits reliability of within-person change estimates [from PDF p. 19]
    - Most participants (1,384 of 1,785) contributed only ONE wave — the MSEM trajectory estimates are heavily influenced by between-person (cross-sectional) age differences, not purely longitudinal change [from PDF p. 19]
    - Sample is 83% white, all from southwestern Pennsylvania — constrained generalizability [from PDF p. 19]
    - Close others were not tracked across waves (no IDs assigned), so rater changes confound other-report developmental estimates [from PDF p. 19]
    - COVID-19 halted Wave 2 recruitment in March 2020 — only 401 of planned sample completed [from PDF p. 8]
    - Exact facet-level slope estimates are in supplementary Table S11, which is not included in this manuscript PDF. The main text reports significance (p < .01 threshold) and direction, and Figure 2 shows approximate trajectories visually. I am reading approximate magnitudes from Figure 2 where possible, but these are visual estimates, not precise coefficients.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** This is one of the most useful papers for facet-level developmental trajectories.

Reported for:

  - All 30 NEO-PI-R facets (self-report, ages 30–70, MSEM trajectories)
  - 14 NEO-FFI facets (self-report AND other-report, latent difference scores across 2 waves)
  - 11 MPQ facets (self-report, ages 30–70, MSEM trajectories)
  - 5 Cook-Medley Hostility facets (self-report, ages 30–70)

**Not reported at aspect level.** The paper does not mention or test the BFAS aspect structure (DeYoung, Quilty & Peterson, 2007). However, the facet-level data can be mapped to aspects post hoc.

**Core Findings**

**Important methodological note**

The significance threshold used was p < .01 (not .05), chosen to balance Type I and Type II error across the many tests. Yearly 99% CIs ranged from ±.005 to ±.008 SD, meaning traits changing less than ~0.18 SD total across ages 30–70 would not reach significance. [from PDF p. 12]

All trait scores were grand-mean standardized — effect sizes below are in SD units across the 40-year span (ages 30–70) unless otherwise noted. [from PDF p. 10]

        ~ −0.35 SD

Within-domain co-development (NEO-PI-R):** Mean r_change = .33 among N facets [from Figure 4 annotation, p. 28]. This is the highest within-domain co-development of any Big Five domain.

**Crosswalk-relevant:** N facets show moderate heterogeneity. N3 Depression and N2 Hostility decline steeply; N1 Anxiety and N5 Impulsiveness do not change. N6 Vulnerability is already at floor by 30. If we map to aspects: Withdrawal facets (N1 Anxiety, N3 Depression, N6 Vulnerability) are split — Depression declines but Anxiety and Vulnerability don't. Volatility facets (N2 Hostility, N5 Impulsiveness) are also split — Hostility declines but Impulsiveness doesn't. N4 Self-Consciousness (typically Withdrawal) declines. The developmental pattern does not cleanly separate by aspect.

**Within-domain co-development (NEO-PI-R):** Mean r_change = .15 among E facets [from Figure 4, p. 28]. Very low — E facets develop largely independently.

**Crosswalk-relevant — this is a major finding for the aspect question:**

Mapping to DeYoung et al. (2007) aspects:

  - **Enthusiasm aspect** (E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, E6 Positive Emotions): Warmth increases, Gregariousness and Positive Emotions are flat. Heterogeneous within aspect.
  - **Assertiveness aspect** (E3 Assertiveness, E4 Activity, E5 Excitement-Seeking): Assertiveness increases, Activity declines, Excitement-Seeking declines steeply. Highly heterogeneous — E3 goes UP while its two aspect-mates go DOWN.

The within-domain co-development of r = .15 is the lowest (tied with Openness) of any domain. E facets are essentially developing independently. This supports treating E facets as poorly cohered at the aspect level for developmental purposes.

E3 Assertiveness and E4 Activity — supposedly on the same aspect — show the most dramatic divergence: +0.25 vs. −0.40 SD. That's a ~0.65 SD gap in developmental trajectory between aspect-mates.

Within-domain co-development (NEO-PI-R):** Mean r_change = .15 [from Figure 4, p. 28]. Same as Extraversion — very low.

**Crosswalk-relevant:**

Mapping to DeYoung et al. (2007) aspects:

  - **Openness aspect** (O1 Fantasy, O2 Aesthetics, O3 Feelings): Fantasy and Feelings decline, Aesthetics flat. All three go in a weakly negative or flat direction — somewhat coherent actually, in that none increases.
  - **Intellect aspect** (O4 Actions, O5 Ideas, O6 Values): Actions and Ideas are flat, but Values increases. Values diverges from its aspect-mates.

O6 Values is the outlier here: it's the only O facet that increases significantly. In the DeYoung structure, Values loads on Intellect. But developmentally it behaves completely differently from O4 Actions and O5 Ideas. This is a potential dissociation flag — though note that Values also has low conceptual fit with Intellect (it's about open-mindedness/tolerance, not cognitive engagement).

O3 Feelings declining is noted by the authors as potentially anomalous — no prior study found this, and related measures of emotional complexity tend to increase with age [from PDF p. 17].

**Crosswalk-relevant:**

Mapping to DeYoung et al. (2007) aspects:

  - **Compassion aspect** (A1 Trust, A3 Altruism, A6 Tender-Mindedness): Trust and Tender-Mindedness increase; Altruism is flat (but already at ceiling). Fairly coherent given the ceiling effect.
  - **Politeness aspect** (A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance, A5 Modesty): Straightforwardness and Compliance increase; Modesty is flat. Moderate coherence.

Agreeableness facets show the cleanest domain-level maturation story. The within-aspect patterns are reasonably coherent. Altruism's flatness is explained by ceiling at age 30; Modesty's flatness could be because it's classified as neutral (health rating 3.13) rather than truly healthy.

Within-domain co-development (NEO-PI-R):** Mean r_change = .31 [from Figure 4, p. 28]. Second-highest after Neuroticism.

**Crosswalk-relevant:**

Mapping to DeYoung et al. (2007) aspects:

  - **Industriousness aspect** (C1 Competence, C4 Achievement Striving, C5 Self-Discipline): Competence increases; Achievement Striving and Self-Discipline are flat (Self-Discipline at ceiling, Achievement Striving below ideal but still flat). Mixed.
  - **Orderliness aspect** (C2 Order, C3 Dutifulness, C6 Deliberation): Order is flat (below ideal); Dutifulness increases steeply; Deliberation increases. Dutifulness and Deliberation cohere but Order does not.

Important: C domain doesn't show significant maturation at the domain level, yet C3 Dutifulness shows one of the steepest positive slopes in the entire study. This is a case where domain-level analysis completely masks facet-level maturation.

C2 Order is the problem child in the Orderliness aspect — it doesn't move despite being below healthy ideal, while its aspect-mates increase. This is a mild dissociation flag within Orderliness.

Crosswalk-relevant:** Social Potency (conceptually related to E3 Assertiveness but behaviorally more dominant/exhibitionistic) declines steeply — converges with E4 Activity declining but diverges from E3 Assertiveness increasing. This suggests the "social dominance/energy" component declines with age while the "self-assurance/confidence" component increases. That's relevant to the Assertiveness aspect: the aspect may conflate two developmentally separable constructs.

Self vs. Other Perspectives on Maturation (NEO-FFI)**

[Source: PDF pp. 13–14, Figure 3 p. 27, Table S14 referenced but not in PDF]

Close others perceived **greater maturation** than the self in:

  - **Domains:** A, C, N (all p < .001) [from PDF p. 14]
  - **Facets:** Self-Reproach (N), Positive Affect (E), Prosociality (A), Goal-Striving (C), Dependability (C), Orderliness (C) — and Nonantagonism (A) and Anxiety (N) showed greater other-rated maturation but with convergence caveats [from PDF p. 14]

Self perceived **greater maturation** in one facet only:

  - Unconventionality (O) [from PDF p. 14]

This is the opposite of what was hypothesized and of prior studies. The authors attribute the discrepancy to relationship type — prior studies used primarily new romantic partners (honeymoon effect), while this study used diverse, established relationships (30% partners, 31% friends, etc.) [from PDF pp. 17–18].

**Co-Maturation Findings**

[Source: PDF pp. 14–15, Figure 4 p. 28, Figure 5 p. 29]

**NEO-PI-R facets (self-report):**

  - Mean co-development among healthy + unhealthy facets: r = |.11| [from PDF p. 15]
  - Mean co-development among healthy facets only: ~|.12| [from Figure 4 annotation]
  - Mean co-development among unhealthy facets only: similar magnitude [from PDF p. 15]
  - Co-development among (un)healthy facets was NOT stronger than co-development between (un)healthy and neutral facets [from PDF p. 15]
  - **Within-domain co-development was stronger than cross-domain** [from PDF p. 15]
  - Among all 18 A+C+N facets: Mean r_change = |.30| — stronger than other facet clusters [from PDF p. 15]

**Within-domain co-development (NEO-PI-R):** **Domain****** **Mean r_change******

        Neuroticism

        .33

        .31

        Agreeableness

        .21

        Extraversion

        .15

[Source: Figure 4 annotations, p. 28]

**NEO-FFI facets — self vs. other:**

  - Self-rated facet co-development: Mean r = |.13| [from Figure 5 annotation, p. 29]
  - Other-rated facet co-development: Mean r = |.26| [from Figure 5 annotation, p. 29]
  - Cross-perspective (same facet): Mean r = |.06| for facets; significant for only 7 of 14 facets [from PDF p. 15]
  - Other-rated Big Five co-development: Mean r = |.32| [from Figure 5 annotation, p. 29]
  - Self-rated Big Five co-development: Mean r = |.17| [from Figure 5 annotation, p. 29]

**Crosswalk-relevant:** The low within-domain co-development for E (.15) and O (.15) compared to N (.33) and C (.31) is consistent with the hypothesis that E and O each contain two relatively independent aspects. The facets within E and O develop so independently that the domain provides very little developmental coherence. N and C facets at least partially move together. A is intermediate (.21).

**Cross-Domain or Structural Findings**

**Correlation between facet adaptiveness rating and magnitude of age-graded change:**

  - r = .43 (p = .018, 95% CI [.07, .68]) across 30 NEO-PI-R facets [from PDF p. 12]
  - Facets rated as healthier showed greater age-graded increases. This is a moderate effect supporting the maturity principle at facet level, but note p = .018 (did not meet their own .01 threshold).

**Facets already at "mature" levels by age 30:**

  - N6 Vulnerability: near healthy ideal by age 30 [from PDF p. 13]
  - C5 Self-Discipline: above healthy ideal by age 30 [from PDF p. 13]
  - A3 Altruism: above healthy ideal by age 30 [from PDF p. 13]

**Facets below healthy ideal at BOTH 30 and 70 (failure to mature):**

  - N1 Anxiety, N5 Impulsiveness, E2 Gregariousness, E6 Positive Emotions, C2 Order, C4 Achievement Striving [from PDF p. 13]

**Dissociation Flags**

**E3 Assertiveness vs. E4 Activity (Assertiveness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Assertiveness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** E3 increases (~+0.25 SD), E4 decreases (~−0.40 SD) across ages 30–70. That's a ~0.65 SD divergence in developmental trajectory between supposed aspect-mates.
  - **Strength:** Strong. The trajectories go in opposite directions, both significantly.
  - **Additional convergence:** MPQ Social Potency (conceptually overlaps with both E3 and E4) declines steeply (~−0.50 SD), tracking E4's trajectory rather than E3's. This suggests that "vigor/energy/dominance display" components decline while "self-assurance/confidence" increases.

**E5 Excitement-Seeking (Assertiveness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Assertiveness aspect
  - **Evidence:** E5 declines steeply (~−0.65 SD), the largest decline among E facets. E3 Assertiveness increases. These are supposed to be on the same aspect.
  - **Strength:** Strong. E5's developmental profile looks nothing like E3's.
  - **Note:** E5 has long been a structural misfit — it's the facet most often flagged as poorly placed in E. This developmental divergence is consistent with that.

**O6 Values vs. O4 Actions and O5 Ideas (Intellect aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Intellect aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** O6 Values increases (~+0.30 SD); O4 Actions and O5 Ideas are flat. Values is the only O facet that increases significantly.
  - **Strength:** Moderate. The divergence is real but the magnitude is smaller than in E. O4 and O5 cohere with each other (both flat), so the issue is specifically that O6 doesn't belong with them developmentally.
  - **Caveat:** O6 Values measures tolerance/open-mindedness, which is conceptually distinct from intellectual curiosity. The developmental divergence may reflect that Values is more of a social-attitude facet than a cognitive-engagement facet.

**C2 Order within Orderliness aspect**- **Currently assigned to:** Orderliness aspect (with C3 Dutifulness and C6 Deliberation)
  - **Evidence:** C2 Order is flat (and below healthy ideal at 70); C3 Dutifulness increases steeply (~+0.50 SD); C6 Deliberation increases (~+0.25 SD).
  - **Strength:** Moderate. Two of three Orderliness facets increase while Order does not.

**N facets do NOT cleanly separate by aspect**- **Withdrawal** (N1 Anxiety, N3 Depression, N6 Vulnerability): Depression declines, Anxiety doesn't change, Vulnerability at ceiling.
  - **Volatility** (N2 Hostility, N5 Impulsiveness): Hostility declines, Impulsiveness doesn't change.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive. Both aspects are internally heterogeneous developmentally. But the heterogeneity doesn't create a clean *alternative* split either — it's more that individual N facets just behave idiosyncratically.

**E facets do NOT cleanly separate by aspect**- **Enthusiasm** (E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, E6 Positive Emotions): Warmth increases, other two flat. Heterogeneous.
  - **Assertiveness** (E3 Assertiveness, E4 Activity, E5 Excitement-Seeking): E3 up, E4 and E5 down. Maximally heterogeneous.
  - **Strength:** Strong for Assertiveness aspect; moderate for Enthusiasm.
  - **Within-domain co-development of r = .15 is consistent with the aspect structure being a weak organizer** — E facets develop nearly independently.

**Notes**- **Precision caveat:** The exact slope estimates from the MSEMs are in supplementary Table S11, which is not included in this PDF. All SD change values above are my visual approximations from Figure 2 (p. 26). They should be treated as approximate — probably within ±0.10 SD of the true values, but I can't be sure. Obtaining Table S11 from the OSF repository (https://osf.io/4ukbv/) would give exact numbers.**- Design interpretation caveat:** The MSEM approach combines within-person change (from the 401 longitudinal participants) with between-person age differences (from all 1,785 participants). The paper doesn't fully disentangle these. Between-person differences dominate the estimates because most participants contributed only one wave. This means potential cohort effects could bias the trajectories. The authors note two specific cases (Alienation and Achievement) where cohort trajectories differed from the overall MSEM trajectory [from PDF p. 13].**- Connections to other papers:** The E4 Activity decline converges with Terracciano et al. (2005), Bleidorn et al. (2009), and Soto & John (2012), all of which found declines in activity-related facets. The Social Potency decline converges with Donnellan et al. (2007) and Blonigen et al. (2008). E3 Assertiveness increasing while E4 Activity and Social Potency decrease is a pattern that should be visible across multiple papers — it's a strong developmental signal.**- Co-maturation for crosswalk purposes:** The finding that E and O facets have within-domain co-development of only r = .15 — compared to N at .33 and C at .31 — is directly relevant. This supports the idea that E and O each contain two relatively independent sub-groupings, which is the aspect hypothesis. But when we look at whether the actual aspect assignments capture the developmental coherence, the answer is mixed: the facets don't co-develop strongly even within their assigned aspects.**- What this paper CAN'T tell us:** No heritability data. No factor loadings or structural analysis. No test of aspect structure. The developmental data is useful for the crosswalk but it's only one evidence stream.**Terracciano, McCrae, Brant & Costa (2005)******

Terracciano, A., McCrae, R. R., Brant, L. J., & Costa, P. T., Jr. (2005). Hierarchical linear modeling analyses of NEO-PI-R scales in the Baltimore Longitudinal Study of Aging. *Psychology and Aging, 20*(3), 493–506. https://doi.org/10.1037/0882-7974.20.3.493

**Design**- **Type:** Longitudinal (accelerated longitudinal / HLM growth curve design)
  - **N:** 1,944 individuals; 5,027 assessments total
  - **Age range:** 20–96 years (M = 65.5, SD = 15.8); but most data from participants aged 60+
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (self-report, computer-administered ~95% of the time)
  - **Assessment interval:** Mean 2.8 years (SD = 2.2) between administrations; 1–11 administrations per person; data collected 1989–2004
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Self-report only (no observer ratings)
    - Under-representation of young adults (heavily weighted toward 60+; see Figure 1)
    - Convenience sample: predominantly white (70.3%), highly educated (M = 16.2 years education), community-dwelling volunteers — not nationally representative
    - Attrition: dropouts were slightly older, less educated, and higher on N at baseline; however, attrition did not significantly predict slope variability on any factor
    - Age centered on grand mean of 64.5 years; estimates at extremes of the age range (20s, 90s) are extrapolated from sparse data

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 30 NEO-PI-R facets reported with full HLM coefficients (intercept, linear slope, quadratic term, cohort and gender effects, random effects). This is the key table for our purposes (Table 2, pp. 28–29). Cross-sectional regression slopes also reported for all 30 facets (Table 1, pp. 26–27).

**Core Findings**

All HLM slopes below are expressed as T-score change per decade, centered on age 64.5. Quadratic terms are reported only where the quadratic model fit significantly better than linear. Source: Table 2 (pp. 28–29) and text discussion (pp. 7–10) unless otherwise noted.

**Within-domain heterogeneity (N):** N5 Impulsiveness is the clear outlier — monotonic linear decline with no late-life upturn, while all other N facets show curvilinear (U-shaped or J-shaped) trajectories. N6 Vulnerability shows the most dramatic late-life increase, diverging sharply from N5. [from text p. 9 and Figure 2b]

**Domain: Extraversion** **Facet****** **HLM Linear (γ₁₀)****** **HLM Quadratic (γ₂₀)****** **Curve shape****** **Notes******

        E (factor)

        −1.49 T/decade**−0.27**

        Convex: stable early, **accelerating decline** in old age

        88% stable variance; quadratic variance not significant

        E1: Warmth

        −0.45 T/decade**−0.31**

        Convex: peak ~age 60, then decline

        Gender effect on intercept (women higher)

        E2: Gregariousness

        −0.21 T/decade (ns)

        −0.15**

        Convex: near-stable with modest late decline

        Slope ns; weak effect

        E3: Assertiveness

        −0.11 T/decade (ns)

        −0.34**Convex: **increases** from 30 to ~60, then declines

        Later-born cohorts higher (cohort on intercept: +1.09**). Linear slope ns — the action is in the quadratic.

        E4: Activity

        −2.34 T/decade**−0.38**

        Convex: stable early, **steepest decline of any E facet** (and arguably any NEO facet) in old age

        Authors attribute to physiological aging. Cross-sectional R² = .085 — among largest age effects in the instrument.

        E5: Excitement-Seeking

        −1.36 T/decade**+0.19**

        **Concave:** largest decline in early adulthood, decelerating

        Gender effect on intercept: men much higher (−4.08**). Already declining substantially by age 30 from adolescent peak.

        E6: Positive Emotions

        −0.88 T/decade**−0.30**

        Convex: peak ~age 60, then decline

        Later-born cohorts higher (cohort on intercept: +1.07**); women higher.

**Within-domain heterogeneity (E):** Massive. E4 Activity shows the steepest monotonic-looking decline of any facet in the entire instrument. E3 Assertiveness *increases* into midlife before declining. E5 Excitement-Seeking has a concave trajectory (fast early decline, slowing), which is the *opposite* curvature from E4 Activity (convex — slow early, fast late). E1 Warmth and E6 Positive Emotions peak around 60. E2 Gregariousness barely changes at all. [from text pp. 7, 9 and Figure 3b]

**Crosswalk relevance (E):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), the BFAS Enthusiasm aspect comprises E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, and E6 Positive Emotions. The Assertiveness aspect comprises E3 Assertiveness, E4 Activity, and E5 Excitement-Seeking. The developmental trajectories partially support this split:

  - **Enthusiasm facets** (E1, E2, E6): relatively similar — stable or slight increase to ~60, then decline. But E2 is nearly flat while E1 and E6 show more movement.
  - **Assertiveness facets** (E3, E4, E5): **highly heterogeneous**. E3 increases then declines; E4 shows the steepest late-life decline; E5 has the opposite curvature (concave, with fastest decline early). These three facets do not share a common developmental trajectory.

        **Significant gender effect on slope** (γ₁₁ = −0.55**, one of only 2 facets with gender × slope interaction): women decline more steeply.

        O4: Actions

        −1.52 T/decade**−0.26**

        Convex: decline accelerating in old age

        Women higher (+5.10**)

        O5: Ideas

        −0.11 T/decade (ns)

        (linear model)

        **Essentially stable******Men higher (−1.31**); later-born cohorts higher (+0.72**)

        O6: Values

        −1.93 T/decade**

        (linear model)

        **Steepest linear decline of any O facet******Later-born cohorts lower (−0.69**). Authors note this is consistent with the belief that older individuals are less willing to re-examine values.

**Within-domain heterogeneity (O):** Dramatic. O2 Aesthetics and O5 Ideas are essentially flat across the entire lifespan (age 30–90), while O6 Values declines nearly 2 T-score points per decade and O3 Feelings and O4 Actions show accelerating late-life decline. [from text p. 9 and Figure 4b]

**Crosswalk relevance (O):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Openness/Intellect splits into Openness (O1 Fantasy, O2 Aesthetics, O3 Feelings) and Intellect (O5 Ideas, O4 Actions, O6 Values — though this assignment is debated and varies across papers). The developmental data here are awkward for either standard aspect assignment:

  - O2 Aesthetics (stable) and O3 Feelings (steep decline) are supposed to be aspect-mates but have divergent trajectories.
  - O5 Ideas (stable) and O6 Values (steepest decline in the domain) are supposed to be aspect-mates but are maximally divergent.
  - O4 Actions (steep convex decline) groups better developmentally with O3 Feelings and O6 Values than with O5 Ideas.

**Within-domain heterogeneity (A):** Moderate. A4 Compliance shows the steepest increase. A1 Trust has an inverted-U trajectory (increases then reverses), which is distinctive. A5 Modesty barely changes. [from text p. 10 and Figure 5b]

**Crosswalk relevance (A):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Compassion = A1 Trust, A3 Altruism, A6 Tender-Mindedness; Politeness = A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance, A5 Modesty.

  - **Compassion facets** (A1, A3, A6): heterogeneous. A1 Trust has an inverted-U (increases then declines); A3 Altruism is similar but weaker; A6 Tender-Mindedness shows increase then decline. They share the convex shape at least, but A1's reversal stands out.
  - **Politeness facets** (A2, A4, A5): heterogeneous. A2 and A4 both show clear linear increases, but A5 Modesty is flat. Two of three Politeness facets share a trajectory; the third dissociates.
