**Within-domain heterogeneity (C):** Very high. C6 Deliberation increases nearly 2 T-score points per decade — one of the largest effects in the entire study. C5 Self-Discipline shows steep late-life decline. C1 Competence peaks mid-life then drops. C4 Achievement Striving barely changes. [from text pp. 9–10 and Figure 6b]

**Crosswalk relevance (C):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Industriousness = C1 Competence, C4 Achievement Striving, C5 Self-Discipline; Orderliness = C2 Order, C3 Dutifulness, C6 Deliberation.

  - **Industriousness facets** (C1, C4, C5): All three show eventual decline, but with very different magnitudes. C1 has a dramatic peak-and-decline. C4 is nearly flat. C5 has the steepest late-life decline. They share a general "not-increasing" profile that contrasts with the Orderliness facets, which is mildly supportive of the aspect split.
  - **Orderliness facets** (C2, C3, C6): Heterogeneous. C6 Deliberation shows the largest increase of any C facet. C3 Dutifulness increases modestly then declines. C2 Order shows weak decline. C6 is the outlier.
  - **Cross-aspect comparison:** C6 Deliberation (Orderliness) and N5 Impulsiveness (Neuroticism) show converging trajectories — both monotonically change in the direction of reduced impulsivity across the lifespan. The authors explicitly note this convergence. [from text p. 10]

**Cross-domain or structural findings**- **Overall stability:** ~85% of variance in NEO-PI-R factor scores is between-persons (stable individual differences); ~15% within-persons. For facets, estimated stable variance ranges from 69% to 83% (M = 76%). [from text p. 8]
  - **Magnitude of change:** The normative changes amount to roughly 1 T-score point (~0.1 SD) per decade for the five factors. Facets show more variation, with some (E4 Activity, O6 Values, C6 Deliberation) changing at 1.7–2.3 T-score points per decade. [from text pp. 7, 10]
  - **Cross-sectional vs. longitudinal agreement:** Rank-order correlation across 30 facets between cross-sectional slopes (Table 1) and HLM longitudinal slopes = .72 (p < .001). [from text p. 9]
  - **Gender × Age interactions:** Only 2 of 30 facets showed significant gender effects on slope: O3 Feelings (women decline more) and A5 Modesty (gender differences widen with age). Both effects are very small. No gender × age interactions for any of the five factors in HLM analyses. [from text pp. 7–8]
  - **Cohort effects:** Modest. Later-born cohorts score lower on N (especially N3, N6) and higher on C (especially C1). Opposite to Twenge's (2000, 2001) findings of rising N and E in successive birth cohorts. [from text pp. 6–7, 9]
  - **Non-normative change:** Significant individual variation in slopes for all five factors. Not explained by attrition. May reflect genetic factors, disease (especially Alzheimer's — N6 Vulnerability and C are most affected), or life experience. [from text pp. 7, 11]

**Dissociation Flags**

**N5: Impulsiveness — dissociates from other N facets**- **Currently assigned to:** Neuroticism → Volatility aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Monotonic linear decline (γ₁₀ = −0.98 T/decade), no late-life upturn. All five other N facets show curvilinear trajectories with upturn after age 70–80. N5 is also the only N facet with no cohort or gender effects on intercept.
  - **Strength:** **Strong.** The developmental trajectory is qualitatively different — not just a matter of degree, but a different curve shape.
  - **Note:** N5 Impulsiveness shows developmental convergence with C6 Deliberation (both change monotonically toward lower impulsivity). This is consistent with the known cross-loading of N5 on Conscientiousness in factor analytic studies and with Jang et al. (1996) heritability data showing N5 shares genetic variance with C.

**N6: Vulnerability — dissociates from other N facets (in opposite direction from N5)**- **Currently assigned to:** Neuroticism → Volatility aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Near-zero linear slope but the largest late-life increase of any N facet (γ₂₀ = +0.53**). The authors link this to brain aging and Alzheimer's disease onset, connecting it to C more than to the affective core of N.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The late-life increase is distinctive, but the other N facets also show some upturn — N6 is just the most extreme. The Alzheimer's connection suggests overlap with C more than a clean dissociation within N.

**E4: Activity — dissociates from E3 Assertiveness (assigned aspect-mates)**- **Currently assigned to:** Extraversion → Assertiveness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** E4 Activity shows the steepest decline of any E facet (γ₁₀ = −2.34 T/decade, convex — accelerating decline). E3 Assertiveness *increases* until ~60 before declining (γ₁₀ = −0.11, ns; the action is in the quadratic γ₂₀ = −0.34**). These are supposed to be aspect-mates but have fundamentally different developmental profiles.
  - **Strength:** **Strong.** E4's decline likely reflects physiological aging (lower energy, reduced pace of life) rather than the agentic/dominance content of Assertiveness. E4 may be more physiological/temperamental, E3 more social-cognitive.

**E5: Excitement-Seeking — opposite curvature from E4 Activity (assigned aspect-mates)**- **Currently assigned to:** Extraversion → Assertiveness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** E5 has a *concave* trajectory (fast early decline, decelerating), opposite to E4's *convex* trajectory (slow early, accelerating decline). Both are assigned to the Assertiveness aspect, yet their developmental curves are mirror images.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** Both ultimately decline, so the direction is consistent — but the timing and shape differ markedly. E5's early decline may reflect sensation-seeking/dopaminergic processes distinct from E4's physical energy decline.

**O2: Aesthetics & O5: Ideas — stable; dissociate from declining O facets**- **Currently assigned to:** O2 → Openness aspect; O5 → Intellect aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Both are essentially flat across the lifespan (γ₁₀ near zero, ns), while their respective aspect-mates decline substantially (O3 Feelings declines −1.43/decade; O6 Values declines −1.93/decade).
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The stability of O2 and O5 is striking against their declining aspect-mates. However, this is a dissociation *within* each aspect rather than a *cross-aspect* reassignment signal. It could reflect that O2 and O5 tap more cognitive/intellectual content that is preserved with age, while O3/O6 tap more affective/motivational content that declines.

**O6: Values — dissociates from O5 Ideas (assigned aspect-mates under some mappings)**- **Currently assigned to:** Varies by paper. Often grouped with Intellect/O5.
  - **Evidence:** O6 Values declines −1.93 T/decade (linear, steepest O facet). O5 Ideas is flat. If these are aspect-mates, their developmental trajectories are maximally divergent within the O domain.
  - **Strength:** **Strong** as a dissociation flag; the trajectories are as different as any two facets within a domain can be.

**C6: Deliberation — diverges from other Orderliness facets**- **Currently assigned to:** Conscientiousness → Orderliness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** C6 increases +1.70 T/decade (steepest increase of any C facet; monotonic). C2 Order declines slightly; C3 Dutifulness increases modestly then declines. C6's trajectory is more similar to the inverse of N5 Impulsiveness than to its Orderliness aspect-mates.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** C6 is directionally consistent with what you'd expect from an Orderliness facet (increasing constraint), but the *magnitude* dwarfs C2 and C3. The convergence with the N5/impulsivity trajectory may be more informative than the aspect grouping.

**A1: Trust — inverted-U trajectory, unlike other Compassion facets**- **Currently assigned to:** Agreeableness → Compassion aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** A1 Trust increases until ~60, then *declines* (γ₂₀ = −0.23**). Other Compassion facets (A3 Altruism, A6 Tender-Mindedness) also show convex curves, but A1's decline is the most pronounced. The reversal in old age may reflect increasing wariness/cynicism rather than changes in the warm affiliative content that defines Compassion.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The shape is similar to A3 and A6 (all convex), but A1's decline is earlier and larger. This may reflect Trust's content (cognitive evaluation of others' motives) being distinct from the affective warmth of Compassion.

**Notes**

  - **This is a primary reference paper for facet-level developmental trajectories.** Table 2 provides the most complete set of HLM growth curve parameters for all 30 NEO-PI-R facets published as of 2005. The BLSA sample, while not nationally representative, is large and well-characterized.
  - **The cross-sectional slopes (Table 1) and the HLM slopes (Table 2) are complementary.** The cross-sectional data give linear slopes with R² values; the HLM data give growth curve parameters with random effects. Both are useful for the crosswalk, but the HLM estimates are better for capturing curvilinear effects and separating age from cohort.
  - **Caution about quadratic terms:** The quadratic model captures curvature but not necessarily the *correct* curvature. A U-shaped or inverted-U fit can be an artifact of sparse data at the extremes. The paper acknowledges under-representation of young adults and very old adults (Figure 1). The late-life upturn in N, in particular, should be cross-referenced against other studies.
  - **The paper does not report aspect-level analyses.** DeYoung et al. (2007) had not yet been published. The facet-level data are what matter here, and we map to aspects ourselves.
  - **The N5 Impulsiveness / C6 Deliberation convergence is noted explicitly by the authors** (p. 10): the monotonic increase in C6 Deliberation "is consistent with other indicators of a lifelong drop in variables related to impulsiveness." This cross-domain developmental convergence is a key signal for the crosswalk.
  - **Connection to Jang et al. (1996):** N5 Impulsiveness also showed distinctive heritability patterns in the twin study (shared genetic variance with C facets). The developmental data here converge with the heritability data in flagging N5 as structurally misplaced or at minimum straddling the N–C boundary.
  - **E4 Activity's steep late-life decline** may partly reflect physical health rather than personality *per se*. The items ("I often feel as if I'm bursting with energy") confound temperamental activity level with physical capacity. This is a measurement issue, not just a structural one — the facet may be measuring something different in 80-year-olds than in 30-year-olds.

**Jang, Livesley & Vernon (1996)**

**Citation**

Jang, K. L., Livesley, W. J., & Vernon, P. A. (1996). Heritability of the Big Five personality dimensions and their facets: A twin study. *Journal of Personality, 64*(3), 577–591.

**Design**- **Type:** Classical twin study (MZ vs. DZ reared together)
  - **N:** 123 MZ pairs (85 female, 38 male); 127 DZ pairs (71 female, 18 male, 38 opposite-sex). Total = 250 pairs, 500 individuals.
  - **Age range:** 16–68 years (MZ M = 30.9, SD = 11.8; DZ M = 31.7, SD = 11.7)
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R, Form S (Costa & McCrae, 1992). All 30 facets and 5 domains.
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (single administration)
  - **Key limitations:**- **Small sample.** 123 MZ and 127 DZ pairs is underpowered for detecting nonadditive genetic effects. The authors explicitly flag this: "twin data typically have low power to detect reliable nonadditive genetic effects" and "the present results should be considered tentative." [from PDF p. 12]
    - **Zygosity by questionnaire only** (Kasriel & Eaves, 1976; Nichols & Bilbro, 1966), not blood/DNA typing. [from PDF p. 4]
    - **Self-report only.** No informant ratings. Completed at home, with instruction to work independently. [from PDF p. 4]
    - **Volunteer sample** from Vancouver area, recruited via newspaper ads. Not nationally representative. [from PDF p. 3–4]
    - **Unequal sex distribution across zygosity groups.** DZ group includes 38 opposite-sex pairs; MZ group is same-sex only. Opposite-sex DZ twins may have lower correlations for reasons unrelated to genetics (e.g., sex-specific environmental effects). The authors tested this by re-running analyses with same-sex DZ pairs only and report "very similar" results. [from PDF p. 13]
    - **Cannot estimate A, D, C, and E simultaneously** from reared-together twins — the model is underidentified. They fit either ACE or ADE models depending on the MZ/DZ correlation ratio. This means shared environment (C) and dominance (D) are confounded — when D is estimated, C is forced to zero, and vice versa. [from PDF p. 5]
    - **Age and gender effects removed via regression** prior to heritability estimation, rather than modeled explicitly (gender-by-genotype analyses not possible due to small male DZ and opposite-sex DZ cell sizes). [from PDF p. 4]
    - **No multivariate genetic analyses.** The paper reports only univariate models — one per facet. It does NOT report genetic correlations between facets, bivariate models, or Cholesky decompositions. This limits what can be said about shared genetic architecture across facets or across domains.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 30 NEO-PI-R facets reported with MZ and DZ twin correlations, best-fitting model, and parameter estimates (h², d², c², e² with standard errors). Five domain scores also reported. [from Table 2, PDF pp. 8–9]

**Core Findings**

All estimates below from Table 2 (PDF pp. 8–9) unless otherwise noted. Parameter estimates are from the best-fitting model selected by AIC. The best-fitting model columns (h², d², c², e²) show which parameters are estimated — blank cells mean that parameter was not in the best-fitting model (i.e., set to zero).

Within-domain pattern (N):** Five of six N facets show additive genetic influence (h² range: .26–.44). The outlier is N2 Angry Hostility, whose genetic variance is entirely nonadditive (d² = .33, h² not estimated). The negative DZ correlation for N2 (−.01) is what drives this — MZ twins correlate .37 but DZ twins show essentially zero resemblance. The authors flag that this could reflect contrast effects (twins differentiating themselves on anger/hostility) rather than true dominance. [from PDF pp. 7, 12–13]

**Crosswalk relevance (N):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Volatility = N2 Angry Hostility + N5 Impulsiveness; Withdrawal = N1 Anxiety + N3 Depression + N4 Self-Consciousness + N6 Vulnerability. The heritability data show:

  - **Withdrawal facets (N1, N3, N4, N6):** All additive genetic influence. h² range: .26–.44. Reasonably coherent etiological profile, though N1 Anxiety is notably lower than N6 Vulnerability.
  - **Volatility facets (N2, N5):** Etiologically *dissimilar*. N5 Impulsiveness = additive (h² = .36); N2 Angry Hostility = entirely nonadditive (d² = .33). These two aspect-mates have qualitatively different genetic architectures in this sample.
  - **N5 Impulsiveness:** h² = .36, additive, with no evidence of nonadditive or shared environmental effects. This is etiologically similar to the Withdrawal facets (all additive) rather than to its Volatility aspect-mate N2. This is a suggestive signal, though the confidence intervals are wide enough that the *magnitude* differences between N facets' h² estimates may not be statistically distinguishable.

Within-domain pattern (E):** Split between additive (E2, E4, E6: h² = .29–.52) and nonadditive (E1, E3, E5: d² = .41–.43). The nonadditive facets show very low DZ correlations (.02–.14), driving the ADE model selection. [from text p. 9]

**Crosswalk relevance (E):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Enthusiasm = E1 Warmth + E2 Gregariousness + E6 Positive Emotions; Assertiveness = E3 Assertiveness + E4 Activity + E5 Excitement Seeking.

  - **Enthusiasm facets (E1, E2, E6):** Etiologically *mixed*. E1 Warmth is nonadditive (d² = .43); E2 Gregariousness and E6 Positive Emotions are additive (h² = .52 and .39). Within the same aspect, the type of genetic influence differs.
  - **Assertiveness facets (E3, E4, E5):** Also etiologically *mixed*. E3 and E5 are nonadditive (d² = .42 and .41); E4 Activity is additive (h² = .29) and has the lowest genetic influence of any E facet. E4 stands out within its aspect — both in magnitude and type of genetic effect.
  - **Cross-aspect comparison:** The additive/nonadditive split does NOT follow the aspect boundary. E1 (Enthusiasm) patterns with E3 and E5 (Assertiveness) as nonadditive; E4 (Assertiveness) patterns with E2 and E6 (Enthusiasm) as additive. The genetic architecture crosscuts the aspect structure.

Within-domain pattern (O):** Highly heterogeneous. Three distinct etiological profiles: additive genetic (O1, O4, O6), nonadditive genetic (O2, O5), and no genetic influence at all (O3 Feelings, best fit by CE model). [from text p. 9 and Table 3]

**Crosswalk relevance (O):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Openness (aspect) = O1 Fantasy + O2 Aesthetics + O3 Feelings; Intellect = O5 Ideas (primary marker, with O4 Actions and O6 Values variably assigned).

  - **Openness aspect (O1, O2, O3):** Maximally heterogeneous. O1 Fantasy is additive (h² = .34), O2 Aesthetics is nonadditive (d² = .57), and O3 Feelings shows NO genetic influence (c² = .39, e² = .61). Three facets assigned to the same aspect have three completely different etiological profiles.
  - **Intellect aspect (O5, and variably O4, O6):** O5 Ideas is nonadditive (d² = .52), while O4 Actions and O6 Values are additive. Also heterogeneous within the aspect, though less dramatically than Openness.
  - **O2 Aesthetics and O5 Ideas share the same etiological pattern** (both nonadditive, both with very low DZ correlations), even though they are assigned to *different* aspects. This is a cross-aspect etiological convergence.
  - **O3 Feelings is unique in the entire instrument** — the only facet whose best-fitting model has no genetic component at all. This raises the question of whether O3 measures something different from the rest of the O domain. The MZ correlation (.44) and DZ correlation (.35) are close, which is what drives the CE model. However, with only ~125 pairs per group, the confidence intervals on these correlations are wide, and the ACE model also fit the data adequately (Table 3 χ² = 0.34, vs. CE χ² = 1.65, both ns). The CE model was selected by AIC parsimony, not because ACE was rejected. The "no genetic variance" conclusion for O3 is fragile.

**Within-domain pattern (A):** Mixed. Three additive (A1, A2, A3), two nonadditive (A4, A6), one with no genetic influence (A5 Modesty). A5 is notable because DZ twins are *more* similar than MZ twins (.36 > .30), which is unusual and consistent with shared environmental effects or contrast effects inflating MZ dissimilarity. [from Table 2, text pp. 7, 9]

**Crosswalk relevance (A):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Compassion = A1 Trust + A3 Altruism + A6 Tender-Mindedness; Politeness = A2 Straightforwardness + A4 Compliance + A5 Modesty.

  - **Compassion facets (A1, A3, A6):** Etiologically mixed. A1 Trust and A3 Altruism are additive (h² = .30 and .34); A6 Tender-Mindedness is nonadditive (d² = .45). Different type of genetic influence within the aspect.
  - **Politeness facets (A2, A4, A5):** Also mixed. A2 Straightforwardness is additive (h² = .47); A4 Compliance is nonadditive (d² = .34); A5 Modesty has no genetic variance (CE model, c² = .33). Three facets, three etiological profiles.
  - **Neither aspect is etiologically coherent.** The additive/nonadditive/environmental-only split crosscuts the aspect boundary, just as it does for Extraversion and Openness.

**Within-domain pattern (C):** The most striking within-domain heterogeneity in the entire instrument. Three facets show additive genetic influence (C1, C3, C4; h² = .34–.44), and THREE facets show NO genetic influence at all (C2, C5, C6; all CE models). Half the domain appears heritable; half does not. [from text pp. 9, 11, 13]

**Crosswalk relevance (C):** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Industriousness = C1 Competence + C4 Achievement Striving + C5 Self-Discipline; Orderliness = C2 Order + C3 Dutifulness + C6 Deliberation.

  - **Industriousness facets (C1, C4, C5):** C1 and C4 are additive (h² = .34, .42); C5 Self-Discipline has NO genetic variance (CE model). This is a within-aspect dissociation: two of three Industriousness facets are heritable, one is not.
  - **Orderliness facets (C2, C3, C6):** C3 Dutifulness is additive (h² = .44); C2 Order and C6 Deliberation have NO genetic variance (both CE models). Again, one facet heritable, two not.
  - **C5 Self-Discipline (Industriousness) and C6 Deliberation (Orderliness) are etiologically similar** — both show no genetic variance and significant shared environmental influence. This pairs C5 with C6 across the aspect boundary. Recall from the developmental data: C5 and C6 also converge developmentally (C5 declines, C6 increases — both change monotonically in the impulsivity-control direction, and both converge with N5 Impulsiveness trajectories). The heritability data add another signal that C5 and C6 share something that is not captured by their respective aspect assignments.
  - **However:** The "no genetic variance" finding for C2, C5, and C6 is fragile. With ~125 pairs per group, the power to discriminate AE from CE models is low when MZ and DZ correlations are similar and modest in magnitude. The authors explicitly state these results are tentative. The Jang, McCrae, Angleitner et al. (1998) cross-cultural replication should be checked to see if this pattern holds.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**- **Shared environment (c²) is negligible for nearly all scales.** Only 5 of 35 scales (30 facets + 5 domains) had significant shared environmental components: A5 Modesty, C5 Self-Discipline, O3 Feelings, C2 Order, and C6 Deliberation. For all other scales, the best-fitting model dropped C. This is consistent with the broader behavioral genetics literature: shared family environment has minimal effects on personality. [from text pp. 7, 9]
  - **Nonshared environment is the largest single component for every scale** — ranging from .39 (Openness domain) to .77 (C6 Deliberation). This includes measurement error, which is confounded with e² in univariate models. [from Table 2]
  - **The type of genetic effect (additive vs. nonadditive) varies within every domain.** No domain has a uniform genetic architecture across all its facets. This is the paper's most important finding for our purposes — it directly supports the idea that domains are not etiologically homogeneous. [from text p. 13]
  - **Domain-level heritability can mask facet-level heterogeneity.** Example: Conscientiousness domain h² = .44, but three of its six facets show no genetic variance at all. The domain-level estimate reflects the aggregate, which obscures the fact that the domain is etiologically split. The authors make this point explicitly. [from text p. 13]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. N2 Angry Hostility — qualitatively different genetic architecture from other N facets**- **Currently assigned to:** Neuroticism → Volatility aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** N2 is the only N facet with nonadditive genetic variance (d² = .33) rather than additive (all others: h² = .26–.44). The DZ correlation is −.01 (near zero or negative), which is anomalous.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The additive/nonadditive distinction matters theoretically (nonadditive effects imply configural gene effects rather than simple parent-offspring transmission), but the sample is small and the authors flag contrast effects as an alternative explanation. The negative DZ correlation is suspicious.
  - **Note:** N2's aspect-mate N5 Impulsiveness is additive (h² = .36), making them etiologically dissimilar. N5's genetic profile is more similar to the Withdrawal facets than to its assigned aspect-mate.

**2. C2 Order, C5 Self-Discipline, C6 Deliberation — no genetic variance; etiological split within C**- **Currently assigned to:** C2 → Orderliness; C5 → Industriousness; C6 → Orderliness
  - **Evidence:** All three show no significant genetic variance (best-fitting model = CE). The other three C facets (C1, C3, C4) are heritable (h² = .34–.44). This splits the C domain into a "heritable" half (C1 Competence, C3 Dutifulness, C4 Achievement Striving) and a "non-heritable" half (C2 Order, C5 Self-Discipline, C6 Deliberation).
  - **Strength:** **Moderate** as a dissociation signal, but **fragile** methodologically. The sample is small, and the difference between AE and CE models when correlations are modest may not be reliable. The CE model was favored by AIC parsimony in each case, but the AE models also fit adequately by χ².
  - **Note:** The heritable/non-heritable split does NOT follow the aspect boundary. C3 Dutifulness (Orderliness) is heritable, while C5 Self-Discipline (Industriousness) is not. C5 and C6 cluster together etiologically across the aspect boundary. This is an important signal that converges with the developmental data (C5 and C6 both change monotonically in the impulsivity-control direction, converging with N5).

**3. O3 Feelings — no genetic variance; unique etiological profile within O**- **Currently assigned to:** Openness → Openness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** O3 Feelings is the only O facet with no genetic variance in the best-fitting model (CE: c² = .39, e² = .61). Its aspect-mates O1 Fantasy and O2 Aesthetics are heritable (h² = .34 and d² = .57, respectively).
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The CE model was selected by AIC, but ACE also fit the data. The MZ (.44) and DZ (.35) correlations are close enough to suggest shared environment rather than genetics, but confidence intervals overlap.
  - **Note:** O3 Feelings taps emotional awareness/responsivity, which is content-wise closer to Neuroticism or Agreeableness than to the aesthetic/imaginative content of the Openness aspect. The lack of genetic influence could reflect that O3 measures something environmentally shaped (emotion socialization?) rather than the same genetically influenced perceptual/aesthetic exploration that drives O2 and O5.

**4. A5 Modesty — DZ > MZ; no genetic variance**- **Currently assigned to:** Agreeableness → Politeness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** r_DZ (.36) > r_MZ (.30). This is reversed from the expected pattern if genetic effects are present. Best-fitting model is CE (c² = .33, e² = .67). This is one of only two facets in the entire instrument where DZ twins are more similar than MZ twins (the other is C5 Self-Discipline: r_DZ = .37 > r_MZ = .30).
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The reversed correlations could reflect contrast effects, shared environmental influences genuinely outweighing genetics, or sampling error in a small sample.

**5. E4 Activity — lowest genetic influence in E; additive while aspect-mates are nonadditive**- **Currently assigned to:** Extraversion → Assertiveness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** E4 Activity has h² = .29 (additive, lowest in E). Its aspect-mates E3 Assertiveness (d² = .42, nonadditive) and E5 Excitement Seeking (d² = .41, nonadditive) both show nonadditive genetic effects with very low DZ correlations. E4's genetic profile is more similar to E2 Gregariousness and E6 Positive Emotions (both additive, Enthusiasm aspect) than to its own Assertiveness aspect-mates.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The additive/nonadditive distinction is meaningful but unreliable in this sample size. The pattern is interesting but should not be given strong weight until replicated.
  - **Note:** This converges with the developmental signal: E4 Activity behaves differently from E3 and E5 developmentally (Terracciano 2005, Brandt 2023, Löckenhoff 2009) and now also etiologically.

**6. Additive/nonadditive genetic type does NOT follow aspect boundaries in any domain**- **Evidence:** In every domain, the split between additive and nonadditive genetic effects crosscuts the aspect boundary. Enthusiasm has both types; Assertiveness has both types; Openness-aspect has all three profiles (additive, nonadditive, no genetics); Compassion has both types; Politeness has all three; Industriousness has both additive and no-genetics; Orderliness has both additive and no-genetics.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate** as a global pattern, but the individual facet-level estimates are imprecise. The overall finding that genetic architecture is heterogeneous within domains is robust (the authors emphasize it as their main novel finding), but the specific additive/nonadditive assignments for individual facets may not replicate.

**Notes**

  - **This is a univariate study.** It tells us how much genetic and environmental variance each facet has, and what type (additive/nonadditive/shared/nonshared environmental). It does NOT tell us whether facets within the same aspect share the same genetic variance (i.e., are influenced by the same genes). For the crosswalk, we need multivariate genetic analyses or at minimum genetic correlations between facets. Jang, McCrae, Angleitner et al. (1998) and potentially other behavioral genetic studies may provide this. Without genetic correlations, we cannot distinguish between "N1 and N6 both have additive h² ≈ .35 because the same genes affect both" and "N1 and N6 both have additive h² ≈ .35 by coincidence, via different genes."**- The paper's main contribution to the crosswalk** is the demonstration that domains are NOT etiologically homogeneous. The authors state this explicitly: "genetic and environmental effects are not uniform across all facets of a dimension... some of the broad dimensions may not be etiologically homogeneous." [from PDF p. 13] This is the heritability evidence stream's version of the developmental finding that facets within a domain can have divergent trajectories.**- The "no genetic variance" findings for O3, A5, C2, C5, C6 should be treated cautiously.** In a small sample, when MZ and DZ correlations are similar and modest, the AE and CE models may both fit well, and AIC may favor the simpler CE model without strong evidence against genetic effects. The 1998 cross-cultural replication paper is essential for checking whether these facet-specific patterns hold up.**- N5 Impulsiveness / C5 Self-Discipline / C6 Deliberation convergence.** These three facets share content related to impulse control, and they show developmental convergence across multiple studies. The heritability data add a suggestive etiological signal: C5 and C6 are both non-heritable (CE models) and N5 is modestly heritable (h² = .36, additive). This does not directly confirm shared genetic variance — we'd need bivariate models for that — but it is consistent with the hypothesis that these facets share variance that crosses domain boundaries. The authors note that facet-level genetic analyses "may provide a different perspective on the structure of personality." [from PDF p. 13]**

  - Connection to the developmental evidence streams:

    - N2 Angry Hostility (nonadditive, dissociates from other N facets genetically) also dissociates developmentally — it decreases across adulthood while N1 Anxiety and N6 Vulnerability increase (Brandt 2023, Ringwald 2024).
    - E4 Activity (lowest h², additive, differs from E3/E5 nonadditive) also dissociates developmentally — steepest decline, only age-differential facet (Brandt 2023), only E facet responding to retirement (Löckenhoff 2009).
    - C5 Self-Discipline (no genetic variance) shows steep late-life decline (Terracciano 2005, Brandt 2023), while C6 Deliberation (also no genetic variance) shows steep increase. They share an etiological profile (environmental) but diverge in developmental direction — converging on opposite poles of the same impulsivity dimension.

  - **Limits of this paper for the master crosswalk table.** This paper provides the "Heritability (Jang)" column data for the master crosswalk, but the data are: (a) from a single small sample, (b) univariate only (no genetic correlations), and (c) subject to the ACE/ADE confound inherent in reared-together twin designs. The heritability estimates should be entered in the crosswalk with appropriate uncertainty markers, and the additive/nonadditive distinction should be noted but not heavily weighted until replicated.**Ringwald, Kaurin, Lawson, Wright & Robins (2024)******

Ringwald, W. R., Kaurin, A., Lawson, K. M., Wright, A. G. C., & Robins, R. W. (2024). The development of personality — from metatraits to facets — across adolescence and into adulthood in a sample of Mexican-origin youth. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 126*(6), 1140–1160. https://doi.org/10.1037/pspp0000487

**Design**- **Type:** Longitudinal (5 waves)
  - **N:** 645
  - **Age range:** 14–23
  - **Instrument(s):** BFI (ages 14, 16) and BFI-2 (ages 19, 21, 23). NOT the NEO-PI-R.
  - **Assessment interval:** Ages 14, 16, 19, 21, 23 (2–3 year intervals)
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Self-report only; no informant data. [from PDF p. 20]
    - Instrument switch at age 19 (BFI → BFI-2) introduces potential non-substantive variance, though partial scalar invariance was established. [from PDF p. 21]
    - BFI facet scales at ages 14 and 16 were constructed *ad hoc* by mapping BFI items onto BFI-2 facet scales. These have not undergone independent psychometric evaluation. Some had low internal consistency. [from PDF p. 21]
    - Sample is Mexican-origin youth in California, mostly from low-SES families (median household income $30–35K). Strengthens generalizability for this population but is a single sociocultural context. [from PDF p. 9]
    - Compassion and Productivity could not achieve longitudinal measurement invariance as latent variables and were modeled from observed scale scores. Results for these two facets should be interpreted cautiously. [from PDF p. 14]
    - Facet internal consistencies were generally low (median ω = .60, median α = .59). [from PDF p. 10]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level** — but these are BFI-2 facets (3 per domain, 15 total), NOT NEO facets (6 per domain, 30 total). Each BFI-2 facet is broader than a NEO facet and roughly approximates a BFAS aspect in content breadth.

BFI-2 facets reported:

  - **Extraversion:** Sociability, Assertiveness, Energy
  - **Agreeableness:** Compassion, Respectfulness, Trust
  - **Conscientiousness:** Organization, Productiveness, Responsibility
  - **Neuroticism:** Anxiety, Depression, Emotional Volatility
  - **Openness:** Intellectual Curiosity, Aesthetic Sensitivity, Creative Imagination

Also reported: metatraits (Plasticity, Stability) and Big Five domains.

Approximate BFI-2 facet → BFAS aspect mapping

This is my inference from item content, not a mapping reported in the paper:

        **BFI-2 Facet****** **Approximate BFAS Aspect******

        Creative Imagination

        Openness (the aspect)

**Caveat:** These mappings are approximate. The BFI-2 was designed with 3 facets per domain; the BFAS has 2 aspects per domain. The "third" facet in each domain doesn't map cleanly. Energy, for example, could span both Enthusiasm and Assertiveness depending on item content. Trust could be Compassion-adjacent rather than purely Politeness. I'm using my best judgment here, but these are not exact correspondences.

**Core Findings**

All statistics below from Table 1 (PDF p. 35–36) and Table 3 (PDF p. 38–39) unless otherwise noted. Slope means are unstandardized, from latent growth curve models with intercept set at age 14. Statistical significance assessed at two-tailed p ≤ .01.

**Key finding for crosswalk:** The domain declined, driven entirely by Energy. Assertiveness and Sociability were stable. In BFAS terms, this roughly means *neither* aspect showed the domain-level pattern — the Enthusiasm-adjacent facets (Sociability, Energy) diverged from each other, and Assertiveness was flat. The Extraversion domain result is an artifact of compositional weighting, not a coherent aspect-level process.

**Additional structural note:** Extraversion could not be modeled from its facets as indicators because the facets were virtually uncorrelated. The authors had to use item parcels instead. [from PDF p. 10] This is itself a dissociation signal — it suggests the three BFI-2 Extraversion facets are capturing substantially distinct constructs in this sample.

**Key finding for crosswalk:** In approximate BFAS terms, Compassion (aspect) increased while Politeness was split — Trust increased but Respectfulness was flat. This is a within-aspect dissociation for Politeness: two facets that should belong to the same aspect moved in different directions.

**Key finding for crosswalk:** Regardless of the Organization discrepancy, the pattern maps roughly onto an Orderliness vs. Industriousness aspect split: Organization (≈ Orderliness) showed minimal or no change, while Productiveness and Responsibility (≈ Industriousness) both increased. This is a clear within-domain divergence along the aspect boundary.

**Key finding for crosswalk:** This is the most striking within-domain divergence in the paper. In BFAS terms, Withdrawal (Anxiety + Depression) either increased or was flat, while Volatility decreased sharply. The two Neuroticism aspects moved in opposite directions developmentally. The domain-level "Neuroticism declines" narrative masks the fact that youth became *more* anxious while becoming *less* emotionally volatile. This is a strong dissociation flag for the Neuroticism aspect structure.

**Key finding for crosswalk:** In BFAS terms, Intellect (≈ Intellectual Curiosity) increased, while the Openness aspect was split — Aesthetic Sensitivity increased but Creative Imagination decreased. This means the two aspects diverged (both increased but one of the Openness-aspect facets went the other direction), AND there's within-aspect heterogeneity in the Openness aspect itself.

**Co-development of domains (Table 2, PDF p. 37)**

**Key finding:** The co-development pattern does NOT show clean discriminant validity for the two metatraits. Openness slope correlated *more strongly* with Agreeableness (.45) and Conscientiousness (.42) slopes than with Extraversion (.41). The authors explicitly note this (PDF p. 14): the pattern was not strongly discriminant. All domains tended to co-develop in the socially desirable direction.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**- **Plasticity metatrait increased despite Extraversion declining.** The shared variance of E and O increased, even though E's unique variance pulled it down. The authors interpret this as evidence that the exploratory processes of early adulthood are captured by the shared (metatrait) variance, not the domain-level totals. None of the Extraversion facets increased, so the increasing component of E captured by Plasticity does not map onto any measured E facet scale. [from PDF p. 16]
  - **Metatrait factor loadings:** Plasticity: E = .60, O = .65. Stability: A = .72, C = .70, N = −.60. These are mean standardized loadings across waves. [from PDF p. 13]
  - **No sex moderation** on any domain, metatrait, or facet trajectory except Anxiety and Emotional Volatility (both small effects). [from PDF p. 14–15]
  - **No nativity moderation** (born in Mexico vs. U.S.) at any level of the hierarchy. [from PDF p. 15]
  - **Rank-order consistency did not increase** from age 14 to 23 for domains or metatraits, contrary to meta-analytic findings. Two exceptions at facet level: Organization increased in consistency; Trust decreased. [from PDF p. 15]
  - **Within-person trajectory maintenance:** Residual correlations were all non-significant, meaning deviations from a youth's trajectory did not persist to the next wave. One exception: Sociability at age 16 showed persistent deviation to age 23, but the authors flag this as likely a multiple-testing artifact. [from PDF p. 15]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Neuroticism: Withdrawal vs. Volatility**- **Facets:** Anxiety (≈ Withdrawal), Depression (≈ Withdrawal), Emotional Volatility (≈ Volatility)
  - **Currently assigned aspects:** Anxiety and Depression → Withdrawal; Emotional Volatility → Volatility
  - **Evidence:** Anxiety *increased* (.014/yr), Emotional Volatility *decreased* (−.025/yr), Depression did not change. The two aspects moved in opposite directions.
  - **Strength:** **Strong.** The trajectories are statistically significant, in opposite directions, and the magnitudes are substantial. This is the cleanest aspect-level dissociation in the paper.
  - **Note:** This is consistent with the BFAS aspect distinction. It does NOT flag misassignment — it flags that the aspects are developmentally distinct, which is what the aspect model predicts. However, Depression (also Withdrawal) did not track with Anxiety, which could be a within-Withdrawal dissociation, though Depression was measured with a single item at ages 14–16 and had low consistency.

**2. Conscientiousness: Orderliness vs. Industriousness**- **Facets:** Organization (≈ Orderliness), Productiveness and Responsibility (≈ Industriousness)
  - **Evidence:** Organization showed minimal/no change; Productiveness and Responsibility increased.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The pattern aligns with the aspect boundary, but there is an inconsistency in the paper about whether Organization's slope was significant, and the overall magnitudes are small.

**3. Extraversion: internal incoherence**- **Facets:** Sociability (≈ Enthusiasm), Energy (≈ Enthusiasm), Assertiveness (≈ Assertiveness)
  - **Evidence:** Energy declined, Sociability and Assertiveness were flat. The facets were so weakly intercorrelated that E could not be modeled from its facets.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate to strong** as evidence that Extraversion lacks internal coherence in this age range and sample. However, the dissociation does not fall cleanly along the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness aspect boundary — both Enthusiasm-adjacent facets diverged from each other.

**4. Openness: within-Openness-aspect divergence**- **Facets:** Aesthetic Sensitivity (≈ Openness aspect) increased; Creative Imagination (≈ Openness aspect) decreased.
  - **Evidence:** Two facets that should both belong to the Openness aspect moved in opposite directions.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The slopes are both statistically significant but the magnitudes are modest. This could reflect genuine within-aspect heterogeneity or could be an artifact of the BFI-2's item content at these ages.

**5. Agreeableness: within-Politeness divergence**- **Facets:** Trust (≈ Politeness) increased (non-linearly); Respectfulness (≈ Politeness) did not change.
  - **Evidence:** Two facets that both map approximately to Politeness diverged.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** Trust's measurement properties were weaker (non-linear, observed-scale modeling for Compassion), and the approximate BFAS mappings are uncertain.

**6. Co-development does not discriminate metatraits**- **Evidence:** Openness slope correlated more with A and C slopes than with E slope. All Big Five slopes intercorrelated in the socially desirable direction. The Stability vs. Plasticity distinction was not supported by discriminant co-developmental patterns.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** This challenges the metatrait model as a developmental construct, even though the superordinate metatrait factors themselves both increased. The developmental co-movement is better described as a general maturity/social desirability factor than as two distinct metatrait processes.

**Notes**- **Instrument matters for downstream use.** This paper uses BFI-2 facets, which are approximately aspect-level constructs, NOT NEO facets. Findings cannot be directly entered into the master crosswalk's NEO facet rows without acknowledging this grain-size difference. They are most useful for the "aspect" columns.**- The Plasticity finding is interesting but potentially misleading for the crosswalk.** The authors found Plasticity increased even though Extraversion decreased. They interpret this as evidence for higher-order exploratory processes. But the co-development analysis shows Openness co-developed just as strongly with Stability domains as with Extraversion, undermining the discriminant validity of Plasticity as a distinct developmental construct. The metatrait result may be capturing general socially desirable change rather than a specific exploratory process.**

  - Connections to other papers in the crosswalk:

    - The Neuroticism Anxiety-increase / Volatility-decrease pattern should be compared against Terracciano et al. (2005) NEO-PI-R facet trajectories, which can decompose this further (N1 Anxiety, N2 Angry Hostility, N3 Depression, N4 Self-Consciousness, N5 Impulsiveness, N6 Vulnerability).
    - The Conscientiousness Orderliness/Industriousness split aligns with what Roberts et al. (2006) found for social dominance vs. social vitality within Extraversion — domain-level change obscures aspect-level divergence.
    - The Extraversion incoherence (facets too weakly correlated to model the domain from them) is an important structural finding that bears on whether E's aspect structure is stable across age and culture.

  - **Open question:** The authors note that the component of Extraversion shared with Openness (i.e., captured by Plasticity) increased, but this increasing component doesn't map onto any E facet scale. What is this shared variance actually measuring? This is relevant to whether Plasticity is a substantive construct or a statistical artifact of correlated measurement error or evaluative bias.**Brandt, Drewelies, Willis, Schaie, Ram, Gerstorf & Wagner (2023)**

**Citation**

Brandt, N. D., Drewelies, J., Willis, S. L., Schaie, K. W., Ram, N., Gerstorf, D., & Wagner, J. (2023). Beyond Big Five trait domains: Stability and change in personality facets across midlife and old age. *Journal of Personality, 91*, 1171–1188. https://doi.org/10.1111/jopy.12791

**Design**- **Type:** Longitudinal (4 waves over 11 years), accelerated longitudinal design analyzed with local structural equation modeling (LSEM)
  - **N:** 1,667 (577 at one wave, 254 at two, 246 at three, 590 at all four)
  - **Age range:** 25–99 years (M = 62.69, SD = 15.62); reliable parameter estimates for ages 35/40–80 only
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (240 items; 239 at wave 1 due to one missing E4 Activity item)
  - **Assessment interval:** 2001, 2005, 2008, 2012 (intervals of 4, 3, and 4 years; 11 years total)
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Self-report only; no observer/informant ratings. [from PDF p. 13–14]
    - Reliable LSEM estimates restricted to ages 35/40–80. Young adults (25–34) and very old adults (80–99) contribute data but no focal-point models were estimable for those age ranges. The most developmentally dynamic periods (young adulthood, fourth age) are therefore excluded from the age-moderation tests. [from PDF p. 5, 13]
    - Selective attrition: longitudinal participants (2+ waves) were more conscientious (d < 0.29) and more open (d < 0.25) than single-wave participants. [from PDF p. 5]
    - Sample is predominantly well-educated (M = 15.55 years education), American, Seattle metropolitan area — not nationally representative. [from PDF p. 5]
    - Cannot disentangle age effects from cohort effects for the rank-order findings. Cohort effects have been found at the trait level in this sample (Brandt et al., 2022) but no facet-specific cohort analyses exist. [from PDF p. 14]
    - Mean-level results cannot separate age from time/period effects. The study finds changes over time that are equal across ages, but these could reflect period effects rather than developmental processes. [from PDF p. 13]
    - Three facets (N4 Self-Consciousness, E3 Assertiveness, C1 Competence) produced negative latent variances in the growth models, preventing age-moderation tests for mean-level change on those facets. [from PDF p. 9]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 30 NEO-PI-R facets analyzed. Results reported for both rank-order stability (4-year latent retest correlations across age, Figure 2) and mean-level change (latent slope means across age, Figure 3). The key methodological advance over Terracciano et al. (2005) is latent variable modeling with full measurement invariance established across both time and age — this corrects for measurement error and ensures that observed differences reflect actual personality change rather than changing psychometric properties. [from PDF p. 1, 6–8]

**Important note on what this paper tests:** The mean-level analyses ask whether the *rate of change over the 11-year study period* differs by participant age. The answer is mostly no — nearly all facets showed the same rate of change regardless of whether the person was 40 or 70. This is a different question from "Do mean levels differ across age?" (cross-sectional) or "What is the shape of the age-graded trajectory?" (Terracciano et al.'s question). The paper reports significant slope means (change over time) but these are not age-differential. [from PDF p. 10, 12–13]

**Core Findings**

**Two types of results reported**

**1. Rank-order stability (Figure 2):** 4-year latent retest correlations as a continuous function of age. These show how stable individuals' relative positions are, and whether that stability varies by age.

**2. Mean-level change (Figure 3):** Latent slope means from second-order growth curve models, as a continuous function of age. These show whether the rate of mean-level change (over the 11-year study window) differs by participant age.

The paper does NOT report the absolute slope means in a table — the primary reporting is via Figures 2 and 3 and text descriptions. I cannot extract exact numerical values for many parameters.

**Rank-order stability (from Figure 2, text pp. 8–9):**

  - All N facets showed substantial rank-order stabilities (r > .75).
  - N6 Vulnerability showed consistently *higher* stability than the N domain across the age range. [from PDF p. 8]
  - N1 Anxiety and N4 Self-Consciousness showed significantly declining rank-order stability in old age (permutation test). [from PDF p. 8–9]
  - N5 Impulsiveness and N2 Angry Hostility showed a pattern roughly consistent with cumulative continuity (peak around 50, decline after). [from PDF p. 11]

**Crosswalk relevance (N):**- **Volatility facets (N2, N5):** Both DECREASED over time. Coherent developmental profile.
  - **Withdrawal facets (N1, N3, N4, N6):** Mixed. N1 Anxiety and N6 Vulnerability both INCREASED; N3 Depression showed no significant change; N4 could not be tested. Two of three testable Withdrawal facets moved in the same direction (increase), which is partially coherent.
  - **Cross-aspect dissociation:** Volatility facets decreased while Withdrawal facets (N1, N6) increased. The two aspects moved in opposite directions, supporting the aspect distinction.
  - **N5 Impulsiveness / C6 Deliberation convergence:** N5 decreased and C6 increased (see Conscientiousness below). This cross-domain convergence replicates Terracciano et al. (2005) and reinforces the signal that N5 shares variance with C facets — consistent with Jang et al. (1996) heritability data showing shared genetic variance between N5 and C.
  - **Comparison with Terracciano et al. (2005):** The BLSA study found all N facets declining with curvilinear upturn, except N5 (monotonic decline). Brandt et al. find a more split picture: N2 and N5 decreased, N1 and N6 increased, N3 flat. The discrepancy may reflect (a) different age emphasis (Brandt's effective range is 35–80; Terracciano had better young-adult data), (b) period/time effects in Brandt vs. age effects in Terracciano, or (c) the latent vs. manifest modeling difference.
  - **Comparison with Ringwald et al. (2024):** The adolescent/young adult sample (ages 14–23) found Anxiety increasing and Emotional Volatility decreasing — the same cross-aspect dissociation pattern seen here in midlife/old age.

**Rank-order stability (from Figure 2, text pp. 8–9, 11):**

  - E4 Activity and E5 Excitement-Seeking showed roughly cumulative-continuity patterns (peak ~50, decline). [from PDF p. 11]
  - E3 Assertiveness showed higher rank-order stability in the Figure 2 plot (around .90 across much of the range), though age moderation could not be formally tested.

**Crosswalk relevance (E):**- **Enthusiasm facets (E1, E2, E6):** E1 Warmth was STABLE while E2 and E6 both DECREASED. Within-aspect heterogeneity: the sociability/warmth component does not track with the positive-emotions/gregariousness components.
  - **Assertiveness facets (E3, E4, E5):** All three DECREASED — coherent direction. But E4 was the ONLY facet in the entire study showing age-differential change, meaning the rate of E4 decline was steeper for older participants. This replicates Terracciano et al.'s finding that E4 has the steepest late-life decline.
  - **E4 Activity remains the outlier.** The age-differential finding for E4 — and for no other facet — is a strong signal that E4's late-life decline reflects physiological aging (reduced physical energy, stamina) rather than personality change per se. This echoes the concern flagged in the Terracciano extraction about E4 items confounding temperament with physical capacity.

**Rank-order stability (from Figure 2, text pp. 8–9, 11):**

  - O4 Actions showed the highest rank-order stability of any O facet — *higher than the domain itself* — and stability increased with age. [from PDF p. 8, 11]
  - O2 Aesthetics showed declining stability in old age (permutation test significant). [from PDF p. 8]
  - O5 Ideas showed a "waved" pattern — lower stability in midlife, higher in old age — the opposite of cumulative continuity. [from PDF p. 8, 11]

**Crosswalk relevance (O):**- **Openness aspect (O1, O2, O3):** All three DECREASED — coherent.
  - **Intellect aspect (O5 Ideas):** Also DECREASED — same direction as Openness-aspect facets.
  - **O6 Values:** INCREASED — the only O facet going in the opposite direction from the rest. This is a striking reversal of Terracciano et al. (2005), where O6 Values showed the *steepest decline* of any O facet. The discrepancy could reflect period effects (people became more open to re-examining values between 2001 and 2012 regardless of age), cohort effects, or the latent vs. manifest modeling difference.
  - **O4 Actions and O5 Ideas rank-order patterns:** These two facets, which load on different aspects (O4 on Openness; O5 on Intellect in 2007), showed opposite rank-order stability patterns (O4 increasing with age, O5 U-shaped). This is a within-domain dissociation in rank-order stability that does not map onto the aspect boundary.

**Rank-order stability (from Figure 2, text pp. 8–9, 11–12):**

  - A5 Modesty and A3 Altruism had consistently *higher* rank-order stabilities than the A domain. [from PDF p. 8]
  - The A facets showed the most diverse patterns of rank-order stability of any domain. [from PDF p. 8, 11]
  - A1 Trust: inverted U-shape in rank-order stability (peak midlife, decline in old age). [from PDF p. 8, 12]
  - A5 Modesty: lowest stability between 50–60, then increasing — opposite of cumulative continuity. [from PDF p. 11–12]
  - A4 Compliance: declining stability in old age (consistent with cumulative continuity). [from PDF p. 11]
  - A2 Straightforwardness: highest in 40s and 70s, lowest in 60s — a non-monotonic pattern. [from PDF p. 8]

**Crosswalk relevance (A):**- **Mean-level change:** All six A facets increased. This is the only domain with complete directional coherence. No within-domain or within-aspect dissociations in mean-level change.
  - **Rank-order stability:** Despite uniform mean-level increases, the rank-order stability patterns varied greatly across facets. A1 Trust (inverted-U) and A5 Modesty (U-shaped) had opposite stability trajectories. This shows that even when facets move in the same mean-level direction, the reshuffling of individuals can differ substantially.
  - **Compassion facets (A1, A3, A6):** Mean levels all increased. Rank-order: A1 Trust peaked midlife then declined; A3 Altruism was consistently high; A6 not specifically described but from Figure 2 appears moderately stable. Rank-order heterogeneity within the Compassion aspect.
  - **Politeness facets (A2, A4, A5):** Mean levels all increased. Rank-order: A4 Compliance declined with age; A5 Modesty showed U-shape; A2 Straightforwardness was non-monotonic. Rank-order heterogeneity within the Politeness aspect.

**Rank-order stability (from Figure 2, text pp. 8–9, 11–12):**

  - C4 Achievement Striving showed consistently higher stability than the C domain. [from PDF p. 8]
  - C5 Self-Discipline showed significantly declining stability in old age (permutation test). Stability peaked around 45–49 and declined most strongly after 64. [from PDF p. 8, 12]
  - C6 Deliberation and C1 Competence showed *increasing* stability with age (opposite of cumulative continuity). [from PDF p. 11]
  - C2 Order: lowest stability between 50–60, then increasing — U-shaped, opposite of cumulative continuity. [from PDF p. 11–12]

**Crosswalk relevance (C):**- **Industriousness facets (C1, C4, C5):** C5 DECREASED; C4 showed no significant change; C1 not testable. Mixed.
  - **Orderliness facets (C2, C3, C6):** C6 INCREASED; C2 and C3 showed no significant change. C6 is the active member of the Orderliness aspect.
  - **Cross-aspect dissociation (C5 vs. C6):** C5 Self-Discipline decreased while C6 Deliberation increased. These are on opposite sides of the aspect boundary (Industriousness vs. Orderliness), and they moved in opposite directions. The authors explicitly note this: "mean-level change in two facets of conscientiousness, with decreases in self-discipline and increases in deliberation. These opposite patterns might wash out effects on the trait-level." [from PDF p. 13]
  - **N5/C6 convergence (replicated):** C6 Deliberation increased and N5 Impulsiveness decreased — both changing monotonically in the direction of reduced impulsivity. This replicates the N5/C6 cross-domain convergence reported by Terracciano et al. (2005).
  - **Rank-order: C5 vs. C6 also dissociate.** C5 stability declined with age while C6 stability increased. These aspect-mates show opposite developmental dynamics in both mean-level and rank-order.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**

  - **Age-differential mean-level change:** Only E4 Activity showed statistically significant age moderation of the latent slope. All other 26 testable facets (3 had negative variances) changed at the same rate regardless of participant age. [from PDF p. 9–10]
  - **The midlife-to-old-age window is a "stabilization" phase.** The authors argue that by age 35, most maturation has occurred, and the fourth-age losses (post-85) are not represented. The age range studied may be inherently stable, explaining the lack of age-differential effects. [from PDF p. 13]
  - **Time effects vs. age effects.** The paper finds mean-level change across the 11-year study window (e.g., all A facets increased, N2 and N5 decreased, etc.) but these changes do not vary by age. This cannot distinguish developmental change from period effects.
  - **Latent modeling matters.** The authors note that previous studies using manifest sum scores may have detected age-related patterns that partly reflected measurement artifacts. The establishment of scalar invariance across both time and age is a methodological strength. [from PDF p. 12–13]
  - **Rank-order stability patterns are more diverse at the facet level than at the trait level.** This is the paper's headline finding for rank-order. The broad trait patterns (inverted-U or flat) mask substantial facet-to-facet variation. [from PDF p. 11, 14]
  - **Significant slope variance for all facets.** Individual differences in rates of change were significant for every facet, meaning that while the average trajectory does not differ by age, people within any age cohort vary substantially in how much they change. [from PDF p. 10]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. N2 Angry Hostility & N5 Impulsiveness vs. N1 Anxiety & N6 Vulnerability — Volatility vs. Withdrawal**- **Facets:** N2 + N5 (Volatility) decreased; N1 + N6 (Withdrawal) increased.
  - **Currently assigned:** N2, N5 → Volatility; N1, N6 → Withdrawal. [DeYoung et al., 2007]
  - **Evidence:** Opposite mean-level directions across the two aspects.
  - **Strength:** **Strong.** This is a clean cross-aspect dissociation that directly replicates the Ringwald et al. (2024) finding in a completely different sample and age range. Volatility facets decrease, Withdrawal facets increase — consistent across adolescence, young adulthood, midlife, and old age (across two studies). This does NOT suggest misassignment — it *validates* the aspect distinction by showing the aspects are developmentally independent.

**2. C5 Self-Discipline vs. C6 Deliberation — cross-aspect mean-level dissociation**- **Facets:** C5 (Industriousness) decreased; C6 (Orderliness) increased.
  - **Currently assigned:** C5 → Industriousness; C6 → Orderliness. [DeYoung et al., 2007]
  - **Evidence:** Opposite directions of mean-level change; also opposite rank-order stability trends (C5 declining, C6 increasing with age).
  - **Strength:** **Moderate to strong.** The mean-level dissociation is consistent with the aspect split. However, the other facets within each aspect (C2, C3 for Orderliness; C1, C4 for Industriousness) mostly showed no significant change, so this is really a C5-vs.-C6 dissociation rather than a full Industriousness-vs.-Orderliness split.

**3. N5 Impulsiveness / C6 Deliberation cross-domain convergence (replicated)**- **Facets:** N5 Impulsiveness decreased; C6 Deliberation increased.
  - **Currently assigned:** N5 → N-Volatility; C6 → C-Orderliness.
  - **Evidence:** Both change monotonically in the direction of reduced impulsivity. This replicates Terracciano et al. (2005) explicitly, who also noted this convergence.
  - **Strength:** **Strong** as a cross-domain convergence signal. This is now replicated in two independent longitudinal samples (BLSA and SLS), using different modeling approaches (manifest HLM vs. latent LSEM). It reinforces the signal that N5 shares variance with C facets — consistent with Jang et al. (1996) heritability data showing shared genetic variance between N5 and C.

**4. E4 Activity — only facet with age-differential change**- **Currently assigned:** Extraversion → Assertiveness aspect.
  - **Evidence:** E4 Activity was the ONLY facet (out of 27 testable) showing statistically significant age moderation of mean-level change — decline was steeper at older ages.
  - **Strength:** **Strong** as a dissociation from all other personality facets, and specifically from its Assertiveness aspect-mates (E3 and E5 showed no age-differential patterns). This replicates Terracciano et al.'s finding that E4 has the steepest late-life decline and supports the interpretation that E4 increasingly reflects physical capacity rather than personality in old age.

**5. E1 Warmth vs. E2 Gregariousness / E6 Positive Emotions — within-Enthusiasm dissociation**- **Currently assigned:** All three → Enthusiasm aspect.
  - **Evidence:** E1 Warmth showed no significant mean-level change; E2 and E6 both decreased.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The direction of E2 and E6 is consistent; E1 is the outlier within the aspect. However, the effect sizes are unclear from the paper's figure-based reporting.

**6. O6 Values vs. all other O facets — direction reversal**- **Currently assigned:** O6 → Openness aspect (in 2007 loadings).
  - **Evidence:** O6 Values INCREASED while all other O facets (O1–O5) decreased. This is a complete direction reversal for O6 relative to its domain.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The O6 finding directly contradicts Terracciano et al. (2005), where O6 Values showed the steepest *decline* of any O facet. The discrepancy could reflect period effects (people became more open to re-examining values between 2001 and 2012 regardless of age), or it could reflect the latent vs. manifest modeling difference. This needs to be flagged but interpreted cautiously.

**7. Rank-order stability: Agreeableness facets show maximal within-domain heterogeneity**- **Evidence:** A facets showed the most variable rank-order stability patterns of any domain. A1 Trust (inverted-U), A5 Modesty (U-shape), A4 Compliance (declining), and A2 Straightforwardness (non-monotonic) had markedly different trajectories.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive** for the crosswalk. The diversity of rank-order patterns does not map cleanly onto the Compassion/Politeness aspect boundary — both aspects show internal heterogeneity. This suggests that the developmental dynamics of individual differences in A facets are driven by facet-specific processes (e.g., trust-relevant life experiences, modesty-relevant social role changes) rather than aspect-level processes.

**Notes**

  - **Methodological advance over Terracciano et al. (2005).** Brandt et al. use latent variable models with established scalar measurement invariance across both time and age. Terracciano et al. used manifest sum scores in HLM. The latent approach corrects for measurement error and confirms that observed changes are not measurement artifacts. This is a meaningful upgrade, and where the two studies disagree, the latent results deserve serious weight.**- Different questions, complementary answers.** Terracciano et al. modeled age-graded trajectories (how do mean levels change as a function of age?). Brandt et al. modeled time-based change with age as a moderator (does the rate of change over 11 years differ by age?). Finding that age does not moderate the rate of change does NOT mean there are no age differences in mean levels — it means that within the 35–80 window, 40-year-olds and 70-year-olds changed at the same rate over 11 years. These are compatible with Terracciano's curvilinear findings if the midlife-to-old-age segment happens to be relatively linear for most facets.**- The O6 Values reversal is puzzling.** Terracciano (2005) found O6 declining ~1.93 T/decade. Brandt et al. find O6 *increasing* over time. If this is a period effect (people becoming more open to re-examining values in the 2001–2012 window), it could actually explain the discrepancy — Terracciano's study ran 1989–2004 and modeled age, not time. Different historical periods, different effects. This is worth flagging but not overinterpreting.**- Confirmation of the N5/C6 cross-domain convergence.** This is now the second independent longitudinal sample (after BLSA) showing N5 Impulsiveness and C6 Deliberation changing in converging directions (toward reduced impulsivity). Combined with the Jang et al. (1996) shared-genetic-variance finding, this is accumulating evidence that N5 is structurally positioned at the N–C boundary.**

  - The Neuroticism Volatility/Withdrawal split is now replicated across three studies and two age ranges:

    - Ringwald et al. (2024): ages 14–23, Anxiety increased / Emotional Volatility decreased
    - Brandt et al. (2023): ages 35–80, Anxiety+Vulnerability increased / Angry Hostility+Impulsiveness decreased
    - Terracciano et al. (2005): ages 20–96, N5 Impulsiveness showed qualitatively different trajectory from other N facets (monotonic vs. curvilinear)

  - The consistency of the Volatility-Withdrawal developmental dissociation across different samples, instruments, and age ranges is a strong signal that these aspects capture genuinely distinct processes.**- E4 Activity continues to stand alone.** It is the only facet in the entire study with age-differential mean-level change. Combined with Terracciano's finding that E4 has the steepest late-life decline, this facet is increasingly looking like it measures something different from personality in older adults — namely, physical energy/vitality. The items ("I often feel as if I'm bursting with energy") may be more confounded with physical health status in older populations than other E facets.**- Limitations for the master crosswalk.** The paper does not report numerical slope means in a table, making it impossible to extract exact effect sizes for most facets. The findings are reported primarily as "statistically significant increase/decrease" and via figures. Direction of change is clear; magnitude is not precisely extractable. The rank-order stability data are also figure-based without tabled exact values, though the supplementary tables (Tables S13–S17) may contain these — I do not have access to the supplementary materials.**Löckenhoff, Terracciano & Costa (2009)**

**Citation**

Löckenhoff, C. E., Terracciano, A., & Costa, P. T., Jr. (2009). Five-factor model personality traits and the retirement transition: Longitudinal and cross-sectional associations. *Psychology and Aging, 24*(3), 722–728. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0015121

**Design**- **Type:** Longitudinal (2 time points) + cross-sectional
  - **N:** 367 longitudinal (304 remained employed, 63 retired between assessments); 144 cross-sectional retirees
  - **Age range:** Longitudinal workers M = 51.13 (SD = 5.80); longitudinal retirees M = 60.59 (SD = 7.83); cross-sectional retirees M = 63.15 (SD = 9.45)
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (240 items, all 30 facets)
  - **Assessment interval:** 1995 to 2004 (~9 years between personality assessments)
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Very small retiree subsample (n = 63 longitudinal retirees). With 30 facets tested, statistical power for detecting facet-level effects is low. [from PDF p. 3, 5]
    - Only 2 time points — cannot detect curvilinear trajectories or distinguish immediate vs. delayed retirement effects. [from PDF p. 7]
    - High attrition from initial ECA recruitment (3481 → 1071 → 367 with personality data). Personality data available for only 42% at 1995 wave. Survivors were younger, more educated. [from PDF pp. 3–4, footnotes 1–2]
    - Self-report only; no informant ratings. [from PDF p. 4]
    - Sample drawn from East Baltimore ECA — community-based but not nationally representative. Predominantly Black and White; 2.6% other ethnicities excluded. [from PDF pp. 3–4]
    - Retirement was measured as a binary self-report variable; no information about partial retirement transitions or gradual workforce exit. [from PDF p. 4]
    - Retirement occurred at varying points within the ~9-year window; the personality assessment at 2004 captures change at different post-retirement durations. Mean years since retirement = 3.73 (SD = 2.72) for the longitudinal retiree subsample. [from PDF p. 4, Table 1]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 30 NEO-PI-R facets reported in Table 2 (PDF pp. 11–12), with standardized betas for the retirement predictor (Block 4) after controlling for baseline personality, demographics, and mental/physical health. This is the table that matters for the crosswalk.

Cross-sectional analyses (Table 3, PDF p. 13) report personality correlates of retirement-related variables at the factor level, with selected facet-level follow-up described in the text (PDF p. 6) but not tabled.

**Core Findings**

All longitudinal findings below are from Table 2 (PDF pp. 11–12). The design is a hierarchical regression predicting 2004 personality from: Block 1 = 1995 personality (controls for baseline), Block 2 = demographics, Block 3 = mental/physical health, Block 4 = retirement status (retired between assessments vs. remained employed). The β values for Block 4 represent the standardized effect of retirement on personality change, net of all controls. The authors flag p < .01 as their interpretive threshold for facet-level effects to guard against alpha inflation from 30 tests.

**Crosswalk relevance (E):** E4 Activity is the sole E facet that moved in response to retirement. Its Assertiveness aspect-mates (E3, E5) did not change. Its Enthusiasm aspect non-mates (E1, E2, E6) also did not change. This is consistent with the accumulating signal from Terracciano et al. (2005) and Brandt et al. (2023) that E4 behaves differently from all other E facets — and specifically from its assigned aspect-mates — across multiple types of evidence (age-graded trajectories, age-differential change rates, and now life-event response).

The authors interpret the E4 decline as reflecting reduced pace and vigor after leaving the workforce, which they link to activity/role theory. [from text p. 6] The change amounted to ~4 T-score points, which is roughly 4× the rate of normative age-related change per decade reported by Terracciano et al. (2005). [from text p. 6]
