**Within-domain heterogeneity notes (Extraversion):**- **E4 Activity clusters genetically with Conscientiousness facets, not with other E facets.** This is stated explicitly in the text: "the personality facet NEO E4 (activity) clusters closer to components of NEO C (conscientiousness) than it does to other facets of NEO E." [from PDF p. 9] The genetic factor analysis (Table S3, not available in PDF) reportedly confirms this. This is a **major finding** for the crosswalk.
  - E4 Activity and E6 Positive Emotions share a distinctive age pattern: genetic variance much higher in young than old. This pattern is characteristic of C facets in this sample (see below). E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, and E3 Assertiveness do NOT show this pattern.
  - E3 Assertiveness has the largest broad h² in the E domain (.419) and a very large non-additive component, diverging from other E facets.
  - **Aspect-level implication:** In DeYoung (2007), E4 Activity is assigned to Assertiveness (with E3 Assertiveness and E5 Excitement-Seeking). But in this Sardinian sample, E4's genetic clustering is with C facets, not E facets. Its age-heterogeneity pattern (genetic variance concentrated in young) matches C facets, not its aspect-mates E3 and E5. E3's heritability profile (large non-additive component, no age heterogeneity) diverges from E4. This is a **strong dissociation flag** for E4's assignment to the Assertiveness aspect.
  - E6 Positive Emotions is assigned to Enthusiasm in DeYoung (2007). Its age-heterogeneity pattern (genetic variance concentrated in young) matches E4/C facets rather than its aspect-mate E1 Warmth (no age heterogeneity). This is a **moderate dissociation flag.**

Within-domain heterogeneity notes (Openness):**

  - O facets are the most internally consistent domain in this paper. No O facet shows significant age heterogeneity in genetic variance (some show constant total variance changes). No strong sex heterogeneity patterns.
  - O1 Fantasy and O4 Actions show mild environmental heterogeneity by sex, but this doesn't affect h² estimates meaningfully.
  - **Aspect-level implication:** The uniformity of O facets' heritability profiles provides no dissociation flags. This is consistent with the published aspect structure (Openness/Intellect) being relatively well-supported at the genetic level, though note that O5 Ideas (typically loading on Intellect) and O2 Aesthetics (typically loading on Openness) have similar heritability profiles here.

**Within-domain heterogeneity notes (Agreeableness):**

  - A2 Straightforwardness is an outlier: very large non-additive component (narrow .134, broad .413) and a dramatic sex difference in h² (males .067, females .183). Its genetic profile diverges from other A facets.
  - A3 Altruism and A4 Compliance share an age pattern (h² much higher in young, genetic variance declining with age) that A1 Trust, A5 Modesty, and A6 Tender-Mindedness do not show.
  - A1 Trust and A2 Straightforwardness show strong sex heterogeneity (much higher h² in females); A3, A4, A5, A6 do not.
  - **Aspect-level implication:** In DeYoung (2007), Compassion includes A3 Altruism, A6 Tender-Mindedness, and (primarily) A1 Trust; Politeness includes A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance, and A5 Modesty. In this sample, A3 and A4 share an age pattern (declining genetic variance) but are assigned to *different* aspects. A1 and A2 share a sex pattern (higher h² in females) but are also assigned to *different* aspects. The heritability profiles don't cleanly separate along aspect lines. This is a **moderate dissociation flag** — but it cuts in multiple directions and doesn't clearly favor reassignment.

**Within-domain heterogeneity notes (Conscientiousness):**

  - C facets show the most internally consistent age pattern of any domain: genetic variance is dramatically higher in younger individuals for C1–C5. The domain-level h² drops from .316 to .107, nearly a 3:1 ratio.
  - **C6 Deliberation is the exception:** its h² does not decline with age. Instead, it shows constant heritability with total variance declining (ratio 0.75). C6 also has the largest narrow/broad gap of any C facet, suggesting a very large non-additive or shared-environment component. This makes C6 the heritability outlier in its domain.
  - No C facet shows significant sex heterogeneity. This is unusual — most other domains have at least some facets with sex differences.
  - **Aspect-level implication:** In DeYoung (2007), Industriousness includes C1 Competence, C4 Achievement Striving, and C5 Self-Discipline; Orderliness includes C2 Order, C3 Dutifulness, and C6 Deliberation. In this sample, C1–C5 all share the same distinctive age pattern (genetic variance concentrated in young), while C6 diverges. Since C6 is an Orderliness facet, this creates a within-Orderliness dissociation: C2 and C3 show the typical C pattern, but C6 does not. This is a **moderate dissociation flag** for C6's placement — its genetic architecture diverges from its aspect-mates.

**Cross-domain and structural findings**- **E4 Activity clusters genetically with C facets.** This is the single most important structural finding in this paper for the crosswalk. From the bivariate genetic correlation clustering (Figure 4, PDF p. 12) and the genetic factor analysis (Table S3, referenced on PDF p. 9): E4 Activity's genetic correlations place it closer to C facets than to other E facets. The text states this explicitly and notes that the genetic factor analysis confirms it. [from PDF p. 9]**- The genetic factor structure of personality largely replicates the phenotypic five-factor structure** — with the E4 → C exception noted above. The text states: "the clustering of facets for the NEO O, NEO N, NEO C, and NEO A factors reinforces the structure of the five-factor personality model." [from PDF p. 9] The E domain is the one that doesn't hold up cleanly.**- Domain-level heritabilities are higher than facet-level averages.** The five domains average .254 in narrow h²; the 30 facets average .177. The paper attributes this to domains being more reliable composite scores, but it could also reflect genetic variance at the between-facet level (i.e., the factor that unites facets within a domain is more heritable than the facet-specific variance). [from PDF p. 4]**- No shared genetic basis for personality and cardiovascular traits.** Genetic correlations between N2 Angry Hostility or A4 Compliance and cardiovascular measures (IMT, PWV, SBP, DBP, heart rate) were not significantly different from zero. [from PDF p. 9]**- Age heterogeneity in heritability is the norm, not the exception.** 22 of 35 personality measures showed significant age heterogeneity. The dominant pattern is higher heritability in younger individuals (12 personality traits showed higher genetic variance in young), consistent with accumulating environmental influences diluting genetic signal over the lifespan. Important exceptions: N2, N3, N4 showed *higher* h² in the older group (environmental variance shrinks with age for these facets). Blood pressure traits also showed dramatically higher h² in older individuals. [from PDF pp. 9, 13]**- Sex heterogeneity: females show higher heritability for N, A, and E domains.** The text states A, N, and E "showed approximately 10% higher heritability in females" with "four facets" also showing this pattern. From Table 3, the specific personality traits with significant sex heterogeneity are: N domain, A domain, N1, N3, N4, N6, E4, E5, E6, O1, O4, A1, A2, A4, A5. The pattern is predominantly higher genetic variance in females (when the heterogeneity is in genetic variance). [from PDF pp. 6, 8]**- Genetic correlation of variance across age groups and sexes does not deviate from 1.0.** This means the *same genes* appear to operate in young and old, and in males and females — but with different effect sizes. No evidence for qualitative genetic differences by age or sex. [from PDF p. 13]**Dissociation Flags**

E4 Activity → Assertiveness aspect

  - **Currently assigned to:** Assertiveness (Extraversion), per DeYoung (2007)
  - **Evidence:** E4's genetic correlations cluster with C facets, not with other E facets. Its age-heterogeneity profile (genetic variance concentrated in young, nearly halving from young to old) matches C1–C5 exactly, and diverges sharply from its putative aspect-mates E3 Assertiveness (no age heterogeneity) and E5 Excitement-Seeking (slightly higher h² in old). The genetic factor analysis reportedly confirms this cross-domain placement.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Strong.** Multiple converging indicators (genetic clustering, age-heterogeneity pattern, genetic factor analysis) all point the same direction. This is the most robust dissociation in the paper.

E6 Positive Emotions → Enthusiasm aspect

  - **Currently assigned to:** Enthusiasm (Extraversion), per DeYoung (2007)
  - **Evidence:** E6's age-heterogeneity profile (genetic variance concentrated in young: .224 → .115) matches E4 and C facets, not its aspect-mate E1 Warmth (no age heterogeneity). However, E6 does not cluster genetically with C facets — the paper only mentions E4 as the cross-domain outlier.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Suggestive.** The age pattern is consistent but the genetic clustering doesn't corroborate it. E6 might simply have a developmental trajectory that happens to parallel C facets without sharing their genetic basis.

C6 Deliberation → Orderliness aspect

  - **Currently assigned to:** Orderliness (Conscientiousness), per DeYoung (2007)
  - **Evidence:** C6 is the only C facet whose h² does NOT decline with age. All other C facets (C1–C5) show dramatic age-related decline in genetic variance. C6 also has the largest non-additive genetic component in the C domain (narrow .093 vs. broad .364). Its genetic architecture diverges from its Orderliness aspect-mates C2 Order (.277 → .093 age decline) and C3 Dutifulness (.175 → .065 age decline).
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Moderate.** C6's divergent age pattern is clear, but a single indicator (age heterogeneity) is not as compelling as E4's multiple converging indicators. C6's content ("thinking carefully before acting") overlaps with impulse control, which could pull it toward N5 Impulsiveness (reversed) rather than away from C.

N5 Impulsiveness → Volatility aspect

  - **Currently assigned to:** Volatility (Neuroticism), per DeYoung (2007)
  - **Evidence:** N5 shows the opposite age pattern from its Volatility aspect-mates N2 and N4. N5 has much higher h² in young (.195 → .106, genetic variance declining); N2 and N4 show higher h² in old. N5 also has a much larger non-additive component (narrow .105 vs. broad .332) than N2 (.186/.304) or N4 (.180/.240). N5 does not show the sex heterogeneity that N4 shows.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Moderate.** N5 is already well-known as a problematic facet (cross-loading on C in many studies). This paper's heritability data adds another dimension to the case.

**A2 Straightforwardness — genetic outlier within Agreeableness**- **Currently assigned to:** Politeness (Agreeableness), per DeYoung (2007)
  - **Evidence:** A2 has a dramatically different heritability profile from other A facets: very large non-additive component (narrow .134 vs. broad .413 — the largest gap in the A domain), and a near-3× sex difference in h² (males .067, females .183). No other Politeness facet (A4, A5) shows this pattern.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Suggestive.** A2's heritability profile is distinctive, but this alone doesn't indicate misassignment — it could reflect A2 tapping a genetically distinctive process (perhaps related to social desirability or deception detection) that is still properly within the Agreeableness space.

**Notes**

  - **This paper shares authors (Terracciano, Costa) with the BLSA longitudinal trajectory paper (Terracciano et al., 2005) and uses the same SardiNIA cohort that generated several subsequent GWAS papers.** The personality data come from the same Italian NEO-PI-R administration described in Terracciano (2005, PAID). Cross-referencing with the BLSA trajectory data would allow comparing heritability patterns (this paper) with developmental trajectory patterns (Terracciano et al.) at the facet level.**- The supplementary Table S3 (genetic factor structure) would be extremely valuable for the crosswalk but is not in the PDF.** It reportedly presents Procrustes-rotated principal components from the genetic correlation matrix of all 30 facets, targeted to the American normative five-factor structure. The paper cites it as confirming E4's genetic placement with C. If this table can be retrieved (the supplementary DOI links are provided), it would give us genetic factor loadings at the facet level — the most direct evidence for or against the published aspect structure.**- The full genetic correlation matrix (8,556 pairwise correlations among 93 traits) was reportedly available online** at http://www.sph.umich.edu/csg/chen/public/sardinia. If still accessible, this would allow direct extraction of every facet × facet genetic correlation, which would be transformative for the crosswalk. Worth checking.**- The age heterogeneity finding has a specific methodological implication for comparing this paper's h² values with Jang et al. (1996) twin estimates.** Jang's sample had a mean age of ~31. Pilia's "young" group (< 42) would overlap substantially with Jang's sample. The "old" group (≥ 42) would not. For C facets especially, comparing Pilia's "young" h² values with Jang's values is more appropriate than comparing the pooled values.**

  - The finding that the same genes operate across age groups (genetic correlation across age ≈ 1.0) but with different effect sizes is important for interpretation. It means the age heterogeneity in h² isn't because different genes "turn on" at different ages — it's because the same genetic influences get diluted by accumulating environmental variance. This is consistent with the Briley & Tucker-Drob (2014) meta-analytic finding of increasing environmental stability and decreasing heritability across the lifespan.

  - Comparison with Jang et al. (1996):** Jang reported facet-level broad-sense h² estimates averaging around .40–.50 for most facets. Pilia's narrow h² estimates are substantially lower (averaging .177 for facets), but the broad estimates (.188 → .299 when dominance is included) are closer. The gap is expected given pedigree vs. twin designs. For the crosswalk, the *relative* ordering of facets within domains and the *patterns* of heterogeneity matter more than the absolute h² values.**Røysamb, Nes, Czajkowski & Vassend (2018)**

**Citation**

Røysamb, E., Nes, R. B., Czajkowski, N. O., & Vassend, O. (2018). Genetics, personality and wellbeing. A twin study of traits, facets and life satisfaction. *Scientific Reports, 8*, 12298. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-018-29881-x

**Design**- **Type:** Classical twin study (MZ vs. DZ, same-sex pairs only)
  - **N:** 1,516 twins (290 MZ male, 247 DZ male, 456 MZ female, 523 DZ female). Of these, 1,272 were pair-responders, 244 were single-responders.
  - **Age range:** 50–65 years (M = 57.11, SD = 4.5)
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (240 items, all 30 facets); Satisfaction With Life Scale (SWLS)
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (single administration, 2010)
  - **Key limitations:**- **Only 4 of 30 facets carried into biometric analyses.** The authors used regression to identify the four strongest facet predictors of life satisfaction (N1, N3, E4, E6), then ran Cholesky models on those four only. Heritability estimates and genetic correlations for the other 26 facets are not reported. [from PDF p. 5, 10]
    - **Same-sex twins only.** The Norwegian Twin Registry cohort used here consists only of same-sex pairs. No opposite-sex DZ pairs means limited ability to detect qualitative sex differences in genetic architecture. [from PDF p. 10]
    - **Narrow age range (50–65).** Heritability estimates are specific to middle-to-late adulthood. Per Briley & Tucker-Drob (2014), heritability tends to be somewhat lower at older ages, which is consistent with the estimates here being in the lower range.
    - **Population:** Norwegian only. Cultural generalizability uncertain.
    - **Facet reliabilities were partly limited.** Alphas ranged from 0.47 (C5 Self-Discipline) to 0.85 (N1 Anxiety), mean = 0.67. Low reliability inflates E estimates and attenuates genetic correlations. The authors note this: "Measurement error is captured in the E-factor in the biometric analyses, and might contribute to reduced environmental, but not genetic, correlations." [from PDF p. 9]
    - **No non-additive genetic effects tested.** Best-fitting models were AE (additive genetic + nonshared environment). C was dropped, D was not tested. MZ correlations were not more than twice DZ correlations for any variable, so D was not indicated. [from PDF p. 5, 10]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level** — but selectively. All 30 NEO-PI-R facets are reported for correlations with life satisfaction and regression betas (Table 2). However, biometric (twin) analyses were conducted only on the four facets with β > 0.10: N1 Anxiety, N3 Depression, E4 Activity, E6 Positive Emotions. Heritability estimates and genetic/environmental correlations are available for these four facets only.

**Missed opportunity:** They had the full NEO-PI-R on 1,516 twins. They could have reported univariate heritability for all 30 facets (as Jang 1996 did). They chose not to because the paper's focus was personality–wellbeing associations, not personality structure per se. This is unfortunate for the crosswalk — a sample of this size at this age range would have been valuable for all 30 facets.

Cross-domain and structural findings**

**Within-domain genetic correlations (from Figure 4, PDF p. 8):**

  - N1 Anxiety ↔ N3 Depression: rG = 0.90. Near-unity. These two facets are genetically almost the same thing.
  - E4 Activity ↔ E6 Positive Emotions: rG = 0.61. Moderate-high. More genetic distinctiveness within E than within N.

**Cross-domain genetic correlations:**

  - N facets ↔ E facets: rG range −0.40 to −0.53. Substantial genetic overlap between N and E at facet level.
  - N1 Anxiety ↔ E4 Activity: rG = −0.44
  - N1 Anxiety ↔ E6 Positive Emotions: rG = −0.40
  - N3 Depression ↔ E4 Activity: rG = −0.49
  - N3 Depression ↔ E6 Positive Emotions: rG = −0.53

**Cholesky decomposition key finding:** Within each domain, the first facet's genetic factor captured all the genetic prediction of life satisfaction. The second facet's unique genetic variance added nothing:

  - A1 (Anxiety genetic factor) → LS: significant. A2 (Depression-unique genetic factor) → LS: n.s. [from Figure 2]
  - A3 (Activity genetic factor) → LS: significant. A4 (Positive Emotions-unique genetic factor) → LS: n.s. (path = .00) [from Figure 2]

This means: **within N, one genetic factor underlies both Anxiety and Depression's link to LS.** Within E, one genetic factor underlies both Activity and Positive Emotions' link to LS. The facet-specific genetic variance doesn't contribute to LS prediction.

**But there IS a unique genetic factor for LS beyond personality (A5 in the Cholesky):**

  - Path loading = 0.34 [from Figure 2]
  - This accounts for 11% of total LS variance
  - So: of total h²(LS) = 0.31, about 65% is personality-related genetics, 35% is personality-independent genetics [from PDF p. 5]

**Variance decomposition for life satisfaction (from Figure 3, PDF p. 8):**

  - Genetic, personality-related: 20%
  - Genetic, non-personality: 11%
  - Environmental, personality-related: 11%
  - Environmental, non-personality: 49%
  - Random error (1−α): 9%

**Dissociation Flags**

**N1 Anxiety and N3 Depression: Genetic near-identity (rG = 0.90)**- **Currently assigned to:** Both assigned to Withdrawal aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence says:** Their genetic near-identity is CONSISTENT with same-aspect placement. This is a confirmation, not a dissociation.
  - **However:** Their environmental correlations are much lower (rE = 0.53 from Figure 4). The environmental influences that distinguish Anxiety from Depression are substantial, even though the genetic influences barely differentiate them. This means the phenotypic differentiation of these two facets is driven primarily by environmental factors.
  - **Strength:** Moderate (confirms published structure genetically; adds nuance about environmental distinctiveness)

**E4 Activity and E6 Positive Emotions: More genetically distinct (rG = 0.61)**- **Currently assigned to:** E4 Activity → Assertiveness aspect; E6 Positive Emotions → Enthusiasm aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence says:** Their genetic correlation of 0.61 is moderate-high — higher than you'd expect for facets assigned to DIFFERENT aspects, lower than the near-unity you see for N1–N3 (same aspect). This is ambiguous for the crosswalk. It doesn't clearly support or contradict the aspect assignment.
  - **Complication:** The environmental correlation between E4 and E6 is only 0.36, which is more consistent with different-aspect placement. But the genetic correlation is on the high side for cross-aspect facets.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive (genetic overlap between E4 and E6 is higher than their aspect assignment would predict, but not definitively so)

**E5 Excitement Seeking: Phenotypically disconnected from other E facets**- **Currently assigned to:** Assertiveness aspect
  - **Evidence says:** r = 0.05 (n.s.) with life satisfaction, while all other E facets range from 0.13 to 0.30. E5 behaves nothing like its domain-mates in predicting wellbeing.
  - **Strength:** Moderate (adds to accumulating evidence from developmental and heritability streams that E5 is a misfit in E)

**C1 Competence: Phenotypically behaves like N/E, not like C**- **Currently assigned to:** Industriousness aspect
  - **Evidence says:** C1 Competence (r = 0.30 with LS) exceeds the C domain correlation (r = 0.28) and matches E6 Positive Emotions (r = 0.30). C1 also loses its unique predictive power in the 30-facet regression, meaning its association with LS is fully captured by N and E facets.
  - **Interpretation:** C1 Competence's wellbeing association may be driven by its overlap with Neuroticism (reversed) and Extraversion rather than by Conscientiousness-specific content. This is consistent with the known high cross-loading of C1 on N (reversed) in factor analyses.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive (no biometric data on C1 here; inference is phenotypic only)

**N5 Impulsiveness: Weak and reversed association with LS**- **Currently assigned to:** Volatility aspect
  - **Evidence says:** N5 shows the weakest N-facet correlation (r = −0.14), and in the 30-facet regression, the beta REVERSES to +0.06*, meaning after controlling for other facets, higher Impulsiveness predicts slightly higher LS. This suppressor pattern is consistent with N5's known partial independence from the emotional core of Neuroticism.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive (adds to developmental and heritability evidence that N5 is structurally misplaced)

**Notes**- **What this paper provides for the crosswalk:** Facet-level heritability estimates for N1, N3, E4, E6 in a 50–65 age sample. These complement Jang (1996; M age ~31) by providing estimates at a later developmental stage. The estimates are somewhat lower than Jang's for comparable facets, consistent with Briley & Tucker-Drob's (2014) finding of modestly declining heritability with age.**

 Comparison to Jang (1996):

    - N1 Anxiety: Røysamb h² = 0.47 vs. Jang h² = 0.45 (very consistent)
    - N3 Depression: Røysamb h² = 0.46 vs. Jang h² = 0.35 (Røysamb higher)
    - E4 Activity: Røysamb h² = 0.42 vs. Jang h² = 0.25 (Røysamb substantially higher; Jang's E4 was the lowest-heritability E facet)
    - E6 Positive Emotions: Røysamb h² = 0.40 vs. Jang h² = 0.46 (comparable)

  - The N3 and E4 discrepancies are notable. Jang's E4 estimate was anomalously low; Røysamb's larger sample suggests the true heritability is moderate (~0.40), not low (~0.25). For N3 Depression, the direction is reversed — Røysamb's estimate is higher — which may reflect the larger sample or the older age range.

  - The key structural finding for the crosswalk** is the within-domain genetic correlation structure. N1–N3 at rG = 0.90 means the Withdrawal aspect has a coherent genetic core. E4–E6 at rG = 0.61 means the E-domain facets are more genetically differentiated — consistent with the fact that E4 and E6 are assigned to DIFFERENT aspects.**

  - The paper does NOT report:

    - Heritability for the other 26 NEO facets
    - Genetic correlations among all 30 facets
    - Any aspect-level analyses
    - Any factor loadings on aspects or domains
    - Developmental trajectories (single timepoint)

  - **The sample is the same Norwegian Twin Registry** used in several of the co-authors' other wellbeing papers (Nes, Røysamb et al. series). Results should not be treated as fully independent replications if other NTR papers appear in the crosswalk.**- E4 Activity's DZ correlation (0.26) is notably high relative to its MZ correlation (0.39).** The MZ:DZ ratio is ~1.5:1, not the ~2:1 expected under pure additive genetics. This could indicate shared environmental effects on Activity, assortative mating effects, or simply sampling variation. The authors did not test for C specifically on E4 — they tested C at the model level and found it unnecessary for overall fit. But E4 specifically shows a pattern where C might contribute. This is worth noting because Jang (1996) also found E4 Activity to be anomalous within E (lowest heritability, additive-only model in a domain where other facets showed nonadditive effects).**- Connection to Terracciano et al. (2005) and Brandt et al. (2023):** E4 Activity showed the steepest developmental decline of any E facet. This paper's sample (age 50–65) is drawn from exactly the age range where that decline is steepest. The moderate heritability (0.42) at this age is consistent with E4's developmental sensitivity to environmental factors (retirement, health changes, activity constraints).**Briley & Tucker-Drob (2012)**

**Citation**

Briley, D. A., & Tucker-Drob, E. M. (2012). Broad bandwidth or high fidelity? Evidence from the structure of genetic and environmental effects on the facets of the Five Factor Model. *Behavior Genetics, 42*(5), 743–763. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10519-012-9548-8

**Design**- **Type:** Twin study (multivariate behavioral genetic, cross-sectional)
  - **N:** 807 twin-pairs (490 MZ, 317 DZ); 326 male pairs, 481 female pairs
  - **Age range:** Approximately 17 years (high school juniors taking the NMSQT)
  - **Instrument(s):** CPI-Big Five — a scoring algorithm applied to the 480-item California Psychological Inventory, developed by Soto & John (2009) to extract FFM facets. NOT the NEO-PI-R.
  - **Assessment interval:** N/A (cross-sectional)
  - **Key limitations:**- **Non-standard instrument.** The CPI-Big Five has 16 facets (3–4 per domain), not the NEO-PI-R's 30. Facet names overlap conceptually with NEO facets and BFAS aspects but are not identical measures. Cross-instrument comparisons must be made with caution.
    - **Adolescent sample.** Participants are ~17, still living at home. Shared environment effects (C) are larger than in adult twin samples — C could not be dropped for Neuroticism or Openness. This is unusual relative to adult samples (e.g., Jang et al., 2002; Johnson & Krueger, 2004) and may affect the distribution of global vs. specific variance.
    - **High-achieving sample.** NMSQT takers are above-average in academic achievement. The Adventurousness facet loaded primarily on Agreeableness (negatively), not on Openness, in this sample — a phenotypic misfit that the authors attribute to range restriction or sample composition.
    - **Few indicators per domain (3–4).** This limits the complexity of models that can be tested and prevents use of Cholesky decomposition, which requires ≥4 indicators.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level**, using the CPI-Big Five scoring system. 16 facets across 5 domains:

        **Domain****** **CPI-Big Five Facets****** **Approximate NEO/BFAS Mapping******

        Gregariousness ≈ Enthusiasm aspect; Social Confidence ≈ (reversed) N-facets + Assertiveness aspect; Assertiveness ≈ Assertiveness aspect

        Agreeableness

        Trustfulness vs. Cynicism; Compassion vs. Insensitivity; Humility vs. Arrogance

        Trustfulness ≈ A1 Trust / Politeness aspect; Compassion ≈ Compassion aspect; Humility ≈ A5 Modesty / Politeness aspect

        Industriousness; Orderliness; Self-discipline vs. Distractibility

        Industriousness ≈ Industriousness aspect; Orderliness ≈ Orderliness aspect; Self-discipline ≈ C5 Self-Discipline (NEO)

        Intellectualism ≈ Intellect aspect; Idealism ≈ Openness aspect; Adventurousness ≈ O4 Actions (NEO) but phenotypically misloaded in this sample

Note: The mapping column is my inference, not stated in the paper. The CPI-Big Five facets were derived from circumplex analysis of CPI items, not from the NEO-PI-R structure. The alignment is conceptual, not psychometric.

**Caveat on Openness:** The authors explicitly warn that the common pathways model "fit" for Openness is partly an artifact. Adventurousness had its primary phenotypic loading on Agreeableness (not Openness), and most of its ACE variance was at the specific (not global) level. The common pathways test is only sensitive to misfit at the global level, so a facet that contributes almost nothing globally will not drive misfit even if it's poorly placed. The authors state: "the results regarding Openness in this study may be sample specific and should be interpreted with caution" [from PDF p. 19].

**Shared environment findings**

For Neuroticism and Openness, the shared environment (C) could not be dropped without significant misfit. This is atypical for adult twin samples and attributable to the adolescent age (~17) of participants still living at home. For E, A, and C, the AE model was adequate. No dominant genetic (D) effects improved fit for any domain. [From PDF pp. 13–14, Table 7]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Humility vs. Arrogance (Agreeableness) — genetically dissociated from domain**- **Currently assigned to:** Agreeableness
  - **Evidence:** Only 9% of genetic variance shared with other A facets. Essentially genetically independent. Univariate h² = .46, but this is almost entirely facet-specific.
  - **Strength:** Strong. This is a dramatic dissociation — the most extreme in the paper.
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** Maps conceptually to A5 Modesty (NEO) / Politeness aspect (BFAS). The finding suggests that whatever genes influence modesty/humility are largely distinct from genes influencing trust and compassion. This is consistent with a Compassion vs. Politeness aspect split in which the aspects have partially distinct genetic architectures — but goes further, suggesting Humility is genetically almost orthogonal to the rest of A.

**2. Gregariousness (Extraversion) — substantial facet-specific genetic variance**- **Currently assigned to:** Extraversion (Enthusiasm aspect in BFAS)
  - **Evidence:** Only 40% of genetic variance shared globally with other E facets. Compare Social Confidence (96%) and Assertiveness (71%).
  - **Strength:** Moderate. The common pathways model was still retained for E overall, so this is within-domain heterogeneity that didn't produce statistically significant structural misfit. But 60% facet-specific genetic variance is substantial.
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** Gregariousness maps to the Enthusiasm aspect of E. The other two facets (Social Confidence, Assertiveness) map more to the Assertiveness aspect. The pattern suggests Enthusiasm-related facets may have more distinct genetic influences than Assertiveness-related facets within E.

**3. Neuroticism facets — distributed genetic heterogeneity**- **Currently assigned to:** Neuroticism domain
  - **Evidence:** Common pathways model strongly rejected (χ²D = 35.52, p < .001). Rumination has 100% global genetic variance; Depression has 65%; Anxiety 80%; Irritability 78%. Rumination's univariate h² is very low (.13) with high C (.23), giving it a different etiological profile. The misfit is distributed — no single facet drives it.
  - **Strength:** Strong for the overall structural finding; specific facet attributions are tentative.
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** In BFAS terms, Anxiety and Depression map to Withdrawal; Irritability maps to Volatility. The distributed misfit could reflect Withdrawal vs. Volatility having distinct genetic architectures, but the paper can't test this directly with these facets because Rumination doesn't cleanly map to either aspect.

**4. Self-discipline vs. Distractibility (Conscientiousness) — facet-specific genetic variance**- **Currently assigned to:** Conscientiousness
  - **Evidence:** 57% global genetic variance (43% specific). Common pathways rejected for C domain overall, with misfit concentrated on Industriousness facet (from Figure 2, PDF p. 28).
  - **Strength:** Moderate.
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** Self-discipline maps conceptually to NEO C5 Self-Discipline. In BFAS, C5 is assigned to the Industriousness aspect. The partial genetic dissociation from the C domain may reflect that self-discipline shares genetic variance with non-C traits (e.g., Neuroticism, Agreeableness) rather than being C-specific.

**5. Adventurousness (Openness) — phenotypic and genetic misfit**- **Currently assigned to:** Openness
  - **Evidence:** Primary phenotypic loading on Agreeableness (negative), not Openness. Only 46% global genetic variance. Factor loading of .23 on latent Openness in the common pathways model.
  - **Strength:** Strong for measurement misfit, but this is likely sample-specific (high-achieving adolescents). The authors flag this explicitly.
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** Limited due to likely sample specificity. Adventurousness maps to NEO O4 Actions. If the finding replicated in a general-population adult sample with NEO-PI-R, it would be very important. As is, it's a yellow flag.

**Notes**

**What this paper contributes to the crosswalk**The primary contribution is **not** univariate heritabilities (which are available from Jang et al., 1996 with more facets). It is the **multivariate structure** — the demonstration that for Agreeableness, Conscientiousness, and Neuroticism, genetic effects on facets cannot be adequately represented as flowing through a single domain-level latent variable. This means the "domain" is not a genetically coherent unit for these three traits. Facets within those domains have partially independent genetic architectures.

This is directly relevant to the aspect-level crosswalk because it raises the question: would the genetic structure be better captured by two aspect-level latent variables per domain (as DeYoung, Quilty & Peterson, 2007 propose) rather than one domain-level variable? This paper can't answer that question because it only tested domain-level latent variables. But the finding of genetic incoherence at the domain level is consistent with — and potentially explained by — the existence of genetically distinct aspects within each domain.

**Key limitation for crosswalk use**The CPI-Big Five facets are **not** NEO-PI-R facets. The mapping is approximate. With only 3–4 facets per domain, we cannot determine which specific NEO facets are driving the dissociations. The Humility finding is striking, but whether it generalizes to NEO A5 Modesty (or to the Politeness aspect as a whole) requires replication with NEO-based measures.

**Comparison with Johnson & Krueger (2004)**Johnson & Krueger used similar methods (common vs. independent pathways) and found the common pathways model fit for Extraversion and Neuroticism but not A, C, or O. Briley & Tucker-Drob found it fit for E and O but not A, C, or N. The consistent finding across both: **Extraversion is genetically coherent as a domain; Agreeableness and Conscientiousness are not.** The inconsistent finding: Neuroticism and Openness flip between studies, likely due to different instruments, different age ranges, or both.

**Comparison with Jang et al. (2002)**

Jang et al. (2002) used a different approach (testing number of genetic factors needed per domain using NEO-PI-R facets) and found two genetic factors were needed per domain. This is broadly consistent with Briley & Tucker-Drob's finding that a single common factor is insufficient, and suggestive of the two-aspect structure — though neither paper tested aspect-level models explicitly.

**Nikolašević et al. (2021)**

**Citation**

Nikolašević, Ž., Dinić, B. M., Smederevac, S., Sadiković, S., Milovanović, I., Bugarski Ignjatović, V., Prinz, M., Budimlija, Z., & Zgonjanin Bosić, D. (2021). Common genetic basis of the five factor model facets and intelligence: A twin study. *Personality and Individual Differences, 175*, 110682. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.paid.2021.110682

**Note:** The PDF is an uncorrected proof. Minor formatting differences from the final published version are possible, but all data tables are legible and complete.

**Design**- **Type:** Twin study (classical twin design, Cholesky decomposition)
  - **N:** 424 twins total (134 MZ pairs = 268 individuals; 78 DZ pairs = 156 individuals, of which 31 pairs were opposite-sex)
  - **Age range:** 16–60; MZ M = 25.06 (SD = 7.36), DZ M = 23.04 (SD = 6.56)
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (Serbian adaptation; Knežević et al., 2004); Raven's Advanced Progressive Matrices Set II (APM; 40-min timed)
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (single assessment)
  - **Key limitations:**- **Small twin sample** — 134 MZ pairs is underpowered for precise facet-level biometric decomposition, especially for genetic correlations. CIs on many rG values are wide.
    - **Education-restricted sample** — described as "on average, upper-level education participants." This truncates variance in both intelligence and (to a lesser degree) personality, attenuating correlations.
    - **APM only** — measures fluid/reasoning intelligence, not crystallized. Genetic correlations are specific to g_f, not g broadly.
    - **No univariate heritability estimates in the main text** — these are in Supplementary Table C, which is not included in the PDF. The paper reports genetic *correlations* (rG) between each facet and APM, but not the heritability of each facet. This limits what we can extract for the crosswalk's heritability column.
    - **Serbian sample** — cultural and linguistic context differs from the North American and Australian samples in Jang et al. (1996) and Luciano et al. (2006). Cross-cultural replication is valuable, but instrument equivalence cannot be assumed.
    - **Multiple testing** — 108 correlation coefficients tested. Authors applied FDR correction; only a subset survive. I report both corrected and uncorrected significance below.
    - **Age and sex residualized** — all correlations computed on scores residualized for age and sex, which is standard for twin analyses but means raw age effects are removed.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 30 NEO-PI-R facets are reported with phenotypic (rPH), genetic (rG), and nonshared environmental (rE) correlations with APM performance. This is the most useful level for the crosswalk.

Domain-level correlations are also reported. The Big Two and GFP were tested in CFA and rejected (poor model fit), so no aspect- or metatrait-level data.

**What's missing:** Univariate heritability estimates for each facet are in Supplementary Table C (not in the PDF). The paper references this table but does not reproduce it in the main text.

**Core Findings**

**What this paper contributes to the crosswalk:** This is NOT a heritability study or a developmental trajectory study in the usual sense. It reports **genetic correlations between each NEO-PI-R facet and fluid intelligence (APM)**. The crosswalk value is: if facets assigned to the same aspect show divergent genetic correlations with g, that's evidence of within-aspect heterogeneity on a genetic dimension. It's a different evidence stream from heritability per se (which asks "how heritable is this facet?") — this asks "does this facet share genetic variance with intelligence?"

All statistics below are from Table 2 [PDF pp. 3–4] unless otherwise noted. Significance: * p < .05, ** p < .01, *** p < .001. **Bold** = survived FDR correction (corrected critical p = .0055 for 108 tests).

This is a weak signal (N5 is nonsignificant) but directionally notable. The Withdrawal facets (N1, N3, N4) are more homogeneously negative in their genetic correlations, though only N4 reaches significance uncorrected.

**Within-domain pattern:** The domain-level null masks opposite-direction facet patterns. E6 Positive Emotions shows a robust positive genetic correlation with intelligence (rG = 0.25, survives FDR). E5 Excitement Seeking also trends positive genetically (rG = 0.17). E1 Warmth shows a negative environmental correlation.

**Aspect-level heterogeneity:** E6 Positive Emotions (Enthusiasm aspect) has the strongest positive genetic correlation, while E1 Warmth and E2 Gregariousness (also Enthusiasm) show nothing. Within Assertiveness, E5 Excitement Seeking trends positive genetically while E4 Activity is null. The aspects are not cleanly differentiated by genetic correlation with g — it's individual facets, not aspects, that carry the signal.

**Within-domain pattern:** O5 Ideas dominates. Its genetic correlation (rG = 0.38) is nearly double the domain-level value. O6 Values and O1 Fantasy also show significant positive genetic correlations.

**Aspect-level heterogeneity:** The Intellect aspect (O5 Ideas + O6 Values) is consistently positively genetically correlated with APM. The Openness aspect is heterogeneous: O1 Fantasy shows a significant positive genetic correlation (rG = 0.23), but O2 Aesthetics (rG = −0.05), O3 Feelings (rG = 0.03), and O4 Actions (rG = 0.01) do not. O1 Fantasy behaves more like the Intellect facets than like its own aspect-mates on this dimension. This is a notable dissociation signal.

**Within-domain pattern:** A5 Modesty and A6 Tender Mindedness drive the domain-level negative association, with substantial negative genetic correlations. A1 Trust is unusual: its genetic correlation is positive (rG = 0.13, nonsignificant) while its environmental correlation is the most negative of any A facet (rE = −0.21, survives FDR). The genes that influence Trust may push slightly toward higher intelligence, but the environments that make people trusting are associated with lower fluid intelligence. That's a genuinely interesting dissociation between genetic and environmental pathways.

**Aspect-level heterogeneity:** Compassion (A1, A3, A6) is messy — A6 Tender Mindedness has rG = −0.21, A1 Trust has rG = +0.13, A3 Altruism has rG = −0.03. Politeness (A2, A4, A5) is more consistently negative, driven by A5 Modesty (rG = −0.26).

**Within-domain pattern:** The domain-level null is a textbook case of within-domain cancellation. C1 Competence has a positive genetic correlation (rG = 0.16), C2 Order has a strongly negative one (rG = −0.28). These cancel to ~zero at the domain level.

**Aspect-level heterogeneity:** The Industriousness facets (C1, C3, C4, C5) range from rG = −0.02 to +0.16. C1 Competence is the only one with a notable positive signal, and the authors acknowledge its content overlaps with self-perceived intelligence. The Orderliness facets (C2, C6) are both negative genetically (rG = −0.28 and −0.10), which is more internally consistent.

C1 Competence continues to be a structurally interesting facet. Its positive genetic correlation with intelligence is consistent with the Luciano et al. (2006) finding that C1's genetic variance overlaps with IQ. The previous extraction flagged C1 as sitting at the C–O/Intellect boundary. This paper adds a new data point: in a different sample and culture, C1 still shares genetic variance with fluid intelligence while its Industriousness aspect-mates (C3, C5) do not.

**Regression: Facets predicting APM**

[from PDF p. 4, Results section]

Seven facets significantly predicted APM score in stepwise regression (R² = .35, reported as 39% in text — the discrepancy may be between R and R²; the multiple correlation in Table 2 bottom row is .35):

        **Facet****** **β****** **p****** **Direction******

        O5 Ideas

        0.28

        < .001

        Positive

        O4 Actions

        −0.15

        < .01

        Negative

        E6 Positive Emotions

        0.11

        < .05

        Positive

        N2 Hostility

        −0.11

        < .05

        C2 Order

        −0.13

        A5 Modesty

        −0.10

        < .05

        Negative

        A6 Tender Mindedness

        −0.14

        < .01

        Negative

The aggregate predicted score from these seven facets had rG = 0.52 and rE = 0.13 with APM. The genetic correlation massively exceeds the environmental one, indicating the personality-intelligence link is primarily genetic in origin at the facet level.

**Notable:** O4 Actions enters the regression as a *negative* predictor (β = −0.15) despite having a near-zero bivariate genetic correlation (rG = 0.01). This is a suppressor effect — Actions contributes incremental prediction by suppressing irrelevant variance in the other predictors, not through a direct association with intelligence.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**- **Hierarchical models above Big Five rejected.** CFA showed poor fit for both the Big Two (Stability/Plasticity) model and the GFP model applied to facets. [from PDF p. 3, Results] This is consistent with skepticism about higher-order factors (Revelle & Wilt, 2013), though CFA on 30 facets with N = 424 is underpowered for complex models.
  - **Facet-level associations are consistently larger than domain-level.** The authors state this explicitly: "the magnitude of covariance between the facets and performance on the APM was often twice as high as with the traits" [from PDF p. 5]. This is directly relevant to the crosswalk's rationale — domain-level analyses miss facet-level heterogeneity.
  - **Facets within the same domain show opposite-sign genetic correlations with g.** C1 Competence (rG = +0.16) vs. C2 Order (rG = −0.28). N5 Impulsiveness (rG = +0.09) vs. N2 Hostility (rG = −0.22). E6 Positive Emotions (rG = +0.25) vs. E1 Warmth (rG = +0.01). This is consistent across all five domains.
  - **Genetic correlations dominate over environmental correlations.** The seven-facet aggregate shows rG = 0.52 vs. rE = 0.13. The personality-intelligence link is primarily a genetic link, not an environmental one. Non-shared environmental correlations were "modest and sparse" [from PDF p. 5].

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. O1 Fantasy — genetically aligned with Intellect aspect, not Openness aspect**- **Currently assigned to:** Openness → Openness aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** O1 Fantasy has rG = 0.23 with APM (significant, survives FDR). Its Openness aspect-mates: O2 Aesthetics rG = −0.05, O3 Feelings rG = 0.03, O4 Actions rG = 0.01. The Intellect facets: O5 Ideas rG = 0.38, O6 Values rG = 0.18. Fantasy's genetic correlation pattern with intelligence is much more similar to the Intellect facets than to the other Openness facets.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The signal is statistically significant and survives FDR. However, genetic correlation with intelligence is only one dimension — factor loadings and other evidence streams may not agree. The authors themselves call this finding "novel and not in line with previous findings" [PDF p. 4], citing DeYoung et al. (2005), who found Fantasy associated with crystallized but not fluid intelligence. This paper uses a fluid intelligence measure (APM), making the discrepancy with DeYoung et al. (2005) harder to interpret.
  - **Connection to other extractions:** This is the first paper in our set to flag O1 Fantasy as potentially misaligned with its aspect on a genetic dimension. Worth tracking.

**2. C1 Competence — genetic overlap with intelligence unlike Industriousness aspect-mates (replicated)**- **Currently assigned to:** Conscientiousness → Industriousness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** C1 Competence rG = +0.16 with APM. Its Industriousness aspect-mates: C3 Dutifulness rG = −0.02, C4 Achievement Striving rG = +0.10, C5 Self-Discipline rG = +0.07. Only C1 reaches significance. The Luciano et al. (2006) extraction flagged the same pattern: C1 shares genetic variance with IQ, other Industriousness facets do not.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate** (strengthened by replication). The effect is modest (rG = 0.16, does not survive FDR in this sample alone) but the direction is consistent with Luciano et al. (2006) in a completely independent sample and culture.
  - **Note:** C1 Competence's content includes self-perceived capability and effectiveness, which partially overlaps with self-assessed intelligence. This may inflate the correlation.

**3. N5 Impulsiveness — opposite-direction genetic correlation from Volatility aspect-mate N2 Hostility**- **Currently assigned to:** Neuroticism → Volatility (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** N5 Impulsiveness rG = +0.09 (nonsignificant); N2 Hostility rG = −0.22 (significant, survives FDR). These are the two Volatility facets, and they point in opposite directions genetically with respect to intelligence.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** N5 is nonsignificant, so the positive direction could be noise. But it's consistent with the broader pattern from other extractions: N5 Impulsiveness repeatedly behaves differently from other N facets — its developmental trajectory aligns with C facets (decreasing with age, converging with C6 Deliberation), its heritability pattern in Jang et al. (1996) was anomalous, and now its genetic correlation with g diverges from its aspect-mate. The convergence across evidence streams is more persuasive than this paper alone.

**4. E6 Positive Emotions — genetic overlap with intelligence unlike Enthusiasm aspect-mates**- **Currently assigned to:** Extraversion → Enthusiasm (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** E6 Positive Emotions rG = +0.25 (survives FDR). Its Enthusiasm aspect-mates: E1 Warmth rG = +0.01, E2 Gregariousness rG = −0.00. The signal is entirely carried by E6, not by the Enthusiasm aspect as a whole.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** Statistically robust for E6, but the question is whether genetic correlation with intelligence should be expected to cohere at the aspect level. If aspects are defined by factor structure rather than external criterion validity, divergent genetic correlations with an external variable (intelligence) don't necessarily indicate misassignment. They just show the aspects aren't homogeneous on this dimension.
  - **Note:** E6 Positive Emotions loads on Enthusiasm in DeYoung et al. (2007), but its content (cheerful, joyful, optimistic) overlaps with temperamental approach motivation, which the authors link to a "stimulation hypothesis" — higher-g individuals seek more cognitive stimulation, and Positive Emotions captures approach motivation generally. Whether this makes E6 structurally misplaced or just externally heterogeneous with its aspect-mates is an interpretive question, not something this paper resolves.

**5. A1 Trust — opposite-sign genetic vs. environmental correlations with intelligence**- **Currently assigned to:** Agreeableness → Compassion (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** A1 Trust: rG = +0.13 (nonsignificant), rE = −0.21 (significant, survives FDR). The genetic and environmental pathways point in opposite directions. Its Compassion aspect-mates show different patterns: A3 Altruism is null on both; A6 Tender Mindedness has rG = −0.21 (significantly negative).
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The opposite-sign pattern is genuinely interesting and may reflect different causal pathways (genes that promote trust also promote something intelligence-related, while environments that promote trust reduce intellectual engagement). But A1 Trust's genetic correlation is nonsignificant, so the positive direction is uncertain.

**Notes**- **Primary crosswalk value:** This paper provides a new evidence stream — genetic correlations with intelligence — that can flag within-aspect heterogeneity. It's not a substitute for heritability estimates or developmental trajectories, but it adds a dimension. The strongest signals for the crosswalk are: O1 Fantasy aligning genetically with Intellect rather than Openness; C1 Competence replicating its genetic overlap with IQ; within-domain cancellation demonstrating that facet-level analysis is essential.
  - **Missing data:** Univariate heritability estimates for each facet (Supplementary Table C) would have been directly useful for the crosswalk heritability column. They exist but are not in the main text PDF.
  - **Relationship to Luciano et al. (2006):** This paper partially replicates and extends Luciano et al. The C1 Competence–IQ genetic link replicates. The finding that C2 Order has the largest negative genetic correlation with intelligence (rG = −0.28) is consistent with Luciano's finding of genetic mediation for C facets, though the specific facets differ.
  - **Regression note:** The 39% variance explained by seven facets is notably high. The authors used cross-validation (5-fold), which mitigates overfitting concern somewhat, but with 30 predictors and N ≈ 424, stepwise regression can capitalize on chance. The regression results should be weighted less heavily than the bivariate genetic correlations.
  - **What this paper does NOT tell us:** Factor loadings, aspect-level structure, developmental trajectories, or how facets relate to each other structurally. It tells us about shared genetic variance with an external criterion (intelligence), which is a different question from internal personality structure.

**Jang, Hu, Livesley, Angleitner, Riemann, Ando, Ono, Vernon & Hamer (2001)**

**Citation**

Jang, K. L., Hu, S., Livesley, W. J., Angleitner, A., Riemann, R., Ando, J., Ono, Y., Vernon, P. A., & Hamer, D. H. (2001). Covariance structure of neuroticism and agreeableness: A twin and molecular genetic analysis of the role of the serotonin transporter gene. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 81*(2), 295–304. DOI: 10.1037//0022-3514.81.2.295

**Design**- **Type:** Twin study (multivariate behavioral genetics) + molecular genetic association study (sibling pairs)
  - **N:** 913 MZ twin pairs + 562 DZ twin pairs (across three samples) + 388 nontwin sibling pairs (U.S.)

    - Canadian twins: 253 MZ, 207 DZ
    - German twins: 526 MZ, 269 DZ
    - Japanese twins: 134 MZ, 86 DZ
    - U.S. siblings: 388 same-sex nontwin sibling pairs (236 male, 152 female)

  - **Age range:**

    - Canadian: ~15–86 (mean ~31–36 depending on zygosity/sex group)
    - German: ~14–80 (mean ~29–33)
    - Japanese: ~15–27 (mean ~19–20; notably younger sample)
    - U.S. siblings: mean 29.13 (SD = 11.13)

  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (self-report); English version for Canadian and U.S. samples, German translation (Ostendorf & Angleitner, 1994) for German sample, Japanese translation (Yoshimura et al., 1998) for Japanese sample
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (single timepoint)
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Analyses restricted to N and A domains only (6 + 6 = 12 facets). No data on E, C, O facets, so we cannot evaluate cross-domain loadings onto those domains.
    - Japanese sample is small (134 MZ, 86 DZ pairs) and young (mostly 15–27), producing some anomalous factor loadings (Depression loading on A factor, Altruism loading on N factor). Authors acknowledge this. [from PDF page 6]
    - Twin model specifies only A + E (additive genetic + nonshared environment); no shared environment or dominance components estimated. This is standard for personality but worth noting.
    - Molecular genetic component examines a single gene (5-HTTLPR) — small effect sizes expected and observed.
    - U.S. sibling sample is 79.6% Caucasian; population stratification was tested and rejected for the main findings. [from PDF page 3]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 6 Neuroticism facets (N1 Anxiety, N2 Angry Hostility, N3 Depression, N4 Self-Consciousness, N5 Impulsiveness, N6 Vulnerability) and all 6 Agreeableness facets (A1 Trust, A2 Straightforwardness, A3 Altruism, A4 Compliance, A5 Modesty, A6 Tender-Mindedness) are reported individually.

**Critical limitation for our purposes:** Only N and A facets are analyzed. No data on the remaining 18 facets from E, C, and O. This means we can evaluate N↔A cross-domain genetic architecture at facet level, but cannot speak to aspect assignments that depend on E, C, or O loadings.

Cross-domain and structural findings**

**1. The N-A boundary is genetically porous, concentrated at specific facets.** The genetic correlation data (Table 5) show that the N↔A genetic overlap is not uniform across facets. It concentrates on:

  - **A1 Trust** correlating genetically with nearly all N facets (consistently rG = −.27 to −.75 across samples)
  - **A3 Altruism** correlating genetically with N6 Vulnerability (rG = −.57 to −.65) and N2 Angry Hostility (rG = −.42 to −.53)
  - **N2 Angry Hostility** correlating genetically with nearly all A facets [from PDF page 7, Table 5]

**2. 5-HTTLPR does not associate with N4 Self-Consciousness or A5 Modesty or A6 Tender-Mindedness.** These three facets show no significant molecular genetic link to the serotonin transporter, while the other N and A facets mostly do. This suggests these facets may be under different genetic control than their domain-mates. [from PDF page 7, Table 6]

**3. Authors explicitly suggest N5 Impulsiveness belongs under Extraversion.** From the Discussion (p. 302): "It is also interesting that the serotonin transporter gene was not associated with NEO-PI-R Impulsivity, which suggests that this facet should be located elsewhere, such as under Extraversion." — However, I note that Table 6 actually *does* show a significant association (r = .117, p = .0011, h² = .0136). The authors appear to be contradicting their own data here, or they may be making a relative argument (smaller effect than expected). This inconsistency should be flagged. [from PDF page 8, Discussion, and PDF page 7, Table 6]

**4. Authors suggest the data support Tellegen's Negative-Emotionality definition over NEO-PI-R Neuroticism.** The rationale: Tellegen's construct includes alienation (distrust of others' intentions), which overlaps with NEO-PI-R Trust (reverse-keyed). The genetic data show Trust is genetically entangled with N facets, suggesting it "belongs" in the broader negative emotionality space. [from PDF page 8, Discussion]

**5. Phenotypic correlations mirror genetic correlations but are weaker.** The phenotypic N-A correlations (Table 3) show the same pattern — Angry Hostility correlates most strongly with A facets (r = −.49 to −.58 with Trust across samples; r = −.32 to −.52 with Compliance) — but the genetic correlations are uniformly larger in magnitude. This is consistent with genetic influences driving the phenotypic structure. [from PDF pages 5 and 7, Tables 3 and 5]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. N2 Angry Hostility — straddling the N/A boundary**- **Currently assigned to:** Volatility aspect of Neuroticism (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Genetic correlations with Trust (rG = −.75, −.47, −.51 across three samples), Altruism (rG = −.42, −.43, −.53), and Compliance (rG = −.51, −.50, −.21). Cross-loads phenotypically on A factor at −.45 to −.53 (Table 2). 5-HTTLPR links it to A facets broadly (Table 7).
  - **Interpretation:** Angry Hostility shares as much or more genetic architecture with Agreeableness (especially Trust, Compliance, Altruism) as it does with its Neuroticism domain-mates. This doesn't necessarily mean it's "misplaced" — it could be a bridging facet — but it means the Volatility aspect (which pairs N2 with N5) is binding together two facets with very different cross-domain genetic profiles.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Strong (replicated across 3 independent samples in genetic correlations; confirmed by molecular data)

**2. N5 Impulsiveness — weak domain membership, possible E candidate**- **Currently assigned to:** Volatility aspect of Neuroticism (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Weakest N factor loading of any N facet (.35–.48 across samples; Table 2). Authors suggest relocation to Extraversion [from PDF page 8], citing Depue & Collins (1999) and dopamine D4 gene associations. However, the 5-HTTLPR data in this paper actually *does* show a significant association for N5 (r = .117, p = .0011), contradicting the authors' own interpretive claim.
  - **Interpretation:** The factor-loading evidence is consistent with N5 being a poor fit for Neuroticism. The molecular evidence is mixed — it has a significant 5-HTTLPR link (suggesting some serotonergic involvement) but the authors' theoretical argument points toward dopaminergic/extraversion placement. The MZ/DZ pattern also shows relatively high DZ correlations (Canadian: .27/.37; German: .29/.41), which could suggest shared environmental or dominance effects differing from other N facets.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Moderate (factor loadings consistent; molecular argument internally inconsistent; no E-domain data in this paper to test the alternative placement)

**3. A1 Trust — genetically entangled with Neuroticism**- **Currently assigned to:** Compassion aspect of Agreeableness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Cross-loads on N factor at −.40 to −.49 (Table 2). Genetic correlations with N facets are large and consistent: rG with N domain = −.61 (Can), −.41 (Ger), −.67 (Jpn). rG with individual N facets ranges from −.27 to −.75 (Table 5). Largest 5-HTTLPR effect of any A facet (h² = .019; Table 6).
  - **Interpretation:** Trust shares more genetic architecture with Neuroticism than any other A facet. The authors explicitly note this parallels Tellegen's alienation construct. For the crosswalk, this is important: Trust is assigned to Compassion (with A3 Altruism and A6 Tender-Mindedness), but its genetic profile looks more like an inverted N facet than a typical A facet. A6 Tender-Mindedness, by contrast, shows no 5-HTTLPR association and has small, inconsistent genetic correlations with N. These two Compassion-assigned facets are genetically dissimilar.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Strong (replicated across 3 samples; confirmed by molecular data)

**4. N4 Self-Consciousness — genetically isolated from 5-HTTLPR system**- **Currently assigned to:** Withdrawal aspect of Neuroticism (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Only N facet not associated with 5-HTTLPR (r = .038, p = .290; Table 6). Its aspect-mate N1 Anxiety *is* associated (r = .074, p = .04), and its other aspect-mates N3 Depression (r = .166, p < .001) and N6 Vulnerability (r = .085, p = .02) also are. Genetic correlations with A facets are also weaker and less consistent than for its Withdrawal aspect-mates.
  - **Interpretation:** This is a within-aspect dissociation. N4 is assigned to Withdrawal alongside N1, N3, and N6, but it does not share the same serotonergic genetic influence. This is a single-gene finding, so it could mean simply that N4 happens to not involve the serotonin transporter while still being genetically correlated with Withdrawal aspect-mates through other pathways. But it's worth tracking.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive (single molecular marker; not contradicted by twin data but not strongly confirmed either)

**5. Within-Compassion heterogeneity: A1 Trust vs. A6 Tender-Mindedness**- **Currently assigned to:** Both assigned to Compassion aspect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** A1 Trust has large genetic correlations with N facets (rG = −.53 to −.75 with several N facets) and significant 5-HTTLPR association (h² = .019). A6 Tender-Mindedness has small and inconsistent genetic correlations with N facets (rG ranging from −.54 to +.26, highly variable across samples) and no 5-HTTLPR association (p = .239). Their genetic profiles are quite dissimilar.
  - **Interpretation:** If Compassion is supposed to be a genetically coherent unit, these two facets don't support that. Trust behaves like a partially inverted Neuroticism facet; Tender-Mindedness does not.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Moderate (clear divergence in molecular data; genetic correlation patterns diverge but with some noise from Japanese sample)

**Notes**

  - This paper predates DeYoung et al. (2007) by 6 years, so it does not use aspect-level language. The aspect assignments referenced in the dissociation flags are applied retrospectively.
  - The paper only examines N and A domains. The most important crosswalk questions for N5 Impulsiveness (does it load on an E aspect?) and for N2 Angry Hostility (does it load on an A aspect?) cannot be fully answered here because E, C, and O data are not reported.
  - The authors' claim that N5 Impulsiveness "was not associated" with 5-HTTLPR appears to contradict Table 6, where it reaches significance (r = .117, p = .0011). This may be a drafting error where the Discussion reflects an earlier analysis, or the authors may have been making a relative rather than absolute claim. Either way, the table should be taken as authoritative over the prose.
  - The Japanese sample is small and young, producing some anomalous results (Depression loading on A factor, Altruism loading on N factor). The authors acknowledge this. Results from this sample should be weighted less heavily, though the genetic correlation patterns are broadly consistent with the other two samples for the strongest effects (Trust-N overlap, Angry Hostility-A overlap).
  - For the master crosswalk table: This paper contributes primarily to the "genetic correlation" and "molecular genetic" columns. It does not contribute developmental trajectory data (cross-sectional only) or full factor-structural data (only N and A domains analyzed).

**Hansen, Laeng, Vassend, Czajkowski, Endestad, Danielsen & Røysamb (2025)**

**Citation**

Hansen, H. M. U., Laeng, B., Vassend, O. M., Czajkowski, N. O., Endestad, T., Danielsen, A., & Røysamb, E. (2025). The role of Big Five personality domains and facets in musical sensibility: A twin study. *Scientific Reports, 15*, 13559. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-025-95661-z

**Design**- **Type:** Twin study (classical twin design), cross-sectional
  - **N:** 2,592 (1,232 paired + 1,360 single responders; 616 complete twin pairs: 119 MZ male, 307 MZ female, 44 DZ male, 146 DZ female)
  - **Age range:** 31–55 (mean ≈ 43–44; predominantly middle-aged)
  - **Instrument(s):** BFI-2 (60 items, 5 domains × 3 facets = 15 facets); MUSEBAQ Module 2 (24-item musical sensibility measure)
  - **Assessment interval:** N/A (cross-sectional)
  - **Key limitations:**- **Not NEO-PI-R.** The BFI-2 has 15 facets (3 per domain, 4 items each), which are broader composites than NEO's 30 facets. Cannot directly populate NEO facet rows in the master crosswalk table. However, BFI-2 facets approximate BFAS aspects in content breadth (see mapping below), so within-domain heterogeneity patterns are informative at the aspect level.
    - **Only 2 facets entered biometric models.** Facet-level heritability is reported only for O2 Aesthetic Sensitivity and A1 Compassion. All other facets have twin correlations via MZ/DZ (Table 2 reports only these 2 facets) and phenotypic data (Table 1 reports all 15), but no model-based heritability.
    - **Sample is 66% women, all Norwegian, all same-sex twin pairs.** Generalizability is reasonable for Western populations but not established beyond that.
    - **Sex differences in heritability not examined** (scores residualized for age and sex before twin modeling).
    - **May be underpowered for small D and C effects** (authors acknowledge this).

**Granularity**

**Facet-level** — all 15 BFI-2 facets reported in phenotypic analyses (Table 1). Two facets (O2 Aesthetic Sensitivity, A1 Compassion) entered biometric twin models with model-based heritability estimates. Domain-level heritability reported for O, N, A (the three domains entered in Cholesky). MZ/DZ twin correlations reported for all 5 domains and the 2 selected facets.

Crosswalk-relevant observation:** The Agreeableness domain has a C pattern (2×DZ > MZ), but A1 Compassion has a D pattern (2×DZ < MZ). This dissociation is striking — the Compassion facet shows a different genetic architecture than its parent domain. This likely reflects the other two A facets (Respectfulness, Trust — both roughly Politeness-aspect) pulling the domain toward a C pattern. This is a within-domain heterogeneity signal at the aspect level.

**Key finding:** The musical sensibility–Agreeableness covariance is ~100% genetic (both at domain and facet level). There is essentially no environmental correlation. In contrast, the Open-mindedness association has both genetic (rA = .63/.69) and environmental (rE = .33/.32) contributions.

**For the crosswalk:** The A1 Compassion genetic correlation (.33) is moderate but the environmental correlation is nil — whatever links Compassion to musical sensibility is entirely genetic in origin. This is an unusual pattern (most personality–outcome links have mixed etiology) and worth noting.

**Variance decomposition of musical sensibility**

[From Figure 4, PDF p. 8]

Of total variance in musical sensibility:

  - 28% = personality-related genetic factors
  - 36% = genetic factors unique to musical sensibility
  - 5% = personality-related environmental factors
  - 31% = environmental factors unique to musical sensibility (including measurement error)

Total heritability: .64. Of that, 44% overlaps with personality genetics, 56% is personality-independent.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**

**Best-fitting model was AE (additive genetic + non-shared environment) for both domain and facet Cholesky models**

  - Shared environment (C) and dominance (D) could both be dropped without significant loss of fit. [From Table 3, PDF p. 6]
  - This is consistent with the modal finding in adult twin personality research.
  - **However**, note the apparent C pattern for Agreeableness in the twin correlations (2×DZ > MZ) that does not translate into a significant C component in the multivariate model. This could reflect low power for C effects or the multivariate model absorbing C-like patterns into A.

**Extraversion and Conscientiousness genetic paths to musical sensibility were non-significant**

In the sensitivity analysis including all 5 domains, neither E nor C had significant genetic or environmental paths to musical sensibility after accounting for O, N, and A. [From PDF p. 5, Supplementary Table S2]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Openness (aspect) vs. Intellect (aspect) — within Open-mindedness**- **Facets involved:** O2 Aesthetic Sensitivity (≈ Openness aspect) vs. O1 Intellectual Curiosity (≈ Intellect aspect)
  - **Evidence:** O2 β = .44, O1 β = .07 in simultaneous regression with musical sensibility. O2's heritability (.58) and genetic correlation with musical sensibility (.69) are both high.
  - **What this means:** An affective/aesthetic criterion variable cleanly separates the Openness and Intellect aspects. This is convergent evidence for the aspect distinction. Not a "misassignment" flag per se — more a "these aspects really are different things" flag.
  - **Strength:** Strong

**2. Compassion (aspect) vs. Politeness (aspect) — within Agreeableness**- **Facets involved:** A1 Compassion (≈ Compassion aspect) vs. A2 Respectfulness + A3 Trust (≈ Politeness aspect)
  - **Evidence:** A1 r = .19, β = .11 with musical sensibility; A2 r = .02, A3 r = .05. Additionally, A1 has a D-pattern in twin correlations (MZ .36, DZ .14) while the Agreeableness domain has a C-pattern (MZ .46, DZ .27) — suggesting the Politeness-aspect facets are pulling the domain toward shared-environment influence while A1 Compassion has a different genetic architecture.
  - **What this means:** Compassion and Politeness aspects have different phenotypic correlates AND appear to have different genetic architectures. The domain-level Agreeableness statistics mask genuine aspect-level heterogeneity.
  - **Strength:** Moderate to strong (phenotypic split is clean; genetic architecture inference is suggestive because A2/A3 twin correlations aren't reported separately)

**3. No Withdrawal vs. Volatility dissociation for this criterion**- **Facets involved:** N1 Anxiety + N2 Depression (≈ Withdrawal) vs. N3 Emotional Volatility (≈ Volatility)
  - **Evidence:** All three N facets show similar associations with musical sensibility (rs = .11–.16, βs = .05–.07).
  - **What this means:** Musical sensibility does not differentiate the two N aspects. This is a *null* finding — it doesn't mean the aspects are the same, just that this particular criterion doesn't split them. Other criteria (e.g., externalizing behavior, anger) might.
  - **Strength:** N/A (null finding)

**Notes**- **Instrument translation issue:** BFI-2 facets ≠ NEO facets ≠ BFAS aspects, but the approximate mapping is close enough to extract aspect-level signals. The key conceptual correspondences (O2 ≈ Openness aspect, O1 ≈ Intellect aspect, A1 ≈ Compassion aspect, A2+A3 ≈ Politeness aspect, N3 ≈ Volatility, N1+N2 ≈ Withdrawal) are well-supported by Soto & John (2017) convergent validity data.**- This paper doesn't report heritability for most facets or any developmental data.** Its main crosswalk contribution is the within-domain phenotypic heterogeneity patterns (which map onto aspects) and the two facet-level heritability estimates. It cannot populate most cells in the master crosswalk table.**- The Agreeableness twin-correlation dissociation (domain = C pattern, Compassion facet = D pattern) is worth tracking.** If other twin studies show Compassion and Politeness aspects have different genetic architectures, that would be a substantial structural finding — the domain-level twin statistics would be misleading averages of two differently-structured aspects.**- O2 Aesthetic Sensitivity h² = .58 in this sample.** Compare to Jang et al. (1996) NEO Aesthetics h² when available. If these converge despite different instruments, that's a strong cross-instrument replication of heritability for the Openness aspect.**- Open-mindedness h² = .63 is notably high.** The paper notes this is consistent with recent cross-national data (Kandler et al., 2021). Worth checking whether this reflects the specific age range (31–55) or instrument effects. The BFI-2 Open-mindedness domain includes aesthetic sensitivity items that might inflate h² relative to broader NEO Openness which also includes Actions and Values facets.**- The sensitivity analysis removing the music-mentioning BFI-2 item (item 20) still showed strong O2–musical sensibility associations** (r = .46, β = .34 at facet level). This rules out trivial item overlap as the explanation. [From PDF p. 5]**Eid, Riemann, Angleitner & Borkenau (2003)**

**Citation**

Eid, M., Riemann, R., Angleitner, A., & Borkenau, P. (2003). Sociability and positive emotionality: Genetic and environmental contributions to the covariation between different facets of extraversion. *Journal of Personality, 71*(3), 319–346.

**Design**- **Type:** Twin study (behavior-genetic, cross-sectional)
  - **N:** 158 MZ twin pairs (118 female, 40 male) + 120 same-sex DZ twin pairs (97 female, 23 male) = 556 individuals
  - **Age range:** 18–68 (MZ: M = 33.97, SD = 12.76; DZ: M = 34.53, SD = 12.92)
  - **Instrument(s):** German NEO-FFI (12 Extraversion items only); situational mood ratings (12 positive affect items across 5 situations)
  - **Assessment methods:** Self-report, other-report (peer), and aggregated situational affect
  - **Assessment interval:** NEO-FFI completed 6 months to 2 years before laboratory session; mood states assessed on a single lab day across 5 situations
  - **Key limitations:**- **NEO-FFI, not NEO-PI-R.** Only 12 E items available. The three "facets" derived are ad hoc 4-item composites from PCA of these 12 items, NOT the standard NEO-PI-R facets. Mapping to E1–E6 is approximate.
    - **Only Extraversion.** No data on any other Big Five domain.
    - **Modest sample size** for behavior-genetic decomposition of multivariate models. Authors could not estimate full ACE independent pathway models (numerical problems, likely overfactoring); settled for AE models.
    - **German sample.** Cross-cultural generalizability uncertain.
    - **No dominance effects tested.** Authors state univariate analyses showed D effects were unimportant, but did not report those analyses.
    - **Zygosity determination:** 84% by genome mapping (<0.9% error); remaining 16% by physical similarity questionnaire.

**Granularity**

**Sub-facet level (ad hoc NEO-FFI composites), NOT standard NEO facet level.**

The authors PCA'd the 12 NEO-FFI Extraversion items (oblique rotation) and extracted three factors with eigenvalues > 1 (4.34, 1.37, 1.15), each marked by 4 items [from PDF p. 9, Table 1]:

        **Scale****** **Items (paraphrased from Table 1)****** **α (self)****** **α (other)****** **Approximate NEO-PI-R mapping****** **Sociability******

        Prefer doing things alone (R); enjoy talking to people; like having people around; prefer own way vs. leading (R)

        .63

        .70

        Closest to **E2 Gregariousness** (social preference/quantity). Some E1 Warmth content ("enjoy talking to people").

        **Positive Affect******

        Cheerful, high-spirited; not a cheerful optimist (R); not "light-hearted" (R); laugh easily

        .81

        .79

        Closest to **E6 Positive Emotions**.

        **Energy******

        Life is fast-paced; bursting with energy; very active person; like to be where the action is

        .65

        .70

        Mixed: "very active" and "fast-paced" = **E4 Activity**; "where the action is" could be **E5 Excitement Seeking**.

Additionally: **Aggregated Positive Affect (AP)** = 12 mood items (elated mood, relaxation, activation) rated in 5 lab situations, aggregated across situations and components. α = .95. This is a state-aggregated measure, not a trait scale from a personality inventory.

**Critical note for crosswalk mapping:** In DeYoung et al. (2007), Enthusiasm = E1 Warmth + E2 Gregariousness + E6 Positive Emotions; Assertiveness = E3 Assertiveness + E4 Activity + E5 Excitement Seeking. Under that mapping:

  - Sociability ≈ Enthusiasm (via E2 Gregariousness)
  - Positive Affect ≈ Enthusiasm (via E6 Positive Emotions)
  - Energy ≈ Assertiveness (via E4 Activity), though "where the action is" might cross to Enthusiasm

**Core Findings**

**Extraversion: Heritability estimates**

From the mixed-method AE independent pathway model (sociability = other-report; PA, Energy, AP = self-report) [from PDF p. 14, Table 3, Figure 1]:

Notes on the model structure [from PDF pp. 13–14, Figure 1]:

  - The "common" genetic factor is anchored by Positive Affect (its unique genetic variance = 0, meaning the common genetic factor IS the genetic component of Positive Affect).
  - Common heritability for the other variables represents how much of their genetic variance is explained by the genes of Positive Affect.
  - 61–71% of genetic variance in Energy, Sociability, and AP is **specific** — not shared with Positive Affect's genetic component.

**Extraversion: Genetic and environmental correlations**
