**Model comparison: AE vs. CE**

[From PDF p. 13, Table 2]

AE models (genetic + nonshared environment) fit better than CE models (shared environment + nonshared environment) across all three analysis sets. CE model was formally rejected for other-report data (χ² = 44.27, df = 30, p = .04). Shared environmental influences appear negligible — consistent with broader literature on Extraversion.

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Energy and Sociability: Weak genetic correlation despite both being "Extraversion facets"**- **Finding:** Genetic correlation between Energy and Sociability = .30 [from Table 5]
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** In BFAS terms, Energy ≈ Assertiveness aspect (via E4 Activity), Sociability ≈ Enthusiasm aspect (via E2 Gregariousness). A genetic correlation of .30 between them is consistent with these being **different aspects** — they share some genetic variance (as expected for facets within the same domain) but the majority of their genetic influence is specific.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Moderate. Consistent with the aspect distinction but doesn't directly test it. The constructs are NEO-FFI composites, not NEO-PI-R facets or BFAS aspects.

**2. Positive Affect genetically correlated with Energy as strongly as with Sociability**- **Finding:** Genetic r(PA, Energy) = .62; genetic r(PA, Sociability) = .57 [from Table 5]
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** Positive Affect ≈ E6 Positive Emotions, which DeYoung assigns to Enthusiasm. Energy ≈ E4 Activity, assigned to Assertiveness. If Positive Affect belonged cleanly to Enthusiasm, we'd expect its genetic correlation with the other Enthusiasm-facet (Sociability) to be *stronger* than with the Assertiveness-facet (Energy). It isn't — it's actually slightly weaker. This is consistent with E6 Positive Emotions being a **domain-general** Extraversion marker rather than sitting cleanly on one aspect.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive. The difference (.62 vs .57) is small and confidence intervals aren't reported, so this could be noise. But the direction is notable: PA's genetic overlap with Energy is at least as strong as with Sociability.
  - **Connection to other evidence:** In Jang et al. (1996), the additive/nonadditive genetic architecture split across E facets also failed to follow aspect boundaries — E6 Positive Emotions (additive) grouped with E2 Gregariousness and E4 Activity, not with its aspect-mate E1 Warmth (nonadditive). The present finding is consistent with that pattern.

**3. Heritability differences across Extraversion facets**- **Finding:** Energy h² = .45; Positive Affect h² = .36; Sociability h² = .28 [from Table 3]
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** Energy (≈ Assertiveness aspect) has the highest heritability; Sociability (≈ Enthusiasm aspect) has the lowest. The .17 gap between Energy and Sociability is a within-domain heritability dissociation, though we don't have confidence intervals to assess significance.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive. Consistent with Jang et al. (1996) where E4 Activity had the lowest h² (.29) among E facets — but note that in the present study, the construct closest to E4 Activity (Energy) has the *highest* h². The discrepancy may reflect the different item compositions (NEO-FFI Energy includes "where the action is" which could pull in E5 Excitement Seeking content).

**4. Environmental correlations: Energy–Sociability stronger than PA–Sociability**- **Finding:** Environmental r(Energy, Sociability) = .41; Environmental r(PA, Sociability) = .27 [from Table 5]
  - **Crosswalk relevance:** At the nonshared environmental level, Energy and Sociability are more correlated than PA and Sociability. This reverses the genetic pattern (where PA–Sociability was stronger). It suggests different causal pathways at genetic vs. environmental levels: genetically, PA is the hub connecting E facets; environmentally, shared behavioral contexts (social situations driving both activity and sociability) may matter more.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive. Interesting for theoretical understanding but doesn't directly test aspect assignments.

**Notes**

**Value for the crosswalk**This paper is **moderately useful.** It provides genetic correlation evidence among Extraversion sub-components, which is directly relevant to the question of whether the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness aspect boundary holds up at the etiological level. However, the constructs are NEO-FFI composites (4 items each), not the standard NEO-PI-R facets. The mapping to E1–E6 and to BFAS aspects is approximate.

**Key takeaway**

The genetic structure within Extraversion does NOT cleanly partition into two clusters corresponding to Enthusiasm and Assertiveness. Positive Affect (≈ E6 Positive Emotions) is genetically correlated with both Energy (≈ Assertiveness) and Sociability (≈ Enthusiasm) at similar magnitudes. This is more consistent with a single general genetic factor for Extraversion plus specific factors, rather than two genetically distinct aspects. The authors' independent pathway model with a single common genetic factor fit the data well, and that common factor explained only 29–39% of genetic variance in each non-anchor variable — meaning most genetic influence is facet-specific.

**Connection to Jang et al. (1996) extraction**

Jang et al. (1996) used the full NEO-PI-R and found that the additive/nonadditive genetic architecture split does NOT follow the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness boundary. The present study adds a complementary finding: genetic *correlations* among E sub-facets also don't show a two-cluster pattern. Together, these two papers suggest that the genetic architecture of Extraversion is organized around a general factor plus specific factors, not around two aspect-level clusters.

**What this paper cannot tell us**

  - Whether the two-aspect structure holds at the phenotypic factor-analytic level (it wasn't tested)
  - Whether the genetic findings generalize from NEO-FFI composites to the full NEO-PI-R 30-facet structure
  - Anything about developmental trajectories
  - Anything about Assertiveness-proper (E3) — the "Energy" construct here is closer to E4 Activity

**Kim, Kim, Yun et al. (2017)**

**Citation**

Kim, S. E., Kim, H.-N., Yun, Y.-J., Heo, S. G., Cho, J., Kwon, M.-J., Chang, Y., Ryu, S., Shin, H., Shin, C., Cho, N. H., Sung, Y. A., & Kim, H.-L. (2017). Meta-analysis of genome-wide SNP- and pathway-based associations for facets of neuroticism. *Journal of Human Genetics, 62*, 903–909. https://doi.org/10.1038/jhg.2017.61

**Design**- **Type:** Meta-analysis of three genome-wide association studies (GWAS), plus pathway-based (MAGENTA) analysis
  - **N:** 5,584 total (Ansan Ansung cohort: 2,424; Young Women cohort: 1,120; Kangbuk Samsung cohort: 2,040)
  - **Age range:** 18–79 across cohorts (mean ages: 58.9, 26.1, 39.3 respectively)
  - **Population:** All Korean
  - **Instrument(s):** Korean version of the NEO-PI-R; T-scores standardized to Korean combined-sex norms (N = 7,418)
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (single assessment per cohort)
  - **Key limitations:**

    - Small sample for GWAS (N = 5,584 is underpowered for typical GWAS effect sizes in personality)
    - Only one SNP reached genome-wide significance (P < 5 × 10⁻⁸); everything else is "suggestive" (P < 1 × 10⁻⁶)
    - Korean-only sample; no replication in other populations
    - NEO-PI-R facet-level reliabilities are low (Cronbach's α for facets: .494–.742; N4 Self-Consciousness = .494, N2 Angry Hostility = .544) [from PDF p. 2]
    - Genetic correlations between facets are mentioned as being in Supplementary Tables S1 and S2 but are not in the main paper and I cannot access the supplement
    - No SNP-based heritability estimates (h²_SNP) are reported for individual facets, only for domain-level neuroticism in the literature review

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All six NEO-PI-R neuroticism facets (N1–N6) analyzed individually for both SNP-based and pathway-based associations. No aspect-level analysis. No data on facets outside neuroticism.

**Missed opportunity:** The NEO-PI-R was administered in full (30 facets), but only the six neuroticism facets were analyzed. GWAS and pathway data for the other 24 facets are not reported.

**Core Findings**

**Neuroticism: SNP-based associations**

[from Table 2, PDF p. 3]

Each facet had its own top-ranked SNPs. The key point for the crosswalk is not the specific SNPs (none replicated across facets; most are suggestive only) but the fact that different facets show different genetic signals. This is expected and doesn't directly test aspect structure.

No SNP was associated with more than one facet. This is consistent with facet-specific genetic architectures, but the sample is too small to draw strong conclusions — null findings could easily reflect power limitations.

**Neuroticism: Pathway-based associations (the interesting part)**

[from Table 3, PDF p. 4; discussion PDF pp. 4–5]

Pathway analysis (MAGENTA, using KEGG and REACTOME databases) reveals which biological pathways are shared versus distinct across facets. This is the most crosswalk-relevant finding.

**How the pathway groupings map onto published aspect assignments**

This is where the paper becomes interesting for the crosswalk, though the evidence is indirect.

**Published assignments (DeYoung et al., 2007):**

  - Volatility = N2 Angry Hostility + N5 Impulsiveness
  - Withdrawal = N1 Anxiety + N3 Depression + N4 Self-Consciousness + N6 Vulnerability

**What the pathway data show:**

The shared biological pathways do NOT respect the Volatility/Withdrawal boundary:

  - **Axon guidance** groups N2 (Volatility) with N4 and N6 (both Withdrawal). N5 (Volatility, N2's aspect-mate) is absent.
  - **Focal adhesion + ECM** groups N2 (Volatility) with N3 (Withdrawal) and partially N1 (Withdrawal). Again, N5 is absent.
  - **N5 Impulsiveness** shares no significant pathways with any other facet. Its only significant pathway (renal cell carcinoma from KEGG) is biologically implausible as a personality mechanism and likely a false positive or indirect pathway artifact.
  - **N2 Angry Hostility** shares pathways with three Withdrawal facets (N3, N4, N6) but not with its own aspect-mate N5.

Put differently: if you grouped facets by shared biological pathways, you'd get {N2, N3, N4, N6} as a cluster and {N1, N5} as isolates — not {N2, N5} vs. {N1, N3, N4, N6}.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**

  - The paper mentions genetic correlations between facets (calculated via LD score regression) in Supplementary Table S2, but these data are not in the main text and the supplement is not available. This is a significant gap — genetic correlations would be the most direct evidence for whether aspect-mates share more genetic variance with each other than with non-aspect-mates.
  - Phenotypic (bivariate) correlations between facets are mentioned in Supplementary Table S1, also not available.
  - Genomic inflation factors ranged from 1.000 to 1.025, indicating no problematic population stratification. [from PDF p. 2]

**Dissociation Flags**

N5 Impulsiveness ↔ N2 Angry Hostility (Volatility aspect)

  - **Currently assigned to:** Volatility (both N2 and N5)
  - **What the evidence says:** N2 shares biological pathways (axon guidance, focal adhesion, ECM receptor interaction) with three Withdrawal facets (N3, N4, N6) but shares NO significant pathways with N5. N5 has no overlapping pathways with any facet. The Volatility aspect-mates are biologically dissimilar in this analysis.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive at best. Pathway analysis is an indirect measure of genetic architecture, the sample is small for GWAS, and null findings for N5 could reflect power limitations or the low reliability of the N5 scale (α = .604) and especially N4 (α = .494). This is a flag to watch, not a conclusion.

**N2 Angry Hostility — potential biological affinity with Withdrawal facets**- **Currently assigned to:** Volatility
  - **What the evidence says:** N2 shares three significant biological pathways with Withdrawal facets (axon guidance with N4/N6; focal adhesion and ECM with N3). This is consistent with the Jang et al. (1996) finding that N2 has a qualitatively different genetic architecture (nonadditive) from the other N facets, and potentially consistent with N2 being more biologically complex than a simple Volatility assignment suggests.
  - **Strength of evidence:** Suggestive. The pathway overlaps are real but their meaning for personality structure is speculative.

**Notes**- **Weight this paper lightly for the crosswalk.** It provides no heritability estimates, no developmental trajectories, no factor loadings. The pathway findings are an indirect evidence stream — they tell us something about which facets may share molecular genetic architecture, but the connection to phenotypic personality structure is several inferential steps removed.**- The missing supplementary data is frustrating.** The genetic correlations between facets (Supplementary Table S2) would be far more useful for the crosswalk than anything in the main paper. If we could see whether N2-N5 genetic correlation is higher or lower than N2-N3 or N2-N6 genetic correlation, that would directly test whether the Volatility grouping holds at the molecular genetic level. Without that table, the paper's most crosswalk-relevant data is inaccessible.**- Convergence with Jang et al. (1996):** The Jang twin study found N2 Angry Hostility has entirely nonadditive genetic variance (d² = .33) while N5 Impulsiveness has additive genetic variance (h² = .36). The current paper's finding that N2 and N5 share no biological pathways is directionally consistent — these two facets appear genetically dissimilar across independent evidence streams (twin-based architecture and molecular pathway overlap). This doesn't prove N2 and N5 don't belong together phenotypically, but it means the Volatility aspect cannot be justified on the grounds that its facets share genetic architecture.**- The N5 isolation is striking but may be artifactual.** N5 Impulsiveness is the NEO facet most often flagged as a poor fit within Neuroticism (it cross-loads on low Conscientiousness and low Agreeableness in many factor analyses). Its genetic isolation here could reflect genuine structural distinctiveness, or it could reflect the low signal-to-noise ratio of a brief, unreliable subscale (α = .604) in an underpowered GWAS sample.**- Korean-only sample.** Personality structure may differ cross-culturally, and the NEO-PI-R factor structure has shown some instability in East Asian samples. These findings may not generalize to the Western samples on which the BFAS aspect structure was developed. This is not a fatal problem — cross-cultural molecular genetic data is valuable — but it means we should be cautious about treating these pathway patterns as universal.**Soto & John (2012)**

**Citation**

Soto, C. J., & John, O. P. (2012). Development of Big Five domains and facets in adulthood: Mean-level age trends and broadly versus narrowly acting mechanisms. *Journal of Personality, 80*(4), 881–914. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-6494.2011.00752.x

**Design**- **Type:** Cross-sectional (Sample 1) + Longitudinal (Sample 2)
  - **N:** 601 (cross-sectional); 125 (longitudinal)
  - **Age range:** 20–64 (cross-sectional, M = 47.68, SD = 9.15); ~21–61 (longitudinal, born 1934–1939, assessed at ages 21, 27, 43, 52, 61)
  - **Instrument(s):** CPI-Big Five (Soto & John, 2009b) — 5 domains and 16 facets scored from the California Psychological Inventory item pool (400+ true/false items)
  - **Assessment interval:** Longitudinal sample assessed at 5 time points across ~40 years (ages 21, 27, 43, 52, 61)
  - **Key limitations:**- **NOT the NEO-PI-R.** The CPI-Big Five has 16 facets, not 30. Some correspond closely to NEO facets (see mapping below); others do not. Direct comparison to NEO-based crosswalk data requires caution.
    - Longitudinal sample is 125 women only (Mills College graduates, predominantly White). Small N limits power for detecting small change correlations (power = .61 for r = .20, power = .20 for r = .10). [from PDF p. 30]
    - Cross-sectional sample is 98% White, Eugene-Springfield Oregon. [from PDF p. 11]
    - Longitudinal sample has substantial missingness: only 58% completed all 5 waves, though multilevel models use all available data. [from PDF p. 11]
    - CPI-Big Five facet alphas averaged .69 (cross-sectional) and .63 (longitudinal) — adequate but lower than NEO-PI-R facets. Lower reliability attenuates both age trends and change correlations. [from PDF p. 12]
    - Preliminary tests for curvilinear age trends found few significant effects and poor cross-sample replication; only linear effects were modeled. This means any nonmonotonic trajectories (e.g., U-shaped or inverted-U) are missed. [from PDF p. 13, footnote 2]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level** — 16 CPI-Big Five facets across 5 domains. This is a non-standard facet set. The authors provide explicit correspondences with NEO-PI-R facets for some but not all. [from PDF p. 4]

This is a substantial gap — 15 of 30 NEO facets have no CPI-Big Five counterpart.

**Core Findings**

**Mean-Level Age Trends**

Age effects are reported as regression coefficients (b₁ for cross-sectional, G₁₀ for longitudinal). Units are T-score points per year of age. To convert to per-decade: multiply by 10. Effect size benchmarks per the authors: 2 T-score points = small, 5 = moderate, 8 = large. [from PDF p. 12]

Cross-sample replication was excellent: r = .86 (p < .001) between the 21 cross-sectional and 21 longitudinal age effects. [from PDF p. 16]

**Cross-sectional gender interaction:** All O facets and the O domain showed significant Age × Gender interactions (b₃ values 0.092*–0.124*), with age trends more positive for women than men. [from PDF Table 2, p. 18]

**Crosswalk relevance:** In the BFAS, O4 Actions → Openness aspect; O2 Aesthetics and O5 Ideas → Intellect aspect. This is a clean cross-aspect dissociation: the Openness aspect (via Adventurousness/Actions) declines while the Intellect aspect (via Idealism/Aesthetics and Intellectualism/Ideas) is stable.

Key within-domain dissociation:** Rumination declines robustly in both samples. Depression declines in the longitudinal sample. Anxiety and Irritability show no significant trends. The N domain is flat because only some facets decline. [from PDF p. 19, Figure 5]

**Crosswalk relevance:** In the BFAS, N1 Anxiety → Withdrawal; N2 Angry Hostility → Volatility; N3 Depression → Withdrawal. Rumination doesn't map cleanly to either aspect — it blends impulsive/intrusive content (Volatility-adjacent) with ruminative worry (Withdrawal-adjacent). The pattern here does NOT cleanly separate Volatility from Withdrawal: Irritability (Volatility-adjacent) is flat, but so is Anxiety (Withdrawal). The only clear decliners are Rumination (ambiguous mapping) and Depression (Withdrawal).

**Individual Differences in Change (Longitudinal Sample)**

All 5 domains and 13 of 16 facets showed significant individual differences in change (random-effect variance of age slopes, U₁ᵢ). [from PDF Table 3, p. 15]

The two facets without significant variability in change:

  - **Trustfulness** (A): marginally significant (p < .10)
  - **Humility** (A): not significant — individuals showed similar changes
  - **Depression** (N): marginally significant (p < .10)

[from PDF p. 21]

The distribution of individual change magnitudes is reported in Table 4. Key observations:

  - For most facets, individuals spanned 4–7 change categories (from large decrease to large increase). [from PDF Table 4, p. 22]
  - **Adventurousness:** 79% showed decreases (14% large, 26% moderate, 39% small). Near-universal decline. [from PDF Table 4]
  - **Humility:** 90% showed trivial or small increases. Very little individual variability. [from PDF Table 4]
  - **Compassion:** Highly variable — 27% showed large increases but 6% showed small decreases. [from PDF Table 4]

        .07

        No

        N–O

        .04

        No

        E–O

        −.10

        No

**All 10 pairwise domain-level change correlations are non-significant.** Average |r| = .09, maximum |r| = .16. [from PDF Table 5, p. 24]

This is a strong finding for narrowly acting mechanisms. Changes in one Big Five domain are independent of changes in other domains. The authors contrast this with Allemand et al. (2007, 2008) who found widespread change correlations — but those studies had unusually high initial domain intercorrelations (averaging .31), suggesting measurement overlap drove those findings. The CPI-Big Five domains intercorrelate only ~.05–.13 on average. [from PDF p. 9, 12]

**Change Correlations Between Facets (Longitudinal Sample)**

**Same-domain facet change correlations:**

  - Extraversion facets: average r = .46 (strongest: Assertiveness–Social Confidence, r = .59)
  - Neuroticism facets: average r = .42
  - Conscientiousness facets: average r = .36
  - Agreeableness facets: average r = .28
  - Openness facets: average r = .19 (weakest; Idealism–Adventurousness only marginally significant at r = .15, p < .10)

[from PDF p. 25]

**Different-domain facet change correlations:**

  - Average |r| = .17
  - 38 of 89 different-domain pairs were statistically significant
  - Strongest: Self-Discipline (C) ↔ Rumination (N), r = −.48

[from PDF p. 25]

**Critical test:** For all 105 facet pairs, the authors compared the change correlation to the initial (age 21) correlation using 95% confidence interval overlap (Allemand et al., 2008 method). In ALL 105 cases, the confidence intervals overlapped, meaning no change correlation was significantly different from the corresponding initial correlation. [from PDF p. 25]

**Interpretation:** The facet-level change correlations are entirely explainable by pre-existing conceptual and measurement overlaps between facets. They do NOT indicate that previously independent facets were drawn together by a common developmental mechanism. For example, Self-Discipline and Rumination change together (r = −.48), but they were already correlated at age 21 (r = −.43). [from PDF p. 25]

**Cross-Sectional Age Differences in Trait Interrelations**

The authors split the cross-sectional sample into three age groups (20–43, 44–52, 53–64) and tested whether domain and facet intercorrelations differed across groups.

  - Domain level: 1 of 30 pairwise tests (3%) significant at p < .05 — at or below chance. [from PDF p. 20]
  - Facet level: 15 of 360 pairwise tests (4%) significant — at or below chance. [from PDF p. 20]
  - Multiple-group covariance models constraining covariances equal across age groups fit well at both domain (CFI = .923, RMSEA = .017, SRMR = .050) and facet (CFI = .989, RMSEA = .030, SRMR = .083) levels. [from PDF p. 20]

**Interpretation:** Trait interrelations are stable across age groups. No evidence that aging introduces new covariance between previously independent traits. Consistent with narrowly acting mechanisms. [from PDF p. 20]

**Dissociation Flags**

1. Extraversion: Gregariousness (↓) vs. Assertiveness (↑) — cross-aspect divergence

  - **CPI facets involved:** Gregariousness (≈ NEO E2) and Assertiveness (≈ NEO E3)
  - **BFAS mapping:** E2 → Enthusiasm; E3 → Assertiveness aspect
  - **Evidence:** Gregariousness G₁₀ = −0.083* (decline); Assertiveness G₁₀ = 0.064* (increase). Opposite-direction trends in the longitudinal sample. Domain-level E is flat, concealing the divergence. [from PDF p. 17, Table 3]
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** Replicates Roberts et al. (2006) meta-analytic distinction between social vitality (declining) and social dominance (increasing). However, the cross-sectional sample showed NO significant E facet trends, so this finding rests on N = 125 women only. Social Confidence (which blends both aspects) is intermediate and non-significant.
  - **Note:** This is consistent with the aspect structure, not evidence against it. The two BFAS Extraversion aspects are developing in opposite directions — exactly what the aspect model would predict if they are separable constructs. This converges with Brandt et al. (2023) and Terracciano et al. (2005).

2. Openness: Adventurousness (↓) vs. Idealism/Intellectualism (flat) — cross-aspect divergence

  - **CPI facets involved:** Adventurousness (≈ NEO O4 Actions), Idealism (≈ NEO O2 Aesthetics), Intellectualism (≈ NEO O5 Ideas)
  - **BFAS mapping:** O4 → Openness aspect; O2 and O5 → Intellect aspect
  - **Evidence:** Adventurousness b₁ = −0.220*, G₁₀ = −0.116* (robust decline in both samples); Idealism and Intellectualism both ~0 and non-significant. [from PDF Tables 2–3, p. 17–18]
  - **Strength:** **Moderate to strong.** Replicates across both samples. Converges with Terracciano et al. (2005) finding that O4 Actions declined but O2 Aesthetics and O5 Ideas did not. Clean separation along the Openness/Intellect aspect boundary.
  - **Note:** Again, this is consistent with the aspect structure, not evidence against it. The Openness aspect declines while the Intellect aspect is stable.

3. Conscientiousness: Industriousness (↑) vs. Orderliness (flat) — cross-aspect divergence

  - **CPI facets involved:** Industriousness (≈ NEO C4 Achievement Striving, partially), Orderliness (≈ NEO C2 Order), Self-Discipline (≈ NEO C5)
  - **BFAS mapping:** C4, C5 → Industriousness aspect; C2 → Orderliness aspect
  - **Evidence:** Industriousness b₁ = 0.226*, G₁₀ = 0.145* (robust increase in both samples); Orderliness non-significant in both. Self-Discipline significant in longitudinal only (G₁₀ = 0.128*). [from PDF Tables 2–3, p. 16]
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** Replicates Jackson et al. (2009). However, CPI Industriousness blends C4 Achievement Striving with broader work-ethic content (including C3 Dutifulness-like items), so the NEO mapping is imprecise.
  - **Note:** Self-Discipline (Industriousness aspect in BFAS) increases, which is consistent with the aspect model — both Industriousness-aspect facets go up while the Orderliness-aspect facet is flat.

4. Neuroticism: Rumination/Depression (↓) vs. Anxiety/Irritability (flat) — does NOT cleanly map to Withdrawal vs. Volatility

  - **CPI facets involved:** Rumination (no clean NEO equivalent), Depression (≈ NEO N3), Anxiety (≈ NEO N1), Irritability (≈ NEO N2 Angry Hostility)
  - **BFAS mapping:** N1 Anxiety → Withdrawal; N3 Depression → Withdrawal; N2 Angry Hostility → Volatility. Rumination has no standard BFAS mapping.
  - **Evidence:** Rumination b₁ = −0.097*, G₁₀ = −0.102* (declines in both samples). Depression G₁₀ = −0.064* (longitudinal only). Anxiety and Irritability non-significant. [from PDF Tables 2–3, p. 19]
  - **Strength:** **Ambiguous.** The decliners (Rumination, Depression) don't align with a single aspect. Depression is Withdrawal; Rumination is hard to map. The non-decliners include one from each aspect (Anxiety = Withdrawal, Irritability = Volatility). This does NOT produce a clean Withdrawal-vs-Volatility dissociation.
  - **Note:** Brandt et al. (2023) found N2 Angry Hostility and N5 Impulsiveness (both Volatility) decreasing while N1 Anxiety and N6 Vulnerability (Withdrawal) increased — the opposite of what you'd predict from this paper's Irritability (≈ N2) being flat. Different instruments, different age ranges, different findings. Be cautious about treating CPI Irritability as equivalent to NEO N2.

**5. Agreeableness: relatively homogeneous development — no clear dissociation**

  - All three A facets trend positive. Only Trustfulness replicates across both samples.
  - Humility shows minimal individual variability in change — almost everyone increases a small amount.
  - This is the domain with the LEAST within-domain developmental heterogeneity in this paper, which is interesting given that Agreeableness has the messiest factor structure (high cross-loadings in DeYoung 2007).
  - **Strength:** **No dissociation flag.** All facets move in the same direction.

**Cross-Domain Structural Finding: Narrowly Acting Mechanisms**

The paper's strongest contribution to the crosswalk is not any individual facet finding — it's the structural argument:

  - **Domain-level changes are independent.** All 10 pairwise change correlations are non-significant (average |r| = .09). [from PDF Table 5]
  - **Facet-level change correlations are explainable by pre-existing overlap.** No change correlation exceeds the initial correlation. [from PDF p. 25]
  - **Trait interrelations don't change with age.** Cross-sectional covariance structures are stable across younger/middle/older groups. [from PDF p. 20]

**What this means for the crosswalk:** The BFAS aspect structure posits that within each domain, two aspects capture meaningful variance. This paper supports the idea that the five domains develop independently, and that facet-level co-development is driven by existing structural overlap (i.e., facets that load on the same aspect change together because they share variance, not because a new mechanism is pushing them together). This is *consistent with* the aspect model's structural claims, though it doesn't directly test them.

**However:** The finding that domain-level changes are fully independent argues against Digman's (1997) higher-order α/β (Stability/Plasticity) metatraits as developmental processes. If Stability were a real developmental force, C, A, and low-N should change in correlated fashion. They don't. The r between C and A change is literally −.01. [from PDF Table 5]

**Notes**- **Instrument mismatch is the main limitation for the crosswalk.** The CPI-Big Five covers only 16 of 30 NEO facets, and some mappings are imprecise. This paper can corroborate NEO-based findings (especially for Gregariousness, Assertiveness, Order, Adventurousness, Anxiety, Depression, Irritability) but cannot substitute for NEO-level data on the 15 unmapped facets.**- The change correlation finding (all domain-level rs non-significant) is the paper's most important contribution.** It's a cleaner test than Allemand et al. (2007, 2008) because the CPI-Big Five domains have near-zero initial intercorrelations (~.05–.13), so change correlations can't be artifacts of measurement overlap.**

  - Converging evidence scorecard for cross-aspect developmental dissociations:

    - E: Gregariousness ↓ / Assertiveness ↑ — now in Roberts (2006) meta-analysis, Soto & John (2012), and Brandt et al. (2023). Well-established.
    - O: Adventurousness ↓ / Idealism+Intellectualism flat — now in Terracciano (2005), Soto & John (2012), and partly in Brandt (2023). Well-established.
    - C: Industriousness ↑ / Orderliness flat — now in Jackson (2009), Soto & John (2012), Terracciano (2005). Well-established.
    - N: Pattern is messy across papers. No consistent cross-aspect dissociation yet.

  - **The Self-Discipline result is worth flagging for cross-paper comparison.** CPI Self-Discipline increased (G₁₀ = 0.128*) in the longitudinal sample. Terracciano et al. (2005) found NEO C5 Self-Discipline *decreased* (γ₁₀ = −0.98 T/decade). These point in opposite directions. Could reflect: (a) different items/content despite the same label, (b) different age ranges or cohorts, (c) different sample compositions. This is a genuine inconsistency that needs resolution before trusting any single C5 developmental trajectory estimate.**- The Openness × Gender interaction** is unique to this paper's cross-sectional sample and not replicated in the longitudinal sample (which is women-only and therefore can't test it). The finding that O trends are more positive for women than men could matter for papers with mixed-gender samples. [from PDF p. 18]**Jang, McCrae, Angleitner, Riemann & Livesley (1998)**

**Citation**

Jang, K. L., McCrae, R. R., Angleitner, A., Riemann, R., & Livesley, W. J. (1998). Heritability of facet-level traits in a cross-cultural twin sample: Support for a hierarchical model of personality. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 74*(6), 1556–1565. https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.74.6.1556

**Design**- **Type:** Classical twin study (MZ vs. DZ), cross-cultural (Canada + Germany), analyzed simultaneously
  - **N:** 998 twin pairs total — 618 MZ (183 Canadian, 435 German) and 380 DZ (175 Canadian, 205 German). A separate American retest sample (N = 338) was used for residual facet score reliability.
  - **Age range:** 14–71 years (Canadian M ≈ 31–33; German M ≈ 29–33; see Table 1 for breakdowns by zygosity). Retest sample: 60–94 years.
  - **Instrument(s):** NEO-PI-R (English version for Canada; German translation by Ostendorf & Angleitner, 1994)
  - **Assessment interval:** Cross-sectional (single administration). Retest reliability computed on a separate 2-year longitudinal sample from the Baltimore Longitudinal Study of Aging.
  - **Key limitations:**- **DZ male pairs are sparse** — only 48 Canadian and 30 German DZ-M pairs. This precluded Sex × Genotype models and limits power for sex-specific heritability estimation. [from PDF p. 3, Table 1]
    - **Zygosity determined by questionnaire**, not blood typing. Both questionnaires predict blood-based zygosity at ≥94% accuracy, but misclassification would attenuate MZ–DZ differences and thus underestimate heritability. [from PDF p. 2–3]
    - **Self-report only.** No peer ratings. The authors discuss this limitation and note that multiobserver designs (as in Riemann et al., 1997) yield higher heritability estimates (~70% vs. ~50%). [from PDF p. 9]
    - **Residual score reliability estimated from a different (older, American) sample** than the twin samples. The retest reliabilities may not generalize perfectly to the younger Canadian/German samples. [from PDF p. 7]
    - **Nonadditive genetic effects (dominance) were tested and found negligible.** The authors report this in a footnote — ADE models did not improve fit over AE models. This differs from Jang et al. (1996), which found nonadditive effects for several facets. The larger sample here may provide a more reliable estimate, or the cross-cultural design may wash out sample-specific nonadditive effects. [from PDF p. 4, footnote 3]

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** All 30 NEO-PI-R facets reported with:

  - Twin correlations (MZ and DZ, by country) for both raw and residual scores [Tables 2, 5]
  - Model-fitting statistics for raw and residual scores [Tables 3, 6]
  - Heritability estimates (h², c², e²) for raw and residual scores [Tables 4, 7]
  - Retest reliabilities for residual scores [Table 7]
  - Heritability as proportion of reliable variance (h²/r_tt) for residual scores [Table 7]

**Domain-level** factor heritabilities also reported (all five factors). [from PDF p. 6]

**No aspect-level analyses.** The paper predates DeYoung et al. (2007). However, the facet-level data can be mapped onto aspect assignments post hoc.

**Core Findings**

**Key design feature: Raw vs. Residual heritability**This paper's central contribution is the **decomposition of facet variance into common (shared with the five factors) and specific (unique to each facet) components**, followed by heritability analysis of each component separately. [from PDF pp. 1–2]

  - **Raw facet scores** confound common and specific variance. Heritability of raw scores could be entirely due to the heritability of the common factors.
  - **Residual facet scores** are standardized residuals from regressing each facet on all five factor scores, plus age and gender. They capture only the specific variance — what makes each facet distinct from its domain.
  - If residual scores are heritable, that means the **facet-specific** variance has its own genetic basis, not just inherited factor-level influence.

The five common factors are all heritable at h² ≈ .48–.50 (combined Canadian–German AE model): N = .49, E = .50, O = .48, A = .48, C = .49. Domain scales: N = .49, E = .50, O = .52, A = .44, C = .46. [from PDF p. 6, footnote 5]

C5 Self-Discipline (like A5 Modesty) shows shared environmental effects in Canada but not Germany. C2 Order shows equivalent form (AE) across samples but different magnitudes (Canada h² = .58 vs. Germany h² = .44). [from Tables 3–4]

Important contrast with Jang et al. (1996):** In the earlier 1996 paper (Canadian sample only), C2 Order, C5 Self-Discipline, and C6 Deliberation all had CE models (no genetic variance). Here in 1998, with a larger cross-cultural design: C2 is AE in both samples; C6 is AE combined; C5 is ACE in Canada but AE in Germany. The "no-genetics" finding for three C facets in the 1996 paper does not replicate. This was already flagged in the Jang (1996) and Luciano (2006) extractions. [inference from comparison with Jang 1996; the 1998 paper does not discuss this discrepancy directly]

**C4 Achievement Striving and C6 Deliberation residuals: CE model best (no heritable specific variance).** [from Tables 6–7]

**C1 Competence: heritable but barely.** Residual h² = .11 — the lowest of any AE-model facet. h²/r_tt = .25, meaning only 25% of reliable specific variance is genetic. C1's specific variance is overwhelmingly environmental. Its retest reliability is also the lowest in the C domain (.44), suggesting much of C1's specificity is poorly measured. [from Table 7]

**C3 Dutifulness: highest h²/r_tt in the entire dataset.** At .65, Dutifulness has the highest proportion of reliable specific variance attributable to genetics. This is partly driven by its very low retest reliability (.43) — less reliable variance means the heritable portion looms larger proportionally. Still, the raw residual h² of .28 is solid. [from Table 7]

**Cross-domain and structural findings**

  - **Domain-level heritability is remarkably uniform.** All five factors: h² = .48–.50. All five domain scales: h² = .44–.52. This uniformity contrasts with the heterogeneity at the facet level. [from PDF p. 6, footnote 5]**- For 26 of 30 raw facets, the AE model was equivalent in form and magnitude across Canada and Germany.** Four exceptions: O6 Values, C2 Order (AE in both, but magnitudes differ), A5 Modesty, C5 Self-Discipline (ACE in Canada, AE in Germany). Cross-cultural invariance is the norm. [from PDF pp. 4–5]**- For the residual facet scores, 26 of 30 showed equivalent AE effects in both samples.** Four residual facets were best fit by CE models: A3 Altruism, A5 Modesty, C4 Achievement Striving, C6 Deliberation. Their specific variance is shaped by shared environment, not genes. [from PDF pp. 7–8, Tables 6–7]**- Additive genetic effects accounted for 25% to 65% of the reliable specific variance** (median ≈ 45%) for the 26 AE-model residual facets. [from Table 7, author summary on PDF p. 1]**- Shared environmental effects are negligible.** For raw scores, only A5 Modesty and C5 Self-Discipline in the Canadian sample showed appreciable c². For residuals, the four CE facets show c² of .18–.26. No other facets show shared environment. [from Tables 4, 7]**- Nonadditive genetic effects (dominance) were tested and found negligible** — ADE models did not improve over AE. This is notable because Jang et al. (1996), using only the Canadian subsample, found nonadditive effects for several facets (E3, E5, O2, A4, C1). The present study's larger sample and cross-cultural design failed to replicate those nonadditive effects. [from PDF p. 4, footnote 3]**- Intercorrelations among residual facets were "only trivial,"** consistent with five factors exhausting the common variance. This means the residual scores genuinely capture facet-specific variance, not residual common structure. [from PDF p. 6, footnote 6]**Dissociation Flags**

**1. A3 Altruism — no heritable specific variance (Compassion aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Compassion (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** A3 Altruism's residual is best fit by CE (c² = .20, e² = .80). Its specific variance has no genetic basis. Aspect-mates A1 Trust (h² = .25–.31) and A6 Tendermindedness (h² = .28) both have heritable specific variance. Whatever makes Altruism distinctive from the A factor is environmentally, not genetically, determined.
  - **Raw heritability is also lowest in A domain:** h² = .27 (vs. .31–.38 for other A facets).
  - **Strength:** Moderate. The CE vs. AE distinction can be fragile in moderate samples. But the pattern is consistent across raw and residual analyses: A3 is the weakest-genetically-influenced A facet at every level of analysis.

**2. A5 Modesty — no heritable specific variance + cross-cultural instability (Politeness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Politeness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** A5 Modesty's residual is CE (c² = .26, e² = .74). Its raw heritability also varies drastically across cultures: h² = .00 in Canada, h² = .46 in Germany. Aspect-mates A2 Straightforwardness (h² = .31 raw, h² = .25 residual) and A4 Compliance (h² = .38 raw, h² = .26 residual) both have stable, moderate heritability.
  - **Strength:** Moderate to strong. The cross-cultural instability in raw heritability is itself a signal — if Modesty's genetic architecture varies by culture, it may be more of a characteristic adaptation (environmentally shaped) than a basic tendency (endogenous). The paper explicitly discusses this theoretical distinction. [from PDF pp. 2, 9]

**3. C4 Achievement Striving — no heritable specific variance (Industriousness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Industriousness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** C4's residual is CE (c² = .26, e² = .74). Its raw heritability is actually the highest in the C domain (.46), suggesting that C4's heritability is entirely attributable to the common Conscientiousness factor. The specific variance — what makes Achievement Striving distinct from C — is shared-environment, not genetic. Aspect-mates C1 Competence (residual h² = .11, borderline) and C5 Self-Discipline (residual h² = .28) both retain heritable specific variance.
  - **Strength:** Moderate. This is a theoretically interesting pattern: C4 Achievement Striving may be well-captured by the broad C factor, with its specific content (goal-setting, ambition) being environmentally shaped.

**4. C6 Deliberation — no heritable specific variance (Orderliness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Orderliness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** C6's residual is CE (c² = .18, e² = .82). Aspect-mates C2 Order (residual h² = .26) and C3 Dutifulness (residual h² = .28) both have heritable specific variance. C6's specific content (careful decision-making, impulse control in planning) has no genetic basis beyond the common C factor.
  - **Strength:** Moderate. C6 Deliberation also has the highest retest reliability (.71) among C residuals, so low heritability is not an artifact of unreliability — the construct is well-measured but just not heritable at the specific level.

**5. C1 Competence — very low specific heritability (Industriousness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Industriousness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** C1 Competence's residual h² = .11, the lowest of any AE-model facet. h²/r_tt = .25, meaning only a quarter of its reliable specific variance is genetic (vs. 40–54% for most facets). C1's retest reliability is also the lowest (.44), suggesting its specificity is poorly measured. Together with C4's CE model, this means two of three Industriousness facets have very weak or absent genetic specific variance.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive. The h² = .11 is low but not zero — the AE model was still selected. The low reliability complicates interpretation.

**6. E5 Excitement Seeking — highest specific heritability in E domain (Assertiveness aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Assertiveness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** E5's residual h² = .36 (highest in E), h²/r_tt = .52 (highest in E). Its aspect-mates E3 Assertiveness (h² = .29) and E4 Activity (h² = .27) are more typical. This isn't necessarily a dissociation — E5's high specific heritability could reflect its unique content (sensation-seeking, risk-taking) having a strong genetic basis. But it does set E5 apart from its aspect-mates in genetic architecture.
  - **This is NOT evidence of misassignment.** It's a note that E5 has more heritable specificity than other Assertiveness facets. Worth tracking but not a flag per se.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive/informational.

**7. O6 Values — cross-cultural instability (Intellect aspect)**- **Currently assigned to:** Intellect (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** O6 Values is one of four facets where raw heritability magnitudes differed across samples: Canada h² = .61, Germany h² = .38. However, the residual score was stably heritable (h² = .35, AE combined), so the instability is in the common-variance component, not the specific. This suggests the relationship between Values and the O factor (not Values itself) varies across cultures.
  - **Strength:** Suggestive. The raw instability is notable but the residual stability undermines it as a structural concern.

**Notes**

  - **This paper is the primary heritability reference for the crosswalk's facet-level column.** It supersedes Jang et al. (1996) in several ways: larger sample, cross-cultural replication, and the crucial decomposition into common vs. specific variance. For any discrepancy between 1996 and 1998, the 1998 estimates should be preferred.**- The residual heritability estimates are the most informative for the crosswalk.** Raw h² tells us "how heritable is this facet?" Residual h² tells us "how heritable is what makes this facet distinct from its domain?" — and it's the distinctiveness that matters for aspect assignment. A facet could have high raw h² but low residual h², meaning its heritability is entirely inherited from the factor, with nothing genetically unique about the facet itself.**- The four CE residual facets (A3, A5, C4, C6) are theoretically significant.** The authors interpret this through McCrae & Costa's Five-Factor Theory: facets with heritable specific variance are "basic tendencies" (endogenous dispositions), while facets whose specific variance is environmental may be "characteristic adaptations" (shaped by experience). The CE facets' specific content may be more culturally malleable than the AE facets'. [from PDF pp. 2, 8–9]**- The CE vs. AE distinction at the residual level does NOT map onto aspect boundaries.** Each domain that has CE residual facets has them distributed across both aspects (A: one per aspect; C: one per aspect). This means the aspect structure is not cleanly reflected in the genetic architecture of specific variance. The genetic heterogeneity crosscuts the aspects.**- Comparison with Jang et al. (1996) on the "no genetics" facets:** Jang (1996) found CE models (no genetic variance) for raw O3 Feelings, A5 Modesty, C2 Order, C5 Self-Discipline, and C6 Deliberation. In the present 1998 paper, all of these show genetic effects at the raw level (though A5 Modesty is unstable). At the residual level, A5 is CE and C6 is CE, partially consistent. But C2, C5, and O3 are all AE in the residuals. The 1996 "no genetics" pattern was substantially sample-dependent.**- The paper's theoretical contribution:** Facet-level traits are not merely exemplars of the factors they define. They are "discrete constructs with their own heritable and thus biological basis" (p. 1556). This supports the hierarchical model where narrow traits have genetic specificity beyond the broad factors — which is a prerequisite for the aspect-level structure to have genetic meaning.**- Limitation for the crosswalk:** This study reports only univariate heritability (how much genetic variance each facet has). It does NOT report genetic correlations between facets (whether the same genes influence co-assigned facets). Two facets could both be heritable but for completely different genetic reasons. Multivariate genetic analyses — genetic correlations or Cholesky decompositions among facets — would be needed to test whether aspect-mates share genetic variance. The Pilia et al. SardiNIA data (already extracted) partially addresses this, but with a pedigree design rather than a twin design.**Jackson, Bogg, Walton, Wood, Harms, Lodi-Smith, Edmonds & Roberts (2009)**

**Citation**

Jackson, J. J., Bogg, T., Walton, K. E., Wood, D., Harms, P. D., Lodi-Smith, J., Edmonds, G. W., & Roberts, B. W. (2009). Not all conscientiousness scales change alike: A multimethod, multisample study of age differences in the facets of conscientiousness. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 96*(2), 446–459. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0014156

**Design**- **Type:** Cross-sectional, two studies. Study 1: community sample with self- and observer reports. Study 2: representative state sample (Illinois) with self-report only.
  - **N:** Study 1: N = 274 (168 F, 106 M); Study 2: N = 613 (351 F)
  - **Age range:** Study 1: 19–94 (M = 51.25, SD = 16.43); Study 2: 19–86 (M = 47.53, SD = 17.71)
  - **Instrument(s):** Conscientiousness Adjective Checklist (CAC; Roberts et al., 2004) — 5 facet scales: Industriousness, Orderliness, Impulse Control, Reliability, Conventionality. IPIP-AB5C Conscientiousness scales (Goldberg, 1999) — 9 scales: Conscientiousness, Efficiency, Cautiousness, Dutifulness, Rationality, Purposefulness, Perfectionism, Organization, Orderliness. Observer ratings via CAC only (Study 1). Study 2 used AB5C only (no CAC, no observer reports).
  - **Assessment interval:** N/A — cross-sectional
  - **Key limitations:**- **Cross-sectional design.** Age differences, not longitudinal change. Cohort effects cannot be ruled out. The authors acknowledge this explicitly. [from PDF p. 14]
    - **Non-standard facet system.** Uses Roberts et al.'s 5-facet model of conscientiousness, not the NEO-PI-R 6-facet system. Translation to DeYoung's Industriousness/Orderliness aspects requires inference. See mapping notes below.
    - **No data on other Big Five domains.** Conscientiousness only. Cannot examine cross-domain patterns.
    - **Study 1 community sample** recruited via fliers/newspaper ads — not representative. Study 2 is representative but had a very low response rate (RR3 = 18.5%). [from PDF pp. 5, 10]
    - **Study 2 lacks observer reports** and lacks the CAC, so only Industriousness, Orderliness, and Impulse Control facets are assessed (no Reliability or Conventionality). [from PDF p. 10]
    - **Measurement is at the adjective/item level** for the CAC and IPIP-AB5C, but results are reported at the facet-composite level. No item-level data reported.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level** within the Conscientiousness domain only. Five Roberts et al. facets reported: Industriousness, Orderliness, Impulse Control, Reliability, Conventionality. Individual AB5C and CAC scale scores also reported.

**Critical mapping note:** These facets are NOT the NEO-PI-R C1–C6. The approximate mapping to NEO-PI-R facets and DeYoung aspects, based on Roberts, Chernyshenko et al. (2005) and item content, is:

        **Roberts facet****** **NEO-PI-R facets that load here****** **DeYoung aspect alignment******

        Industriousness

        C4 Achievement Striving, C5 Self-Discipline, C1 Competence (partial)

        → Industriousness

        Orderliness

        C2 Order, C3 Dutifulness (partial)

        → Orderliness

        Impulse Control

        C6 Deliberation, plus N5 Impulsiveness content

        → Orderliness (C6) + cross-domain (N5)

        Reliability

        C3 Dutifulness (partial), plus Agreeableness content

        → Ambiguous (C/A boundary)

        Conventionality

        Openness (reverse) content, plus C3 Dutifulness (partial)

        → Ambiguous (C/O boundary)

This mapping is my inference from Roberts, Chernyshenko et al. (2005), which the authors cite, combined with the scale descriptions in Table 1 [PDF p. 5]. The authors themselves note that Reliability is almost equivalently correlated with Conscientiousness and Agreeableness [PDF p. 5], and that Conventionality is "often thought to belong to the domain of openness to experience" [PDF p. 5]. These are genuinely cross-domain constructs, not clean C facets.

Bold d values and r values indicate p < .05 per the paper's reporting. [from PDF Table 3, p. 9]

**Key finding:** Massive heterogeneity within the C domain. Orderliness shows essentially zero age differences (d₁₃ = 0.07 for composite), while Impulse Control shows nearly a full SD difference across the lifespan (d₁₃ = 0.88). Industriousness increases early, Conventionality increases late.

**Curvilinear effect:** CAC Industriousness showed a significant quadratic effect (β = −.12, p < .05): increases until middle age, plateaus, slight drop after age 80. [from PDF p. 8]

**Key finding:** Observer reports show no significant young→middle differences for any facet. All significant differences are middle→older. This is a striking self/observer discrepancy: self-reports locate Industriousness increases in young→middle adulthood, while observers only see the difference in middle→older adulthood. [from PDF p. 8–9]

The authors interpret this as either: (a) internal changes in self-perception precede observable behavioral change, or (b) observers are slower to update their impressions (reputational lag). [from PDF p. 13]

**Note on Orderliness:** Orderliness still shows the weakest age differences in observer reports, consistent with self-reports, though the r with age (.15) reaches significance here. [from PDF p. 8]

**Replication:** Study 2 replicates Study 1 in all key respects: Industriousness increases young→middle then plateaus; Orderliness shows minimal age differences; Impulse Control shows the largest cumulative differences. Effect sizes in Study 2 fall within the confidence intervals of Study 1. [from PDF p. 11]

**Curvilinear effects (Study 2):** Industriousness composite (β = −.14), Purposefulness (β = −.11), Dutifulness (β = −.11), Organization (β = −.16), Conscientiousness (β = −.13) — all increased until middle age then plateaued or slightly decreased. [from PDF p. 11]

**SES Interactions (Study 2)**

Two significant Age × SES interactions out of nine scales tested [from PDF p. 12]:

  - **Cautiousness (Impulse Control):** β = −.081, p < .05. Low-SES individuals showed steeper age differences in cautiousness than high-SES individuals. [from PDF p. 12, Figure 1]
  - **Perfectionism (Orderliness):** β = −.091, p < .05. Low-SES individuals increased in perfectionism with age; high-SES individuals decreased. [from PDF p. 12, Figure 2]

No Age × SES interaction for any other scale. Main effects of SES on Dutifulness (β = .17), Efficiency (β = .09), Organization (β = .27), and AB5C Conscientiousness (β = .12) — higher SES = higher scores, but same developmental pattern. [from PDF p. 12]

**Measurement Invariance**Strict measurement invariance (configural + metric + scalar) was achieved for **all 14 self-report scales** and **all 5 observer-report scales** across the three age groups in Study 1, and for all AB5C scales in Study 2. [from PDF pp. 7–8, 10]

Minor exceptions: In Study 2, scalar invariance was marginal for Rationality and Dutifulness by chi-square, but RMSEA confidence intervals overlapped, and the authors retained the invariance interpretation. [from PDF p. 10]

This means the age differences are not artifacts of changing measurement properties across age groups.

**Cross-Domain or Structural Findings**

**The Roberts 5-facet model vs. NEO-PI-R vs. DeYoung aspects**

This paper uses a different facet system than the rest of our crosswalk. Key structural observations:

  - **Impulse Control is the strongest developmental signal in the C domain.** d₁₃ = 0.88 (self-report) and 0.59 (observer). This facet maps primarily to C6 Deliberation / NEO Cautiousness content, which falls in DeYoung's **Orderliness** aspect. It also includes N5 Impulsiveness content — genuinely cross-domain.**- Orderliness (neatness, organization) shows zero developmental change.** This is specifically the neatness/tidiness/perfectionism cluster. In DeYoung's system, this maps to C2 Order, which is the core of the **Orderliness** aspect. If Orderliness-as-neatness doesn't change but Impulse Control (C6 Deliberation, also Orderliness-aspect) increases dramatically, then the two facets within DeYoung's Orderliness aspect are developing completely differently.**- Industriousness increases early, then plateaus.** Maps to C4 Achievement Striving and C5 Self-Discipline in the NEO system, which are DeYoung's **Industriousness** aspect. The curvilinear pattern (rise, then plateau or slight decline after 80) is consistent with Terracciano et al.'s finding that C5 Self-Discipline declines in late life.**- Reliability is cross-domain (C/A boundary).** The authors state it correlates about equally with C and A. [from PDF p. 5] This is consistent with A3 Altruism and A5 Compliance cross-loading on C in some factor analyses, and with DeYoung's Politeness aspect sharing variance with Conscientiousness.**- Conventionality is cross-domain (C/O boundary).** Only increases in old age. Content is about adherence to social norms and tradition. The authors note it was "often thought to belong to the domain of openness to experience." [from PDF p. 5] This maps loosely to reverse-O6 Values, or to the C/O boundary region that several factor analytic studies have identified.**Self-Report vs. Observer Discrepancy**

The self/observer discrepancy is the most novel finding. Self-reports detect industriousness changes in young→middle adulthood; observers don't see any C-facet changes until middle→older adulthood. This has implications for how we weight self-report vs. observer-report developmental data in the crosswalk. The two methods may be tracking different things: internal shifts in self-concept (self-report) vs. observable behavioral change (observer report).

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. C6 Deliberation (Impulse Control) vs. C2 Order — within-Orderliness aspect divergence**- **Facets involved:** Roberts' Impulse Control (≈ C6 Deliberation) vs. Roberts' Orderliness (≈ C2 Order)
  - **Currently assigned to:** Both map to DeYoung's Orderliness aspect
  - **Evidence:** Impulse Control shows d₁₃ = 0.88 (self) and 0.59 (observer). Orderliness shows d₁₃ = 0.07 (self) and 0.24 (observer). These are on completely different developmental trajectories. Impulse Control increases monotonically; Orderliness is flat.
  - **Strength:** **Strong.** Replicated across two studies, two methods (self and observer), and multiple ethnic/SES subgroups. The gap is enormous — nearly a full SD difference in cumulative effect size.
  - **Caveat:** The Roberts Impulse Control facet includes N5 Impulsiveness content, not just C6 Deliberation. So this dissociation may partly reflect cross-domain variance (N vs. C), not purely within-Orderliness divergence. Still, the Orderliness-as-neatness flatness is clean.

**2. Industriousness vs. Impulse Control — different developmental timing**- **Facets involved:** Roberts' Industriousness (≈ C4/C5) vs. Roberts' Impulse Control (≈ C6 + N5)
  - **Currently assigned to:** Industriousness → DeYoung's Industriousness; Impulse Control → mostly DeYoung's Orderliness
  - **Evidence:** Industriousness increases young→middle (d₁₂ = 0.42) then plateaus. Impulse Control increases monotonically across the full lifespan (d₁₃ = 0.88). These are cross-aspect dissociations, which is *consistent* with the aspect model (different aspects, different trajectories). But the magnitude difference is notable — Impulse Control changes far more than Industriousness.
  - **Strength:** **Strong** as a cross-aspect dissociation. This actually *supports* the aspect distinction rather than challenging it.

**3. Reliability — C/A boundary construct**- **Currently assigned to:** Conscientiousness domain (in Roberts' system)
  - **Evidence:** Increases across the full lifespan (d₁₃ = 0.79 self, 0.58 observer). But the authors note it correlates about equally with C and A. [from PDF p. 5] This is not a clean C facet.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive** for crosswalk purposes. If Reliability is partly Agreeableness content, its developmental trajectory may be driven by A-domain maturation rather than C-domain maturation. We cannot disentangle this with these data alone.

**4. Conventionality — C/O boundary construct, late-onset only**- **Currently assigned to:** Conscientiousness domain (in Roberts' system); content overlaps with reverse-Openness
  - **Evidence:** No change young→middle; increases only in older adulthood (d₂₃ = 0.38 self, 0.60 observer). Unique trajectory among C facets. [from PDF Table 3, Table 4]
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** The late-onset pattern is consistent with this being an Openness-domain phenomenon (cognitive rigidity increasing with age) rather than a Conscientiousness phenomenon. But this paper cannot test that because it doesn't measure O.

**Notes**

**Relation to other crosswalk papers**- **Terracciano, McCrae, Brant & Costa (2005):** The present paper's finding that Impulse Control increases monotonically while Orderliness-as-neatness is flat is broadly consistent with Terracciano's finding that C6 Deliberation increases while C2 Order shows modest change. However, Terracciano used the NEO-PI-R at facet level longitudinally, which is stronger evidence. The Industriousness plateau/decline pattern here is also consistent with Terracciano's finding that C5 Self-Discipline declines in later life.
  - **Ringwald et al. (2024):** That paper found C5 Self-Discipline declining while C6 Deliberation increased — same cross-aspect dissociation.
  - **Jang, Livesley & Vernon (1996) / Luciano et al. (2006):** The heritability evidence is relevant here: if C6 Deliberation and C2 Order have different heritabilities *and* different developmental trajectories, that's converging evidence that they shouldn't be grouped in the same aspect. But this paper has no heritability data.
  - **Roberts, Walton & Viechtbauer (2006) meta-analysis:** The heterogeneity in the meta-analytic estimates is exactly what this paper was designed to explain. The meta-analysis found overall C increases, but the present paper shows these increases are driven by Impulse Control and Industriousness, not Orderliness.

**Weights for the crosswalk**- **For developmental trajectory evidence:** Moderate weight. Cross-sectional, not longitudinal. But two independent samples, strong measurement invariance, observer replication, and effect sizes with CIs. The Impulse Control vs. Orderliness dissociation is very robust.
  - **For aspect-level structural conclusions:** Lower weight. The facet system doesn't map cleanly to DeYoung's aspects. Impulse Control conflates C6 with N5 content; Reliability conflates C with A; Conventionality conflates C with reverse-O. The mapping is inherently approximate.
  - **Novel contribution:** The self/observer discrepancy is unique and important. No other paper in the crosswalk has observer-rated developmental data across this age range.

**Open questions raised**

  - Is the Orderliness-as-neatness developmental flatness specific to neatness/organization content, or does it extend to C3 Dutifulness (which Roberts' system splits between Orderliness and Reliability)? This paper can't tell us because the facet boundaries are different.
  - Does the self/observer timing discrepancy replicate with NEO-PI-R facets? If self-reports detect C4/C5 changes in young adulthood but observers don't see them until middle age, that would have implications for which evidence stream we trust for early-adult developmental trajectories.
  - The Conventionality late-onset pattern: is this C or reverse-O? This paper can't answer it, but a paper with both C and O facets measured (e.g., Terracciano) could.

**Ashton & Lee (2016)**

**Citation**

Ashton, M. C., & Lee, K. (2016). Age trends in HEXACO-PI-R self-reports. *Journal of Research in Personality, 64*, 102–111. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jrp.2016.08.008

**Design**- **Type:** Cross-sectional
  - **N:** 97,583 (after screening from ~100,000; sex-balanced: 50.9% male, 49.1% female)
  - **Age range:** 14–74 (restricted to ages with ≥100 respondents per year)
  - **Instrument(s):** HEXACO-PI-R 100-item version (25 facet scales, 4 items each, plus Altruism interstitial facet)
  - **Assessment interval:** N/A (cross-sectional)
  - **Key limitations:**- **Cross-sectional only.** Cannot distinguish developmental change from cohort effects. Authors argue persuasively (by reductio on the cohort-effect interpretation) that developmental change is the more plausible explanation, and note prior work showing cross-sectional and longitudinal trends broadly converge [from PDF p. 9–10]. Still, this is not longitudinal data.
    - **Self-selected online sample.** Participants found hexaco.org via search or links and completed the inventory for feedback. Likely above-average Openness. Possible (though speculative) differential participation by age × trait interactions [from PDF p. 8–9].
    - **HEXACO, not NEO.** Facets do not map 1:1 onto NEO-PI-R facets or BFAS aspects. Mapping to the crosswalk requires inference. I provide approximate mappings below but flag them clearly as inferences.
    - **No precise numerical statistics reported for age trends.** All findings are described as approximate z-score differences read from figures (e.g., "about one full standard deviation unit"). No regression coefficients, no confidence intervals, no slope estimates. This limits the precision with which findings can be entered into the master crosswalk table.
    - **Figures are small and crowded.** Z-score estimates I report below are approximations from the text descriptions and my reading of the figures. Where I'm uncertain, I say so.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level** — 25 HEXACO-PI-R facets across 6 domains, plus 1 interstitial facet (Altruism). Item-level age trends are discussed qualitatively for selected items but not reported systematically.

Core Findings**

All age trends below are approximate z-score differences read from text descriptions and Figures 1–6 [from PDF pp. 4–6]. The paper does not report regression coefficients or effect sizes — only graphical patterns and approximate SD-unit differences stated in prose.

**Key detail:** The upward age trend was steeper for men than women (~25% steeper). Sex differences in H were large among 18-year-olds (~0.5 SD, women higher) but smaller among 60–70-year-olds (~0.25 SD). Men in their 60s–70s scored >0.6 SD higher than 18-year-old women. [from PDF p. 6]

**Within-domain heterogeneity:** Essentially none. All four H facets move in lockstep. This is the most internally coherent domain in the paper. [from PDF p. 7]

**Within-domain heterogeneity:** **Strong.** Social Self-Esteem, Social Boldness, and Liveliness all rise; Sociability falls then plateaus. This parallels Big Five findings (Roberts et al., 2006; Soto & John, 2012) where "Social Dominance" rises and "Social Vitality" falls. [from PDF pp. 5, 6–7]

**Within-domain heterogeneity:** **Moderate.** Three facets track together (U-shaped, weak); Flexibility goes its own way (monotonic increase). [from PDF p. 5]

**Critical crosswalk note:** The near-absence of overall age trends in HEXACO-A, combined with the strong upward trend in Big Five A found elsewhere, tells us something important. The paper explains: Big Five A's upward trend is driven primarily by the Altruism interstitial facet (which shares variance with H, Emotionality, and A in HEXACO) and by H variance that leaks into Big Five A measures. The anger-control/even-temper content that defines HEXACO-A shows almost no net upward age trend. [from PDF p. 7]

This means: **the developmental trajectory of "Agreeableness" depends entirely on what content you put in it.** If your A measure is heavy on prosocial warmth and trust (like NEO A), you see upward trends. If it's primarily anger-control and tolerance (like HEXACO A), you see almost nothing. This is directly relevant to the Compassion vs. Politeness aspect distinction.

**Within-domain heterogeneity:** **Moderate-to-strong.** The teen-years upward trend is shared. But after the mid-20s, Prudence continues climbing steeply, Organization and Diligence climb slowly, and Perfectionism flatlines. This parallels Big Five findings (Jackson et al., 2009) where Industriousness-type content rises more than Orderliness-type content — except here, Prudence (≈ Deliberation, arguably Industriousness-adjacent) shows the steepest rise, while Perfectionism (Orderliness-adjacent) flatlines. [from PDF pp. 5–6]

**Within-domain heterogeneity:** **Strong.** Aesthetic Appreciation and Inquisitiveness rise through adulthood; Unconventionality falls; Creativity is flat. The Openness domain masks completely opposed facet trajectories. [from PDF p. 6]

**Crosswalk relevance:** Inquisitiveness (≈ Intellect aspect) and Aesthetic Appreciation (≈ Openness aspect) both rise, but Unconventionality (≈ Openness aspect) falls. So the two putative Openness-aspect facets (Aesthetic Appreciation, Unconventionality) go in opposite directions. This would be a dissociation within the Openness aspect if the HEXACO→BFAS mapping holds. But I'm uncertain about mapping Unconventionality — it may blend O6 Values with novelty-seeking content that doesn't sit cleanly in either aspect.
