        This is the facet most closely aligned with Big Five Agreeableness [from PDF p. 7]

**Item-level findings (qualitative)**

The paper reports that items within the same facet sometimes show opposite age trends. Three examples given [from PDF p. 6]:

  - **Fearfulness items:** "Don't mind dangerous work" shows upward trend (less concern with age); "wouldn't panic in emergency" shows downward trend (more calm with age). Same facet, opposite item-level trends.
  - **Sociability items:** "Enjoy having lots of people around" shows downward trend; "avoid small talk" shows upward trend (reverse-keyed, so older people engage *more* in small talk). Could reflect genuine within-facet heterogeneity or item desirability confounds.
  - **Diligence items:** "Push myself hard for goals" shows downward trend; "do only minimum work" shows upward trend (reverse-keyed, so older people do more than minimum). Again, could be substantive or desirability-driven.

**Desirability confound:** The paper constructs ad hoc scales of the most- and least-desirable item per facet. Undesirable items showed a steeper downward trend (~1.2 SD, teens→70s) than desirable items showed an upward trend (~0.7 SD). This asymmetry suggests a response style confound: older adults may endorse undesirable statements less, beyond true trait change. [from PDF pp. 6–7]

**Cross-domain findings**

  - HEXACO Agreeableness correlated .42 with Honesty-Humility at the scale level [from PDF p. 3]. Despite this correlation, their developmental trajectories are strikingly different: H shows a ~1.0 SD upward trend, while A is nearly flat. This is strong evidence that H and A, despite their overlap, are developmentally distinct constructs.
  - The paper computes a HEXACO-derived Neuroticism composite (using the formula from Lee & Ashton, 2013) and finds persons in their 70s average >0.75 SD lower than teens. This is broadly consistent with Big Five N findings. [from PDF p. 7]
  - Standard deviations were smaller for older participants (avg SD = 1.07 for teens, 0.85 for 70s across all six components). Not clearly attributable to response range restriction alone. [from PDF p. 9, footnote 8]

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Emotionality: Sentimentality vs. Anxiety (opposite developmental trajectories)**- **HEXACO facet:** Sentimentality
  - **Assigned to:** Emotionality domain
  - **Nearest NEO mapping:** A6 Tender-Mindedness (Compassion aspect of Agreeableness)
  - **Evidence:** Sentimentality increases ~0.33 SD from teens to 60, while Anxiety (same domain) decreases ~0.5 SD from 20s to 70s, and Fearfulness is flat. Sentimentality's upward trend is more consistent with the Altruism/prosocial trend than with the Neuroticism-decline trend.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The developmental divergence is clear, but this is expected from the HEXACO model's own description of Emotionality as a rotated factor. The crosswalk implication is for NEO A6 Tender-Mindedness: if it shares developmental trajectory with Sentimentality (upward) rather than with N-facets (downward or flat), that supports its placement on the Compassion aspect of Agreeableness rather than as a Neuroticism-adjacent facet. Consistent with DeYoung (2007) placement.

**2. Extraversion: Sociability vs. Social Boldness/Liveliness/Social Self-Esteem (opposite trajectories)**- **HEXACO facets:** Sociability vs. Social Boldness, Liveliness, Social Self-Esteem
  - **Nearest NEO mapping:** E2 Gregariousness (Enthusiasm) vs. E3 Assertiveness (Assertiveness) + E4 Activity + E6 Positive Emotions
  - **Evidence:** Sociability declines ~0.25 SD from late teens to 40, while the other three facets rise 0.4–0.8 SD across adulthood. This maps onto the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness aspect split: the Enthusiasm-aligned content (gregariousness, sociability) goes down while the Assertiveness-aligned content (boldness, confidence) goes up.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate-to-strong** as corroboration. The pattern directly parallels Roberts et al.'s (2006) Social Vitality (down) vs. Social Dominance (up) distinction, and is consistent with the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness aspect split. This paper can't tell us about NEO E1 Warmth or E5 Excitement-Seeking, but it strengthens the case that the E domain splits along the aspect boundary developmentally.

**3. Conscientiousness: Perfectionism vs. Prudence/Organization/Diligence (flat vs. continued rise)**- **HEXACO facets:** Perfectionism vs. Prudence, Organization, Diligence
  - **Nearest NEO mapping:** Perfectionism ≈ C2 Order blend (Orderliness); Prudence ≈ C6 Deliberation (Industriousness per DeYoung 2007); Organization ≈ C2 Order (Orderliness); Diligence ≈ C4 Achievement Striving (Industriousness)
  - **Evidence:** After the shared teen-years increase, Perfectionism flatlines while the other three continue rising (Prudence most steeply, ~0.4 SD; Organization and Diligence ~0.2 SD).
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** This doesn't cleanly map onto the Industriousness/Orderliness split. Organization (Orderliness-adjacent) continues rising, while Perfectionism (also Orderliness-adjacent) flatlines. The dissociation is more within-Orderliness than between-aspects. Prudence's steep rise is interesting because C6 Deliberation loads on Industriousness in DeYoung (2007), and this paper's Prudence content is about cautiousness/self-control — arguably more Orderliness-like in content despite the factor loading.

**4. Openness: Unconventionality vs. Aesthetic Appreciation/Inquisitiveness (opposite trajectories)**- **HEXACO facets:** Unconventionality vs. Aesthetic Appreciation, Inquisitiveness
  - **Nearest NEO mapping:** Unconventionality ≈ O6 Values (Openness aspect); Aesthetic Appreciation ≈ O2 Aesthetics (Openness aspect); Inquisitiveness ≈ O5 Ideas (Intellect aspect)
  - **Evidence:** Unconventionality declines ~0.3 SD from mid-20s to 70; Aesthetic Appreciation and Inquisitiveness each rise ~0.3 SD over the same span.
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The interesting wrinkle: both Unconventionality and Aesthetic Appreciation would map to the Openness aspect (not Intellect), yet they go in opposite directions. If this mapping is right, it's a dissociation *within* the Openness aspect. But the Intellect-aligned content (Inquisitiveness) also rises, matching Aesthetic Appreciation. So the split isn't Intellect-vs-Openness; it's Unconventionality-vs-everything-else.

**5. Agreeableness (HEXACO): Flexibility vs. Forgivingness/Gentleness/Patience (different trajectory shape)**- **HEXACO facets:** Flexibility vs. Forgivingness, Gentleness, Patience
  - **Nearest NEO mapping:** Flexibility ≈ A4 Compliance (Politeness); Forgivingness/Patience ≈ reversed N2 Angry Hostility (Volatility, reversed)
  - **Evidence:** Flexibility shows a monotonic increase of ~0.5 SD (teens→60). The other three show a U-shape: down ~0.2 SD to age 40, then up ~0.25 SD to 70.
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive.** This is interesting because Forgivingness and Patience (anger-control content → Volatility-reversed) track together, while Flexibility (compliance/accommodation → Politeness) diverges. If HEXACO-A's facets correspond to a Volatility-control vs. Politeness-compliance distinction, this is a dissociation flag. But the mapping is loose enough that I wouldn't push hard on this.

**6. Honesty-Humility vs. HEXACO Agreeableness: sharply different trajectories despite r = .42**- **Not a within-domain dissociation** but a critical cross-domain structural finding for the crosswalk.
  - **Evidence:** H rises ~1.0 SD (18→60s). HEXACO-A is essentially flat. Yet these dimensions share substantial variance and both contribute to Big Five A.
  - **Implication for the crosswalk:** The "Agreeableness" of Big Five/NEO measures blends content with very different developmental trajectories. To the extent that NEO A facets capture H-type variance (especially A2 Straightforwardness, A5 Modesty, possibly A1 Trust), those facets should show steeper upward trends than facets capturing HEXACO-A-type variance (anger control, forgiveness). This is testable against the Terracciano et al. (2005) and Soto & John (2012) data.
  - **Strength:** **Strong** — the N is enormous, the pattern is consistent across all H facets and items, and the contrast with HEXACO-A is striking.

**Notes**- **This paper's main value for the crosswalk is not the specific HEXACO numbers** (which are approximate and from a different instrument), **but the structural insight:** within-domain facet heterogeneity in developmental trajectories is the norm, not the exception. Every HEXACO domain except Honesty-Humility shows at least one facet going in a clearly different direction from its domain-mates. This is strong evidence that domain-level developmental findings are misleading and that facet- or aspect-level analysis is necessary.**- The Honesty-Humility finding is the headline.** A ~1.0 SD upward trend from 18 to 60, consistent across all facets and all items, with steeper trends for men — this is the largest personality age trend ever documented at this scale. The authors argue (credibly) that Big Five studies missed this because HH variance is distributed across multiple Big Five dimensions, diluting the signal. For the crosswalk, this means any NEO facet that captures HH-type variance should show a distinctive upward developmental trajectory. The main candidates are A2 Straightforwardness, A5 Modesty, and to a lesser extent A1 Trust. If those facets show steeper age increases than their aspect-mates (A3 Altruism, A6 Tender-Mindedness for Compassion; A4 Compliance for Politeness), that would be an HH-driven dissociation within Big Five A.**- The desirability confound is a real concern.** The asymmetry between desirable-item and undesirable-item age trends (0.7 SD vs. 1.2 SD) suggests some portion of age trends — especially for HH, which has the strongest trends — may reflect older adults' greater reluctance to endorse undesirable self-descriptions rather than true trait change. The paper acknowledges this but cannot resolve it with cross-sectional self-report data. This is a caveat on all developmental findings, not just this paper's.**- Connection to Brandt et al. (2023) extraction.** That paper (if extracted) showed Conscientiousness facets differentially changing, with N2 Angry Hostility and N5 Impulsiveness (Volatility) decreasing while N1 Anxiety and N6 Vulnerability (Withdrawal) showed different patterns. The present paper's finding that HEXACO Anxiety (≈ N1/N6) declines while Fearfulness is flat is broadly consistent, though the HEXACO Emotionality domain is rotated relative to Big Five N.**- The item-level divergence finding is an important methodological warning.** Items within the same facet can show opposite age trends. This means facet-level analysis, while better than domain-level, may still conceal item-level heterogeneity. The crosswalk should treat facet-level findings as the best available granularity for most purposes, but acknowledge that items within a facet are not interchangeable developmental units.**Haehner, Krämer, Schaefer, Hopwood & Bleidorn (2025)**

**Citation**

Haehner, P., Krämer, M. D., Schaefer, B., Hopwood, C. J., & Bleidorn, W. (2025). Stability and change in lower-order personality traits in a representative Swiss sample. *European Journal of Personality*. https://doi.org/10.1177/08902070251405544

**Design**- **Type:** Longitudinal panel study (5 waves, 6-month intervals, 2-year span)
  - **N:** 4,495 at W1 (retention: W2 = 2,461; W3 = 2,552; W4 = 2,086; W5 = 2,355)
  - **Age range:** 18–85+ (M = 50.06, SD = 19.89 at W1)
  - **Instrument(s):** Big Five Aspect Scales – Short Form (BFAS-S; 40 items, 4 items per aspect); Satisfaction With Life Scale; Rosenberg Self-Esteem Scale
  - **Assessment interval:** 6 months between waves
  - **Key limitations:**- **BFAS-S only — no NEO facets.** This paper measures at the aspect level directly (10 aspects, 4 items each), not at the NEO facet level. We cannot map NEO facets to aspects from this data.
    - **Short form reliability is marginal for some aspects.** ω ranges from .51 to .76 for aspects (politeness, openness, and stability appear to be at the low end based on the validation paper they cite). The latent variable modeling compensates somewhat but doesn't fix conceptually weak scales.
    - **Only 2-year window.** Short for capturing developmental trajectories. The paper partially addresses this by using cross-sectional age moderation (LSEM) to examine age-graded intercept differences, but within-person slopes are estimated over only 2 years.
    - **Swiss sample only.** Western, wealthy, democratic. Authors acknowledge this limits generalizability.
    - **Self-report only.** No informant data.
    - **Attrition is differential but small** (younger, less agreeable, less emotionally stable, less open participants dropped out; all |d| ≤ .19).
    - **Correlated change CIs are extremely broad** — the authors themselves flag this. Many 99% CIs span Δr ≥ .50. These estimates should be treated with caution.

**Granularity**

**Aspect-level** (BFAS 10 aspects): Compassion, Politeness, Industriousness, Orderliness, Assertiveness, Enthusiasm, Stability, Resilience, Intellect, Openness. Also reports Big Five domain-level summaries.

**No facet-level or item-level data.** The BFAS-S has no facet subscales — each aspect is defined by 4 items, with no further decomposition reported.

**Missed opportunity?** No — the instrument simply doesn't have a facet level. The BFAS was designed as a domain → aspect → item hierarchy with no intermediate facet level. This is a structural limitation of the instrument, not an analytic choice.

        [from Figure 4, PDF p. 56; from discussion p. 25]

        Industriousness: rising intercepts + declining slopes. Orderliness: stable intercepts + rising slopes. This is one of the clearest within-domain dissociations in the paper.

        Correlated change (Industriousness ↔ Orderliness)

**Cross-domain and structural findings**

**Rank-order stability:** Average 1-year latent test-retest correlations were r = .88 for domains and r = .87 for aspects — essentially identical. [from Table 2, PDF p. 19] This is notably higher than the meta-analytic r = .60–.75 reported by Bleidorn et al. (2022), which the authors attribute to using latent (measurement-error-corrected) rather than manifest variables. Both domains and aspects were more rank-order stable than life satisfaction (r = .81) and self-esteem (r = .83). [from Table 2]

**Cumulative mean-level change:** The most dynamic aspects were Industriousness (M_cum = 5.90) and Enthusiasm (M_cum = 5.12). The least dynamic were Openness (M_cum = 0.22) and Self-esteem (M_cum = 1.12). On average, Big Five domains (M_cum = 2.02) and aspects (M_cum = 2.70) showed comparable cumulative change to the comparison constructs (M_cum = 1.55). [from Table 4]

**Correlated changes — between-domain:** Average r = .33 across Big Five domain pairs. Range: r = −.17 (O/I ↔ Emotional Stability) to r = .58 (Extraversion ↔ O/I). [from Table 5] CIs are broad.

**Correlated changes — within-domain aspect pairs:** Average r = .42. Range: r = .20 (Compassion ↔ Politeness) to r = .51 (Stability ↔ Resilience). [from Table 6] Within-domain correlated changes were NOT consistently stronger than cross-domain correlated changes. For example, Enthusiasm ↔ Politeness r = .78, which exceeds all within-domain pairs. [from Table 6]

**Key structural implication from correlated changes:** The authors explicitly note that correlated changes did not align with metatrait structure (Stability/Plasticity). They state this "question[s] the validity and assumed biological basis of these meta-trait domains." [from discussion, PDF p. 27] Similarly, within-domain aspect correlated changes were not consistently stronger than cross-domain, "raising questions about the 'longitudinal structure' of personality traits." [from discussion, PDF p. 27]

**However:** The CIs on virtually all correlated change estimates are enormous. The authors themselves acknowledge that even with N ≈ 4,500 and 5 waves, correlated change is estimated imprecisely. They note that "existing research may also have suffered from an imprecise assessment of correlated changes." [from discussion, PDF p. 28] This is an important methodological warning for the entire correlated-change literature.

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. Conscientiousness: Industriousness vs. Orderliness — opposing mean-level patterns**- **Aspects:** Industriousness and Orderliness
  - **Published structure:** Both are aspects of Conscientiousness (DeYoung et al., 2007)
  - **Evidence:** Industriousness shows strong intercept increases age 20–60 paired with within-person decreases. Orderliness shows stable intercepts paired with within-person increases. Opposite developmental dynamics. Cumulative change also diverges (5.90 vs. 2.60). [from Table 4, Figure 4]
  - **Strength:** **Strong** for developmental dissociation. This does NOT suggest misassignment — both aspects clearly belong under Conscientiousness structurally. But it demonstrates that the two aspects are driven by different developmental mechanisms. The authors speculate: cohort effects may explain Industriousness intercept differences, while work-related social roles may drive Orderliness within-person increases. [from discussion, PDF pp. 25–26]

**2. Emotional Stability: Resilience vs. Stability — asymmetric maturation**- **Aspects:** Resilience and Stability
  - **Published structure:** Both are aspects of Emotional Stability / low Neuroticism
  - **Evidence:** Resilience shows the largest intercept age-gradient of any trait (SD = 0.224), with dramatic increases from 20 to 60. Stability shows a much flatter intercept trajectory (SD = 0.067). [from Table 4, Figure 4]
  - **Strength:** **Strong.** The classic "emotional maturation" finding appears to be primarily a Resilience phenomenon. Stability (low volatility, emotional evenness) does not show the same maturation pattern. This is consistent with the Withdrawal/Volatility dissociation seen in other papers — different developmental mechanisms act on the two halves of Neuroticism.

**3. Extraversion: Enthusiasm vs. Assertiveness — opposite intercept directions**- **Aspects:** Enthusiasm and Assertiveness
  - **Published structure:** Both are aspects of Extraversion
  - **Evidence:** Enthusiasm intercepts decrease with age. Assertiveness intercepts increase from 20 to 60. Cumulative change differs nearly 4× (Enthusiasm: 5.12 vs. Assertiveness: 1.28). [from Table 4, Figure 4]
  - **Strength:** **Strong** for developmental dissociation. This replicates the Social Vitality / Social Dominance divergence from Roberts et al. (2006) meta-analysis using the BFAS labels directly.

**4. Openness/Intellect: Intellect vs. Openness — dynamic vs. inert**- **Aspects:** Intellect and Openness
  - **Published structure:** Both are aspects of Openness/Intellect
  - **Evidence:** Intellect shows substantial intercept changes (SD = 0.102) and cumulative change (M_cum = 3.25). Openness shows minimal intercept changes (SD = 0.057) and near-zero cumulative change (M_cum = 0.22 — lowest of any trait). [from Table 4]
  - **Strength:** **Strong** for developmental dissociation. **But:** Openness has the lowest reliability and lowest rank-order stability of any aspect. Some of its apparent "inertness" may reflect measurement noise rather than true stability. With only 4 items and ω likely ~.55, there may not be enough reliable variance to detect change.

**5. Agreeableness: Compassion vs. Politeness — diverging intercepts**- **Aspects:** Compassion and Politeness
  - **Published structure:** Both are aspects of Agreeableness
  - **Evidence:** Compassion intercepts decline from ~40 onward. Politeness intercepts show slight increases. [from Figure 4]
  - **Strength:** **Moderate.** The divergence is visible in Figure 4 but the effect sizes are smaller than for the other domains. Correlated change (r = .20) is the weakest of any within-domain pair, though the CI [−.47, .86] renders this uninformative.

**6. Correlated changes do not respect domain or metatrait boundaries**- **Evidence:** Within-domain aspect correlated changes (r_avg = .42) were not consistently stronger than cross-domain aspect correlated changes. Enthusiasm ↔ Politeness (r = .78) exceeded all within-domain pairs. The Stability/Plasticity metatrait structure was not supported by discriminant co-developmental patterns. [from Table 6, discussion p. 27]
  - **Strength:** **Suggestive** — but heavily caveated by the enormous confidence intervals. The authors themselves describe these results as imprecise. This finding should not be weighted heavily until replicated with more measurement occasions.

**Notes**

**Relationship to the crosswalk project:** This paper is moderately useful. It provides the first large-scale, representative, longitudinal examination of all 10 BFAS aspects. The within-domain divergence findings (especially for C, E, and Emotional Stability) are directly relevant and converge with the facet-level findings from Terracciano et al. (2005), Roberts et al. (2006), Ringwald et al. (2024), and Soto (2016). However, because it uses the BFAS-S (not the NEO-PI-R), it cannot tell us which specific NEO facets drive these aspect-level patterns. It confirms the aspect-level dissociations but cannot resolve facet-to-aspect assignment questions.

**The intercept vs. slope distinction matters.** This paper carefully distinguishes between cross-sectional age differences in *baseline levels* (intercepts) and within-person *change over the study period* (slopes). For most traits, the intercepts showed significant age moderation but the slopes did not. The authors note that intercept differences and slope differences sometimes went in opposite directions (e.g., Industriousness: intercepts increase with age, but within-person slopes are negative). This could reflect cohort effects, initial-elevation bias, or genuine age-vs-period dissociations. For the crosswalk, we should primarily attend to the intercept trajectories as estimates of developmental change, while noting that within-person slopes over 2 years were largely nonsignificant.

**The correlated change results are honestly reported but functionally uninformative.** The authors deserve credit for reporting 99% CIs and explicitly stating that most estimates are imprecise. This is a useful warning for interpreting correlated change findings from smaller studies that may not have reported CIs.

**Convergence with prior extractions:**

  - The Enthusiasm/Assertiveness divergence converges with the Social Vitality/Social Dominance split from Roberts et al. (2006) and the Terracciano et al. (2005) finding that E1 Warmth, E6 Positive Emotions decline while E3 Assertiveness increases.
  - The Industriousness/Orderliness divergence converges with Conscientiousness facet divergences in Terracciano et al. (2005) and Soto (2016).
  - The Resilience/Stability divergence converges with the Withdrawal/Volatility dissociation seen in Ringwald et al. (2024).
  - The Intellect/Openness divergence converges with the stability of O5 Ideas vs. declining O-facets in Terracciano et al. (2005), though note the measurement concerns for the BFAS-S Openness scale.

**What this paper does NOT tell us:** Whether any NEO facet is misassigned to the wrong aspect. It confirms that aspects within domains behave differently, which is the premise of the crosswalk — but it operates at the level where the crosswalk's dependent variables live (aspects), not at the level where its independent variables live (facets/items).

**Brandes, Kushner, Herzhoff & Tackett (2020)**

**Citation**

Brandes, C. M., Kushner, S. C., Herzhoff, K., & Tackett, J. L. (2020). Facet-level personality development in the transition to adolescence: Maturity, disruption, and gender differences. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*. (In press at time of preprint; DOI not available from this version.)

**Design**- **Type:** Cohort-sequential longitudinal (4 annual waves)
  - **N:** 440 children (50.2% girls); wave-level Ns: W1 = 328, W2 = 237, W3 = 355, W4 = 310. An additional 93 families were recruited at W3 to offset attrition.
  - **Age range:** 9–13 years (M_W1 = 9.97, SD = 0.81; M_W4 = 13.11, SD = 0.84). Data binned into five 1-year age groups (9, 10, 11, 12, 13) for cohort-sequential analysis.
  - **Instrument(s):** Inventory of Children's Individual Differences – Short Form (ICID-S; Deal et al., 2007). 50 items, mother-report, 7-point scale. 5 domains, 15 facets. **This is NOT the NEO-PI-R.** Facets are empirically derived for children and do not map 1:1 onto NEO facets.
  - **Assessment interval:** ~1 year between waves (M_T1-T2 = 1.18 yrs; M_T2-T3 = 0.94 yrs; M_T3-T4 = 0.97 yrs). Total span T1–T4: M = 3.14 years.
  - **Informant:** Mothers only. No self-report, no father report across all waves.
  - **Key limitations:**- **Instrument mismatch with our crosswalk.** The ICID-S facets are not NEO facets. Mapping to the master crosswalk NEO rows requires conceptual bridging, not direct slotting. Some ICID facets have no NEO equivalent; some NEO facets have no ICID equivalent.
    - **Mother-report only.** Parent reports may capture more stable personality variance (higher stability estimates) and may be less sensitive to short-term fluctuations than self-report. Authors acknowledge this and note prior evidence that parent vs. self stability doesn't differ much except for Neuroticism.
    - **Age range is preadolescent (9–13).** Developmental processes here (puberty onset, school transitions) differ from the adult samples in our other extractions. Maturity/disruption patterns may not generalize to adult developmental trajectories.
    - **Predominantly Caucasian (70%), high-SES Canadian sample.** 87% of parents had postsecondary degrees; 35% reported household income >$100K CAD. Not representative.
    - **Short form.** 50 items across 15 facets = ~3–4 items per facet. Internal consistency was adequate (.73–.90 at facet level), but measurement precision is lower than the NEO-PI-R's 8-item facets.
    - **Some latent growth models failed to converge** (negative residual variances), preventing reliable gender-separated estimates for Negative Affect (girls), Distractible (girls), and Strong Willed (girls). Descriptive statistics were reported instead — these should be interpreted cautiously.

**Granularity**

**Facet-level.** 15 ICID-S facets reported, plus 5 domains. No item-level data. No aspect-level analysis (the ICID does not have an aspect layer).

**Core Findings**

**Measurement Invariance**

[from PDF pages 20–21]

Across time/cohort: 6 facets achieved strict invariance, 2 scalar, 4 metric, 3 configural only. The three configural-only facets were **Negative Affect, Considerate, and Activity Level** — results for these should be interpreted with extra caution. The paper flags these with superscript "a" in Table 3.

**Rank-Order Stability**

[from PDF pages 21–23, Table 2]

**Overall:** Stability was higher than expected. T1–T4 domain stability: r = .63 (A) to .72 (E). T1–T4 facet stability: r = .57 (Antagonism) to .72 (Sociable). Year-to-year stabilities were substantially higher.

**Within-domain stability differences:** Only Extraversion showed significant within-domain differences. Sociable (r = .72) was significantly more stable than Positive Emotions (r = .62, z = 2.10, p = .035) and Considerate (r = .61, z = 2.25, p = .024). Other domains showed no significant within-domain stability differences.

**Gender differences in stability:** Girls were significantly more stable than boys on Organized (r = .72 vs .51, z = 2.54, p = .011) and Achievement Orientation (r = .68 vs .46, z = 2.44, p = .015), but NOT on Distractible (r = .63 vs .60, ns). This is a within-C dissociation: C2-like and C4-like facets show gender-differentiated stability, but C5-like does not.

**Domain: Neuroticism**

**Within-domain heterogeneity: STRONG.** Three facets declined (Activity Level, Sociable, Positive Emotions) while one increased (Considerate). The authors explicitly note that Considerate's trajectory was "much more similar to those of Agreeableness facets than to those of other Extraversion facets" [from PDF page 42]. This is the most structurally interesting finding in the paper for the crosswalk.

**Activity Level as the extreme mover:** E4 Activity showed the steepest decline of any E facet, replicating a pattern seen across multiple studies (De Fruyt et al., 2006; Soto et al., 2011; cf. Terracciano et al., 2005 and Brandt et al., 2023 in adult samples). This is now consistent across child AND adult developmental literatures.

**Within-domain heterogeneity:** Moderate. All three facets trended in the same direction (maturity), but the effect was entirely driven by girls. **Boys showed zero change on all three C facets.** This is a complete gender × domain interaction. Within girls, facets were largely consistent (all increasing), though Achievement Orientation showed the largest effect.

**Gender difference in C stability is notable:** Girls were more stable on Organized (r = .72 vs .51) and Achievement Orientation (r = .68 vs .46) but NOT on Distractible. Combined with the mean-level finding that girls increased on all C facets while boys didn't change, this suggests girls' C development in preadolescence is both more consistent (higher stability) and more maturational (mean increases). The divergence of Distractible from the other two C facets in the stability data is a minor within-C dissociation.

**Within-domain heterogeneity: STRONG.** Complete dissociation: Intellect was flat while Openness declined substantially. This is one of the most replicated facet-level findings in the child developmental literature — creativity/imagination facets decline while intellect/ideas facets do not (also found in De Fruyt et al., 2006; Prinzie & Deković, 2008; Soto et al., 2011). The O5 Ideas vs. O1/O3/O4 split is robust across instruments and age groups.

**Cross-domain or structural findings**

**Considerate as a misplaced facet.** The paper's most structurally interesting finding is that ICID Considerate (scored under Extraversion) showed developmental trajectories aligned with Agreeableness, not Extraversion. The authors devote substantial discussion to this [from PDF pages 41–42], noting that in child personality structure, prosocial tendencies often load on E rather than A, but by adolescence, the structure starts to resemble the adult form where prosociality sits under A. This is a structural transition, not just a mean-level finding — it suggests the E/A boundary shifts developmentally.

**Compliant as a boundary facet.** Compliant cross-loads on both A and C in the original ICID validation. Its trajectory tracked A facets more closely than C facets (where boys showed no change), suggesting its developmental behavior is more agreeableness-like in this age range.

**Slope variance patterns.** All E and O facets showed significant individual differences in rate of change. Within N, only Fear did. Within C, Organized and Achievement Orientation did but Distractible did not. Within A, Antagonism did but Strong Willed did not. This suggests that E and O facets have the most variability in developmental trajectories — consistent with E and O being the domains with the most within-domain heterogeneity in multiple evidence streams.

**Intercept-slope correlations.** Five facets showed significant negative correlations between baseline level and rate of change: Fear (r = −.45), Sociable (r = −.34), Considerate (r = −.51), Openness (r = −.35), and domain-level Openness to Experience (r = −.40). All negative: higher starting levels → less change. This is a regression-to-the-mean-like pattern, or possibly a ceiling effect in parent ratings.

**Dissociation Flags**

**1. ICID Considerate (E) behaves developmentally like Agreeableness**- **Currently assigned to:** Extraversion (in ICID scoring)
  - **Evidence:** While three other E facets declined, Considerate increased — matching A-facet trajectories. Authors explicitly note this and discuss structural implications.
  - **Approximate NEO mapping:** No clean NEO-E equivalent. Content (prosocial warmth, sensitivity to others' feelings) maps better onto A1 Trust / A3 Altruism / A6 Tender-Mindedness.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Strong** for the ICID measure. **Not directly transferable** to NEO structure, but conceptually supports the idea that warmth/prosociality content sits ambiguously between E and A in personality structure, especially in child/adolescent samples.
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** This converges with the known problem of NEO A1 Warmth, which was originally an Extraversion facet (Costa & McCrae, 1985) and still cross-loads between E and A in some analyses. If warmth/prosociality migrates from E to A developmentally, this has implications for how we interpret adult A-facet factor loadings.

**2. ICID Intellect vs. Openness dissociation within O domain**- **Facets:** Intellect (≈ O5 Ideas) vs. Openness (≈ O1/O3/O4 blend)
  - **Evidence:** Intellect showed zero change; Openness declined by 3–4 T-scores. Complete directional dissociation.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Strong** — this replicates across multiple instruments (ICID, HiPIC) and multiple studies (De Fruyt et al., 2006; Prinzie & Deković, 2008; this study).
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** Directly relevant to the Intellect/Openness aspect distinction (DeYoung, 2007). Developmental data consistently show these two clusters behave differently. However, note that the ICID "Openness" facet blends O1/O3/O4 content, so we can't distinguish whether the decline is driven by Fantasy (O1), Feelings (O3), or Actions (O4) specifically.

**3. ICID Activity Level shows steepest E decline**- **Facet:** Activity Level (≈ E4 Activity)
  - **Evidence:** Steepest decline among E facets (−3 T overall), replicating pattern found in adult samples (Terracciano et al., 2005; Brandt et al., 2023).
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Moderate** — consistent direction, but the magnitude differences between E facets are small (−3 vs. −1 for Positive Emotions and Sociable). More notable as a replication of the adult pattern than as a standalone finding.
  - **Relevance to crosswalk:** E4 Activity is assigned to the Assertiveness aspect (DeYoung, 2007). Its developmental distinctiveness from E2 Gregariousness (Enthusiasm aspect) is expected if the aspect distinction holds. But its developmental distinctiveness from E3 Assertiveness and E5 Excitement-Seeking (also Assertiveness aspect) cannot be evaluated here because the ICID doesn't measure those constructs.

**4. Gender × Conscientiousness interaction**- **Facets:** All C facets (Organized ≈ C2, Achievement Orientation ≈ C4, Distractible ≈ C5/C6)
  - **Evidence:** Girls increased on all C facets; boys showed zero change on all C facets. Girls were also more rank-order stable on 2 of 3 C facets.
  - **Strength of evidence:** **Strong** for the gender effect. **Not directly a structural dissociation** — all C facets moved in the same direction within each gender. But it suggests that the developmental processes driving C maturation in preadolescence are gender-specific, which could complicate cross-gender structural analyses.

**Notes**

**Value for the crosswalk**This paper is **moderately useful** with an important instrument caveat. The ICID-S is not the NEO-PI-R, and the facet mappings are approximate. The paper cannot be directly slotted into the master crosswalk table's NEO rows without conceptual bridging.

**What it adds:**

  - Independent developmental evidence for the O5 Ideas / O1-O4 dissociation, from a different instrument and age group (children rather than adults).
  - Evidence that E4-like Activity content declines more steeply than other E facets, replicating the adult pattern.
  - The Considerate finding, which speaks to the broader E/A warmth boundary problem.
  - Facet-level developmental data from a multi-wave longitudinal design in children — rare and valuable even with the instrument limitations.

**What it cannot tell us:**

  - Anything about NEO-specific facets not represented in the ICID (e.g., E1 Warmth, E3 Assertiveness, E5 Excitement-Seeking, N5 Impulsiveness, N6 Vulnerability, all O facets individually).
  - Anything about the BFAS aspect structure directly — no aspect-level analysis was conducted and the ICID doesn't have an aspect layer.
  - Heritability. This is not a twin study.
  - Whether the developmental patterns generalize beyond mother-report in this age range.

**Connection to other extractions**- **Terracciano et al. (2005):** E4 Activity's steep decline replicates in adults. The O5/O1-O4 dissociation also appears in Terracciano, though less cleanly because adult O facets show more heterogeneous patterns.
  - **Brandt et al. (2023):** E4 Activity again flagged as the most developmentally distinct E facet.
  - **Jang et al. (1996):** No direct comparison possible (different instruments, no heritability here), but the O domain heterogeneity converges — O facets show the most within-domain variation across both genetic and developmental evidence streams.
  - **de Haan et al. (2017):** Most directly comparable study (also multi-wave, child sample, parent-report). Used HiPIC rather than ICID. Brandes et al. found more maturity and less disruption than de Haan et al., which may reflect instrument differences, sample differences, or both.

**Methodological note on convergence failures**

Three gender-specific latent growth models failed to converge (girls' Negative Affect, girls' Distractible, girls' Strong Willed). The authors attribute this to low cell coverage in the cohort-sequential design. For these three facets among girls, only descriptive statistics are available. This is a non-trivial limitation that affects 3 of 30 gender × facet combinations.

**Seifert, Rohrer & Schmukle (2023)**

**Citation**

Seifert, I. S., Rohrer, J. M., & Schmukle, S. C. (2023). Using within-person change in three large panel studies to estimate personality age trajectories. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*. https://doi.org/10.1037/pspp0000482

**Design**- **Type:** Three independent longitudinal panel studies, each analyzed with fixed effects modeling (FEM) using age dummies — purely within-person change estimates, no between-person confounding
  - **N:** Study 1 (HILDA, Australia): N = 15,268; Study 2 (SOEP, Germany): N = 22,833; Study 3 (LISS, Netherlands): N = 10,163. Total N > 48,000.
  - **Age range:** 15–91 (HILDA), 16–93 (SOEP), 16–89 (LISS)
  - **Instrument(s):**

    - Study 1 (HILDA): Subset of Saucier's (1994) Mini-Markers (adjectives). After item reduction: N=6 items, E=4, C=5, A=5, O=5. 7-point scale.
    - Study 2 (SOEP): BFI-S — 3 items per trait (statements from BFI translated to German). 7-point scale.
    - Study 3 (LISS): 50-item IPIP Goldberg markers, reduced to 6 items per trait. 5-point scale.

  - **Assessment interval:**

    - HILDA: 4 waves (2005, 2009, 2013, 2017); ~4 years between waves; M = 3.02 waves/person
    - SOEP: 5 waves (2005, 2009, 2013, 2017, 2019); M = 3.27 waves/person
    - LISS: 12 waves (2008–2020); M = 4.50 waves/person

  - **Key limitations:**- **No facet-level or aspect-level reporting.** All instruments are short Big Five measures. No NEO facets, no BFAS aspects, no BFI-2 facets. Findings are at domain and item level only.
    - **Item pools are small and heterogeneous across studies.** SOEP has only 3 items per trait. Items differ across the three studies, making cross-study comparisons item-content-dependent rather than construct-equivalent.
    - **All three samples are WEIRD** (Australia, Germany, Netherlands).
    - **Panel span is 12–14 years** — shorter than a full life span, so the life-span trajectory is pieced together across cohorts assuming no cohort × age interaction.
    - **Self-report only.** No informant reports.
    - **Working paper status** — published as SOEPpaper #1191 and accepted at JPSP, but the PDF is the preprint, not the final copy of record.

**Granularity**

**Domain-level (Big Five scores) and item-level.** No facet or aspect scores are computed or reported. The instruments used do not map onto established facet systems (NEO, BFI-2, HEXACO). However, the item-level developmental trajectories reveal within-domain heterogeneity that is interpretable in terms of facet/aspect content — this is where the crosswalk value lies.

**Missed opportunity:** The LISS panel uses 50 IPIP items (10 per trait before reduction), which could in principle support facet-level scoring (IPIP facet keys exist), but the authors did not pursue this. They reduced to 6 items per trait for psychometric reasons and analyzed items individually rather than grouping into facets.

**Core Findings**

All trajectories are within-person (FEM), expressed in T-score units (M = 50, SD = 10 at second observation). Effect size benchmarks used by the authors: 2 T = small, 5 T = medium, 8 T = large. All findings below are from Figures 1–3 and the accompanying text. Exact numerical estimates for individual items are not tabled in the paper; they are read from figures and described in prose. I am reporting what the authors state and what is visually apparent in the figures.

**Neuroticism**

**Domain-level trajectory:**

  - Consistent decline across all three studies throughout adulthood. [from PDF p. 51, General Discussion]
  - HILDA: ~6–8 T decline across the life span. [from Figure 1, p. 30]
  - SOEP: ~8 T decline across the life span; largest effect in this study. [from Figure 2, p. 38; text p. 39]
  - LISS: ~8 T decline, with minor deviations from monotonic decline at oldest ages. [from Figure 3, p. 45; text p. 46]
  - Most robust maturation finding across all three samples.

**Item-level heterogeneity:**- **Worry items show steepest decline.** "Worries often" (SOEP) and "Worry about things" (LISS) had the most pronounced downward age trend of any Neuroticism items. Fréchet distance analyses confirmed worry items were more similar to each other across studies than to other N items within the same study. [from PDF p. 51]
  - **Stress/relaxation items diverge in old age.** "Is relaxed, handles stress well" (recoded, SOEP) *increased* in old age — meaning people reported being *less* relaxed and less able to handle stress as they aged, even as overall Neuroticism declined. [from PDF p. 39, Figure 2]
  - **Remaining items** (e.g., "Gets nervous easily" in SOEP; "Get upset easily," "Get stressed out easily," "Get irritated easily" in LISS) generally followed the overall declining trend but with some developmental heterogeneity in old age in LISS. [from Figures 2–3]
  - In HILDA, item-level trends for Neuroticism were "comparably homogenous." [from PDF p. 31]

**Crosswalk-relevant interpretation:** The worry content (which maps loosely onto N6 Vulnerability or the Withdrawal aspect) drives the Neuroticism decline. The stress-handling content (which maps loosely onto N1 Anxiety / Volatility) may actually *worsen* in old age. This is a within-domain dissociation at the item level that could correspond to an aspect-level divergence if mapped properly.

**Extraversion**

**Domain-level trajectory:**

  - Inconsistent across studies. [from PDF p. 52]
  - HILDA: Pronounced decrease followed by plateau roughly ages 40–70, then further decline. ~6–8 T total change. [from Figure 1, p. 30; text p. 31]
  - SOEP: Slight *increase* across the life span, with some decline after ~80. ~3–4 T total change. [from Figure 2, p. 38; text p. 39]
  - LISS: Clear decline of ~8 T across the life span. [from Figure 3, p. 45; text p. 46]
  - All three studies showed decline in the oldest ages (80+). [from PDF p. 52]

**Item-level heterogeneity:**- **Vitality items decline markedly.** "Lively" (HILDA) and "Liven things up" (LISS) showed the strongest age-related declines of any Extraversion items. Fréchet distance confirmed vitality items were more similar to each other across studies than to other E items. [from PDF p. 52]
  - **Social/assertive items are flat.** "Talkative" (HILDA), "Am quiet around strangers" (recoded, LISS), "Is communicative, talkative" (SOEP) showed weak or no age trends. [from Figures 1–3]
  - **Study 2 (SOEP) contained no vitality items** — all three SOEP Extraversion items are social/communicative in content ("Is reserved" [recoded], "Is communicative, talkative," "Can be outgoing, is sociable"). This may explain why SOEP showed no E decline. [from PDF p. 52]

**Crosswalk-relevant interpretation:** This is a clear Enthusiasm vs. Assertiveness (or Social Vitality vs. Social Dominance) dissociation. Items with vitality/energy content decline sharply with age; items with social boldness/assertiveness content do not. The authors explicitly connect this to Roberts et al.'s (2006) Social Vitality vs. Social Dominance distinction. However, they note their vitality items' *decline* contrasts with Roberts et al.'s meta-analytic finding that Social Vitality showed *weak* age effects — possibly because Roberts et al. used different item content or shorter age spans.

**Conscientiousness**

**Domain-level trajectory:**

  - All three studies: Steep increase until ~age 30. [from PDF p. 52]
  - After 30: Continued increase (HILDA, LISS) or plateau (SOEP) through middle adulthood.
  - Old age: Sharp decrease in SOEP (~8 T from peak), weaker decrease in LISS (~4 T from peak), fairly nonexistent in HILDA. [from PDF p. 52–53; Figures 1–3]
  - Inverted U-shape in SOEP and LISS; monotonic increase in HILDA.

**Item-level heterogeneity:**- **Work-contextualized items drive the old-age decline.** "Works thoroughly" (SOEP) and "Am exacting in my work" (LISS, excluded from mean score) showed pronounced declines at older ages. Fréchet distance confirmed work-related C items developed similarly to each other but differently from work-unrelated C items. [from PDF p. 53]
  - **General conscientiousness items (orderliness, laziness) hold steadier.** "Is rather lazy" (recoded, SOEP) declined less in old age than the other items. "Like order" and "Pay attention to details" (LISS) were more stable. [from Figures 2–3]
  - Items were relatively homogeneous in HILDA (no work-contextualized items in that set). [from Figure 1]
  - **Study 2 (SOEP) included work items in the mean score; Study 1 (HILDA) did not.** This may explain the much more dramatic old-age decline in SOEP. [from PDF p. 53]

**Crosswalk-relevant interpretation:** The Industriousness vs. Orderliness aspect distinction is partially visible here. Work-contextualized items (Industriousness-adjacent) decline in old age, plausibly due to retirement and reduced relevance of work contexts. Orderliness-type items (order, neatness, carefulness) hold steadier. The authors explicitly warn that contextualized personality items become problematic when life circumstances change (retirement), creating an interpretive ambiguity: is it real C decline or just item-context mismatch? This is a measurement invariance issue, not necessarily a trait-change issue.

**Agreeableness**

**Domain-level trajectory:**

  - All three studies: Increase until ~age 30. [from PDF p. 53]
  - After 30: Continued strong increase (HILDA), modest continued increase (SOEP), no change (LISS). [from Figures 1–3]
  - Mixed evidence for maturation continuing beyond young adulthood.

**Item-level heterogeneity — the strongest within-domain divergence in the paper:**- **Kindness items increase.** "Kind," "Sympathetic" (HILDA); "Is considerate and kind to others," "Is sometimes a bit rude to others" (recoded, SOEP); "Insult people" (recoded, LISS) — all showed the strongest age-related increases. [from PDF p. 54]
  - **Non-kindness items decrease or flatten.** "Harsh" (recoded, HILDA) *declined* until age 40 (i.e., people endorsed being harsh more). "Can forgive" (SOEP) *declined*. "Take time out for others" (LISS) *declined*. "Cooperative" and "Warm" (HILDA) showed weaker increases than kindness items. [from Figures 1–3; PDF p. 54]
  - The authors note: "It may even be possible to select Agreeableness items in a manner that leads to a decrease with age." [from PDF p. 54]
  - Substantive speculation from the authors: "general kindness is mostly adaptive (or, at least socially desirable) and is thus indeed a sign of a 'mature' personality, whereas forgiving others and taking time out for others indiscriminately can backfire." [from PDF p. 54]

**Crosswalk-relevant interpretation:** This maps loosely onto a Compassion vs. Politeness split but not cleanly. The items that increase (kindness, not insulting) span both aspects — "Kind" and "Sympathetic" are Compassion-adjacent; "not rude" and "not insulting" are Politeness-adjacent. The items that decline or flatten (forgiving, taking time for others, harshness) are harder to place. "Can forgive" could be Politeness (compliance, not holding grudges) or Compassion (empathy-driven forgiveness). "Harsh" declining (i.e., increasing harshness) is anti-Politeness. This is genuine within-domain heterogeneity that doesn't map neatly onto the published two-aspect structure.

**Openness**

**Domain-level trajectory:**

  - Divergent across studies, loosely inverted U-shaped but with wildly different timing. [from PDF p. 54]
  - HILDA: Increase until ~60, then decline.
  - SOEP: Stable early, increase 30–70, sharp decline after 70.
  - LISS: Sharp increase until 25–30, decline after 30–40.
  - All agree on decline in oldest age. [from PDF p. 54]

**Item-level heterogeneity:**- **Imagination items decline.** "Imaginative" (HILDA) decreased. "Has a vivid fantasy, imagination" (SOEP) decreased. "Have a vivid imagination" (LISS, excluded from mean score) decreased. [from PDF p. 55; Figures 1–3]
  - **Aesthetic items increase.** "Values artistic, aesthetic experiences" (SOEP) increased with age. [from PDF p. 39, Figure 2]
  - **Intelligence-proxy items track cognitive trajectories.** "Am quick to understand things" (LISS) showed a trajectory mirroring fluid intelligence (increase in young adulthood, continuous decline). "Have a rich vocabulary" (LISS) mirrored crystallized intelligence (increase until midlife, stable plateau). The authors explicitly draw these parallels. [from PDF p. 55]
  - "Am full of ideas" (LISS) and "Is original, comes up with new ideas" (SOEP) — declined more after midlife. [from Figures 2–3]
  - Remaining items typically showed small changes. [from PDF p. 55]

**Crosswalk-relevant interpretation:** This is the clearest Intellect vs. Openness (aesthetic/experiential) split in the developmental data. Imagination declines; aesthetics increase; intelligence-proxy items track cognitive aging curves. This supports the view that the Openness domain is structurally heterogeneous, with its aspects (Intellect and Openness proper) having genuinely different developmental substrates. The fact that "Openness" trajectories vary wildly across studies is partly explained by different item mixes sampling different proportions of these aspects.

**Cross-domain and structural findings**

**FEM vs. MLM comparison (Figure 4, PDF p. 49):**

  - Age trajectories from FEM (within-person only) and standard MLM (blended within- and between-person) diverged substantially in multiple cases. Most strikingly, for Neuroticism in SOEP, MLM showed *nearly no change* while FEM showed a pronounced ~8 T decline. [from PDF p. 47–48]
  - This divergence implies large cohort effects on Neuroticism in the German sample: older cohorts had higher baseline N, creating a between-person increase with age that masks the within-person decrease.
  - Divergences also appeared for E (Studies 2, 3), C (Study 2), A (Study 3), O (Studies 1, 2). [from PDF p. 47–48]
  - **Implication for the crosswalk:** Many prior developmental studies used MLM or cross-sectional designs. Their reported trajectories may be confounded with cohort effects. FEM-based estimates from this paper should be weighted more heavily for the developmental trajectory column of the crosswalk.

**Measurement invariance (Tables 1–3, PDF p. 28, 36, 43):**

  - Metric invariance (equal factor loadings across age/cohort) was consistently supported across all traits and all three studies. ΔCFI < .01 in all cases.
  - Scalar invariance (equal item intercepts across age/cohort) was consistently *not* supported. ΔCFI exceeded .01 for most traits in Studies 1 and 2. Study 3 was ambiguous (ΔCFI mostly below .01 but close). [from Tables 1–3]
  - **Implication:** Items within the same domain do NOT change uniformly with age. Domain-level mean scores mask real developmental heterogeneity. This is the paper's central methodological finding and it directly motivates examining development below the domain level.

**Within-person factor structure (MLCFA, Supplemental Materials, summarized PDF p. 22–23):**

  - When individual Big Five dimensions were tested in separate MLCFA models, the same factor structure held on both between-person and within-person levels. [from PDF p. 22]
  - When all five traits were tested in a combined model, fit was unsatisfactory at both levels, but relatively better at the within-person level. [from PDF p. 23]
  - The Big Five factor structure fits at least as well for within-person change as for between-person differences. [from PDF p. 23]

**Effect sizes across the life span (all from Figures 1–3 and text):**

  - Overall changes ranged from ~3 T (small-to-medium) to ~8 T (large) depending on trait and study.
  - Neuroticism and Conscientiousness showed the largest changes. Agreeableness and Openness were more variable. Extraversion was study-dependent.

**Dissociation Flags**

**Extraversion: Vitality items vs. social/assertive items**- **Currently grouped as:** All items scored under Extraversion domain (no aspect assignment in this paper, but vitality items would map to Enthusiasm; social items to Assertiveness in BFAS)
  - **Evidence:** Vitality items (Lively, Liven things up) decline sharply and consistently across studies. Social/assertive items (Talkative, Reserved, Outgoing, Sociable) show weak or no age trends. Study with no vitality items (SOEP) shows no E decline at all.
  - **Strength:** Strong — replicated across 2 of 3 studies with convergent Fréchet distance analysis; absence of vitality items in Study 2 explains the discrepant pattern.

**Agreeableness: Kindness items vs. other A items**- **Currently grouped as:** All items scored under Agreeableness domain (kindness items span Compassion and Politeness aspects; declining items also span both)
  - **Evidence:** Kindness items increase with age. Forgiving, taking time for others, and harshness items decline or flatten. The direction of age change literally reverses depending on item content within the same domain.
  - **Strength:** Strong — replicated across all three studies. The most dramatic within-domain divergence in the paper. Does not map cleanly onto Compassion vs. Politeness; the split is more "prosocial warmth/politeness norms" vs. "effortful prosocial behavior."

**Openness: Imagination vs. aesthetics vs. intellect-proxy items**- **Currently grouped as:** All items scored under Openness domain (imagination maps partly to Openness aspect; aesthetics maps to Openness aspect; intellect-proxy items map to Intellect aspect)
  - **Evidence:** Imagination items decline. Aesthetic items increase. Intelligence-proxy items track cognitive aging curves (fluid: up-then-down; crystallized: up-then-plateau).
  - **Strength:** Moderate to strong — imagination decline replicated across all three studies; aesthetic increase seen clearly only in SOEP (only study with an aesthetic item in the scored set); intelligence-proxy patterns from LISS only.

**Conscientiousness: Work-contextualized items vs. general items**- **Currently grouped as:** All items scored under Conscientiousness domain (work items map loosely to Industriousness; orderliness items to Orderliness)
  - **Evidence:** Work-contextualized items decline sharply in old age. General orderliness/laziness items hold steadier. Whether C declines in old age depends on item content.
  - **Strength:** Moderate — work vs. non-work distinction replicated across Studies 2 and 3 via Fréchet distance. But interpretive ambiguity: decline could reflect real Industriousness change OR item-context invalidity after retirement.

**Neuroticism: Worry items vs. stress-handling items**- **Currently grouped as:** All items scored under Neuroticism domain (worry maps loosely to Withdrawal; stress-handling maps loosely to Volatility, though the mapping is rough)
  - **Evidence:** Worry items show steepest decline. Stress-handling item ("Is relaxed, handles stress well") *increases* (= people report being less relaxed) in old age, diverging from overall N decline.
  - **Strength:** Moderate — worry-item pattern replicated across Studies 2 and 3. Stress-handling divergence seen only in SOEP (only study with that item content).

**Notes**- **Methodological gold standard for developmental trajectories.** FEM with age dummies is the strongest available approach for isolating within-person age effects from cohort confounds. The FEM vs. MLM comparison (Figure 4) demonstrates that standard MLM can produce dramatically different — and likely confounded — trajectories. For the crosswalk, developmental findings from this paper should be weighted above most prior studies that used MLM or cross-sectional designs.**- The item-level findings are the primary crosswalk contribution.** This paper doesn't report facets or aspects, but the item-level divergences it documents are *exactly* the kind of within-domain heterogeneity that motivates the crosswalk. The fact that items within the same domain can show opposite developmental trends (e.g., kindness A items increasing while forgiveness A items decrease) is evidence that the domain level is too coarse for developmental questions.**- Connection to other crosswalk papers:** The Extraversion vitality/social split aligns with Roberts et al. (2006) Social Vitality vs. Social Dominance distinction and with the BFAS Enthusiasm vs. Assertiveness split. The Openness imagination/aesthetics/intellect split aligns with the Intellect vs. Openness aspect distinction. These convergences strengthen the case that aspect-level (or finer) analysis is necessary.**- Limitation for the crosswalk:** Without standardized facet scoring, we cannot directly populate the developmental trajectory column of the master crosswalk table from this paper. What we *can* do is flag which NEO facets' content domains show divergent developmental trajectories when those content domains appear as items in these studies. This is indirect but still informative.**- Initial elevation bias:** All three studies found systematic effects of being in one's first wave of assessment, though the pattern varied (higher item agreement in HILDA; higher construct expression in SOEP/LISS). Controlling for this did not substantively change trajectories but is methodologically important. Prior longitudinal studies that did not control for initial elevation may have biased trajectories, particularly at younger ages where participants are disproportionately at their first assessment.**- The authors' framing aligns with the "personality nuances" literature** (Mõttus et al., 2017, 2019; McCrae & Mõttus, 2019) — the idea that individual items or small item clusters are meaningful units of analysis, not just noisy indicators of higher-order constructs. This is philosophically compatible with the crosswalk's approach of treating facets as potentially independent units rather than interchangeable indicators of domains.
